## R: road-kill–ruva-ruva

### road-kill
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  From Cuba Street roadkill to Gandalf's date at the Oscars - you've come a long way, baby. <i>(Apr 3, 2002,Capital Times,7</i>)

>  And, after the "laying on of the sprigs" ceremony by the Canterbury pack, I extracted myself from the Carisbrook bog like a piece of resurgent road-kill. <i>(Aug 22, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B13</i>)

>  Possum dishes such as Road Kill, Headlight Delight Pie, Guess That Mess and Shovel-Flipped Roadside Pizza have incensed Animal Liberation Victoria (ALV). <i>(Jun 27, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  [Hdg] Roadkill wine. What better way to spend your Friday afternoon than drinking red wine and eating pie? Especially if the Roadkill Wine in question is a 2000 Hawke's Bay cabernet merlot... <i>(Nov 26, 2004,Dominion Post,A6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> animals along the road and roadside killed by passing motorists [The earliest previous reference is from the USA in 28 Dec. 1979. Term commonly used in N America.]

>  [Hdg] Race to avoid being financial roadkill... This year will go down in share marker history books as one of paving the way for change, but next year exchanges face greater pressure to really shake-up or risk being financial roadkill. <i>(Dec 24, 2001,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  Further on we pass the whitewashed remains of a pilot whale. It is unusual roadkill - a welcome change from flattened possums. <i>(Jun 25, 2002,Dominion,15</i>)

>  This infamous collection of genetically unrelated species prefers eau de roadkill - a stomach-turning blend redolent of fetid flesh, with undertones of startled skunk and week-old offal. <i>(May 4, 2003,Sunday Star Times,18</i>)

>  He was driving one-handed when he hit the possum. It had no chance, except for the chance of a graceful and violent death... "Roadkill," she said softly. "Is that what you call it here?" he said. <i>(Jan 6, 2002,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

>  Historically, hedgehogs have been recorded scavenging shrimps washed up on beaches, dead possums and sheep, and even cannibalising on 'hedgehog pizza' road-kills. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 306/10</i>)

>  Some [meat] has gone to an elderly neighbour who was such a fan of rabbit meat he was picking up roadkill and taking it home to cook. <i>(Sep 16, 2004,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  Alternatives [to entertainment] included rolling roadkill into the road, singing all the bits of the <i>Mikado</i> I could remember, reenacting my brief appearance as Old Peasant One in the school production of the <i>Caucasian Chalk Circle</i>, and swerving extremely slowly towards the many cars that sped recklessly through the site. <i>(Dec 30, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  The Fondue Generation had arrived, and no-one who survived it will ever forget the haute cuisine of the '50s - those unappetising logs of minced meat, followed by whipped-fluff desserts, which contributed to the death of in-home entertainment. Mexican macaroni, or road-kill? You made the call. <i>(Sep 1999,Cuisine,No. 76 : 105</i>)

>  <b>REDMOND</b> [Hdg] The Road-Kill Cafe. <i>Ibid.</i> 41 Another innovation was "road-killed" venison, that is sold to some of the more gullible, but daring, drivers. <i>(2000,Truckers: NZ Truckers on the Road,36</i>)

>  "It's bad, Bob, terminally bad ... you've run out of roadkill," says the voice. <i>(Sep 30, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  Bob Brockie [PhD in hedgehogs]... Claims New Zealand is the <i>Roadkill Capital of the Western World</i>... What will the new animal welfare regulations mean for drivers who used to be able to hit-and-run roadkill with impunity? <i>(Jan 16, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  I'm not sure I was ready for the secret passions of truckies, or the nudge-nudge, wink-wink tacky innuendo, scattered like road-kill throughout the programme... Coc-Kroft giggled a lot when one truckie said the radio was a useful "tool to play with.".. Then it was all-abored for the truckie sisterhood. <i>(Jul 26, 2000,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Cherry rescues a sick puppy, run-over and left as road-kill, and nurses him back to health. <i>(Sep 18, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  England ended the match as roadkill in the rear-view mirror of a resurgent Irish side. <i>(Nov 11, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B1</i>)

>  Reeve writes of the rural and patincolous, nodding a wink to monopteral falcon fare - the failure of country capeador - in "Roadkill", the slouched face of a tiny-town counter-dweller in "Central Otago", his nose a parody of the hills. <i>(Sep 15, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  You can also hit bull's eyes by the naming of a dish, e.g. "la rout de la mort" (sic) - "road kill" - is cameron-glazed ponsie of rabbit with kumara mash and balsamic tyre impression. <i>(Jul 7, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,6</i>)

>  Pukeko, rare in some parts of New Zealand, are plentiful alongside Auckland motorways, where Australasian harrier also quarter the verges for roadkill. <i>(May 2002,Forest & Bird,No. 304/27</i>)

>  Even better, you could delve into the wonderfully textural world of road kill... <Ibib.</i> 57 [Hdg] Roadkill. <i>(Jul 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  The high incidence of sleaze, crooks and mayhem in novels.. has caused Hiassen to be labelled as a crime writer, albiet a very funny and satirical one with characters twice as large as life - like the one-eyed ex-Governor who lives off roadkill. <i>(Feb 9, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  .. back on the highway, roadkill attracts the native New zealand hawk. <i>(Dec 12, 2004,Sunday Star Times,p12</i>)

>  ...the concoction tasted like burned urine with a pronounced after-taste of rotting roadkill. <i>(Jul 28, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A12</i>)

>  <i>Road Kill</i> is a quick, formulaic tale of a boy, his Harley and an apparently murdered teacher... <i>(Dec 7, 2001,Evening Post,11</i>)

>  I've drunk Heineken in Amsterdam, Tuborg in Copenhagen and domestic claret in Morocco that might as well have been liquefied roadkill. <i>(Dec 7, 2001,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  Whatever - it works (when it doesn't wind up as road-kill). <i>(Feb 28, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I wasn't even between a rock and a hard place - I was roadkill. <i>(Nov 29, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  Bores, you've had it. You're road-kill. <i>(Jan 1, 2003,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  Instead I'll awake this morning, hoping Andrew Mehrtens, Sam Tuitupou and the rest haven't been squished overnight by Schalk Burger and his mates; that the flat backline isn't so much a concept as an epithet. For rugby roadkill. <i>(Aug 15, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

>  Deadpan and blues, she sang of manipulated, hapless men cast off as "road-kill". <i>(Jun 26, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

### roadie
 
 <i>n.</i> a roadman

>  Roadies riled [headline] Speed freaks are putting road builders in potentially life-threatening positions. `I'm not scared of dying but the rehearsals are giving me the sh...,' was how one worker known as Blue described the situation at road building sites he had worked on. <i>(Jun 15, 1986,Sunday News,25</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> I've known roadies who knew theirs was a high place in the scheme of things, ... <i>(1983,Bone People, The,241</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a road user

>  DESPITE all the ranting and raving by Mr Prebble to the contrary it would appear that even NZ Railways concede that costs by road are cheaper than by rail. ... It is certainly satisfying to have a `roadies' view endorsed by the corporation <i>(Jun 10, 1985,National Business Review,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [road trip]

>  He still has friends in the area and they will be doing a roadie up to Dunedin to see him in black. <i>(Aug 3, 2003,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> someone who rides a bicycle on the road, as opposed to an off-road or MTB cyclist

>  Now it's possible to buy feather-light mountain bikes that can almost foot it with the "roadies", or skinny-tyred fraternity, on smooth surfaces. <i>Ibid.</i> 100 All of this is.. incomprehensible to 99.9 per cent of people who knew all about the joy of riding long before MTBS were invented. These are the "roadies". <i>(Feb 1997,North & South,99</i>)

### roading
 
 <i>n.</i> ? NZ = roads collectively (Miles Fairburn) -->eg The roading in the district needs money spent on it. [check]

### roadless North
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) North Auckland/Northland

>  ".. Whether the Government will find in this treatment a stimulus and encouragement to further exertions on behalf of 'the roadless North' we do not pretend to say ..." <i>(Apr 7, 1894,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  With special reference to Auckland's roadless north and ill-roaded King Country came a proposal before the Farmers' Union Conference yesterday that the Government should construct and maintain main arterial roads. <i>(Jul 17, 1913,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Locals no doubt derived grim satisfaction from the difficulties besetting the parliamentary tour of the "roadless North." <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,74</i>)

>  Some days ago a deputation of Auckland members waited upon the Premier to urge the claims of the "roadless North." <i>(Sep 12, 1893,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>BARR</b> The chauffeur who had driven Sir Joseph Ward over the roadless north in this same 34-horse doesn't know `nerves'. <i>(1908,British Rugby Team in Maoriland,60</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> But the north was not respectable. It was the land of lost men. It was peopled with nomads and wasters. And it was `the roadless north', the `barbaric north', where the Maoris still might war upon you in the night. It had no railway beyond Helensville. The Southerner curled his lip at it. <i>(1925,Allen Adair,6,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> .. a post bearing the legend `Waihopo Landing' seemed to indicate that in comparatively recent times, when the term `roadless north' expressed the literal truth, the little settlement was saved by boat. <i>(1946,Farthest North,34</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> Northland The mill was closed in winter, for the reason that may be apparent only to those who remember the `roadless north' in the later decades of the nineteenth century. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,235</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> The building was erected when this part of the country [Pukenui] could literally be described as `the roadless north' <i>(1961,North Cape to Bluff,30</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> `Smallpox in New Zealand,' scoffed everybody, `and in the roadless North! ...' <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,114</i>)

>  The `roadless North' was despairing of the Liberals, but not yet [pre-WWI] prepared to trust reform. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/365</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> Nevertheless the `Roadless North', to use the term in general use at that time [c1904], was still well named. It was far more accurate then than the term `Winterless North' which came later. <i>(1970,Way Up North,72</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> Nevertheless the `Roadless North', to use the term in general use at that time [c1904], was still well named. It was far more accurate then than the term `Winterless North' which came later. <i>(1970,Way Up North,72</i>)

### roadman
 
 <i>n.</i> a road worker

>  Each roadman was responsible for maintaining a section of the road - clearing slips, filling in potholes - and lived with his family in a hut along his stretch. <i>(Sep 2003,NZ Heritage,No. 90/10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a road worker, roadie

>  <b>HARPER</b> The roadmen in charge of the [Otira] pass reported very deep drifts ... <i>(Jul 10, 1868,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,129,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Immediately ... the Provincial Government had sent on a number of roadmen to repair the worst places, to build bridges, to drain swamps, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 120 Next morning the party of roadmen ... returned over the Pass ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,70</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> I ... [relate] what a New Zealand hill hotel-keeper and butcher told me:- `I buy a sheep ... I sell the meat ... to the road-men, and use the rest in my hotel.' <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,76</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> [a swagger speaks] But de'il tak' the hour I agreed to gang amang the cockatoos wi' ye, Bill; an' I could wish mysel' back amang the squatters again. Your wee bit farmers are a measly lot ... To be sure, ane is aye made at hame in a bush fa'ers' or roadmen's camp to a share o' what's goin'; ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,7</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> It is true that this is primarily a trampers' hut, but trampers don't usually carry periodicals in their swags. ... But here the two journals are now, and I wish I knew how often they are read by the other users of this shelter - rabbiters, deer-cullers, roadmen, storm-bound swaggers, and so on. <i>(Mar 19, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,25,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> The roadmen were invaluable. Not only did they keep their allotted [176] stretch of road in order but they watched every river and creek. <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,175</i>)

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> A new-comer was Eddie who was taking a few days off from his job as roadman and from his home and family down near the Timahanga turnoff. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,84</i>)

>  <b>BATTYE & EAKIN</b> Well Jock McDonald, the roadman, had an inkling for a while that the dog didn't eat all the meat <i>(1980,Shadow of the Valley,41</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> Sometimes we came across one [swagger] boiling his billy in a roadman's clearing ... and eating cold meat and bread from his tucker box <i>(1987,Swagger on Our Doorstep,113</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person who maintained rural gravelled roads before the use of heavy machinery and tar-sealing.

>  <b>PASCOE</b> The Cunninghams sold injured sheep for meat to the roadmen ... <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,124</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the servicing of the road was done by a solitary road man. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,27</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Bill Harrod, who was renewing an old fenceline, had been a roadman on the Taihape for many years ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,164</i>)

>  The Wairarapa South County Council built the roadman's cottage which is still there today. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,185</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Joe Dickie worked for many years for the local county as a roadman. [road man] <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,214</i>)

### roadroller
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> roadroller bushman or hick; early C20. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,93</i>)

### roads and bridges
 
 <i>phr.</i> (historical)

>  FOR ROADS AND BRIDGES .. The ratepayers of Akitio county, which is situated between Pahiatua and the East Coast, on Saturday carried a proposal to raise GBP28,000 for bridging and road works. <i>(May 22, 1911,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  The Colonists of Van Dieman's Land could not presume to disparage the settlement of Port Nicholson; they do not question .. the liberal expenditure of the New Zealand company, the vast number of emigrants it has employed in the formation of roads and bridges ... <i>(Dec 26, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  The Hon. A. W. Hogg, Minister for Roads and Bridges, visited the East Road and Ohura country this week. <i>(Mar 4, 1909,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  WE, the undersigned, Carriers, are obliged through the bad state of the roads and bridges at Silver Stream, Pakuratahi, Waihoine, Waingawa, also the incompleteness of railway accomodation, to raise the up-carriage from Wellington to Masterton fifteen shillings per ton, until such time as the roads and bridges are made good, dated from 1st January, 1876. <i>(Feb 11, 1876,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  There would still be many millions to be borrowed for the completion of our hydro-electric schemes, telephone extensions, schools, public buildings, roads and bridges and other public works. <i>(Nov 13, 1928,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  P. R. Harman, Secretary, South Island Motor Union, asked support in seeking fair treatment in expenditure on Public Works on roads and bridges in the South Island. <i>(Nov 22, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,4</i>)

>  <i>(1867,Ordinances of Taranaki</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Roads and Bridges Ordinance, 1858. in 1867 Ordinances of Taranaki 93 hist.]

>  <b>MULGAN</b> Often settlers went right out into the wilds and had to wait long for reasonably good communications. This partly explains the importance of the `roads and bridges member' in the New Zealand Parliament, a term that has well-nigh disappeared. One might laugh at him and deplore his preoccupation with local matters, but road or bridge could be life or death to an outback settler. The central Government was the main source of bounty, so grants were continually asked for. <i>(1958,Making of a New Zealander, The,17</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Among a rather mediocre team Coates stood out for initiative and vigour. By his sustained use and encouragement of experts, by his disregard for the time-honoured `roads and bridges' system, Coates broke new ground [c1920-1926]. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/365</i>)

### roads district
 
 <i>n.</i> an administrative district for the maintenance of roads

>  roads district NZ administrative district for the maintenance of roads; <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,660</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### roadster
 
 <i>n.</i> a horse [(station, ? rare)]

>  <b>W.H.S. ROBERTS</b> We now had quite a large mob of breeding mares on the run, with a very useful stamp of horse which we called a roadster, who gloried in the name of `Sir Roger de Coverley'; ... <i>(Aug 1856,Diary,34</i>) [NOTES: 1856 June W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 34]

### roady
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  [In reply to a vote of thanks to the Contractors and Workmen on the Sumner Road] ... (A voice; Three cheers for the roady mob). <i>(Aug 26, 1857,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

### roany
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  It takes Bill and I two hours to catch that old roany Maori weed we used for packing tucker. <i>(Jul 5, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/47 : 251</i>)

### roar
 
 <i>n.</i> noise made by a stag during the rutting season to attract hinds and signal his size to his rivals. Also a synonym for the rut.

>  <b>LENTLE & SAXTON</b> roar: noise made by a stag during the rutting season to attract hinds and signal his size to his rivals. Also a synonym for the rut. <i>(1991,Red Deer in New Zealand,186</i>)

### roarer
 
 <i>n.</i> a shepherd who can't command a sufficiently penetrating whistle, so relies on a voice strengthened by years of training

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The "roarer" is a shepherd who can't command a sufficiently penetrating whistle, so relies on a voice strengthened by years of training. <i>(1967,Return to Open Country,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a stag [ca 1925 W.H.B.Orsman (Marlborough)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> He developed into a 'roarer' (broken-wind) which ended his racing but he remained the King's favourite hack. <i>(2000,First Past the Post,37</i>) [NOTES: Joe Lorrigan "Famous Horse! Sacred Cow?" in <i>The Park Sensation</i> 1987]

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> He was a `roarer' too; that is, he was broken-winded, so that every breath blew out of his lungs with a horse roar ... <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,117</i>) [NOTES: cf EDD 4. (3) a restless cow Midl.; nf OED.]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a horse with broken wind.

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> he was a roarer too - that is, he was broken-winded, so that every breath blew out of his lungs with a hoarse roar like the sound of the worn-out engine of the Dargaville-Kaihu train. <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,117</i>)

### Roaring Meg
 
 <i>n.</i> a common name from gold-digging and coaching days for swift, rapid-rising creeks or small rivers (cf CRYING JENNY, GENTLE ANNIE) The first is very small, and in a dry season merely drips from the road [ca 1951 a mountain stream in Tararuas (NCC Small) 1955 Dec 24 Illustrated London News 1113 [caption] -->R. Mason notes of the creek name that the following may have some bearing on the original naming. A model of Londonderry's famous gun `Roaring Meg': in silver on an oak stand, presented to Sir Winston Churchill when he became freeman of Londonderry. cf OED (1575) a huge cannon etc.; (cf Illustr London News. 1955 Dec 24 p1113) (1632) kind of top; (1811) (Geol) Trans. Geol Soc. This structure of clay .... goes by the name of shaggy metal, and the fresh water which makes its way through the pores has the expressive appellation of Roaring Meg.; W3 only common usage (cf. Roaring (adj) 1848. boisterous; (of health) exuberant)]

>  The Company is to be formed for the purpose of constructing a water-race from the Roaring Meg to German Gully, and Black Ball Creek to German Gully ... <i>(Aug 23, 1887,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  [Photo caption] THE ROARING MEG FALLS, KAWARAU RIVER. <i>(Jul 24, 1907,Otago Witness,49</i>)

>  <b>McNEISH</b> In 1864 the first true Roaring Meg was built. This was an inn of rock. <i>(1957,Tavern in the Town,238</i>) [NOTES: 1966 edition]

>  It has to be remembered that below the dam the Shotover River and the streams, "Roaring Meg" and "Gentle Annie" empty into the Kawerau River. <i>(Sep 8, 1926,NZ Observer,2</i>)

>  The prospecting claim at the Roaring Mag [sic] was granted upon the prospect of one pennyweight to the tin dish, and that at the Five Mile Creek upon two pennyweights to the tin dish. <i>(Jan 24, 1863,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  A rush has taken place at the Roaring Meg, one of the tributaries of the Kawarau. <i>(Jan 31, 1863,Otago Witness,8</i>)

>  The famous Roring Meg, which later surges through Kawarau Gorge, starts in these hills. <i>(Jul 9, 2002,Dominion Post,D3</i>)

>  <i>Roaring Meg</i>, usually a creek and far more rough and tumble, is apt to be in the neighbourhood of <i>Gentle Annie... Roaring Meg</i> may come from the name of the famous gun of Londonderry. <i>(Sep 1959,Tararua,13/49</i>)

>  The river known as the "Roaring Meg," in the Otaki Gorge, is to have a bridge erected across it at a cost of GBP53 10s. <i>(Jan 11, 1910,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Mr Grenfell describes passing Roaring Meg and Gentle Annie as "child's play:" <i>(Jan 22, 1924,Otago Witness,44</i>)

>  There were surprised by a party of prospectors.. who.. by way of Kawarau Gorge, Roaring Meg and Gentle Annie, had reached the Arrow river. <i>(Apr 1, 1924,Otago Witness,64</i>)

>  They are prepared to borrow $1.3 million to harness the Pukeatua Stream, also known as Roaring Meg. <i>(Oct 6, 1992,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> By the side of `Roaring Meg' ... [3] The mountain torrent where to allusion has been made was the stream named by surveyors as the `Kirtleburn', but which is best known by the fantastic appellation conferred on it by the miners - `Roaring Meg' There are three creeks in succession on this line of road - tributaries of the Kawarau River. ocks, and is called `Crying Jenny' The second, which tumbles down from the mountains impetuously and with great noise, is `Roaring Meg' The third, being a sluggish stream, has received, by popular assent, the designation of `Gentle Annie' <i>(1874,George Washington Pratt,1,3</i>)

>  <b>HOLLOWAY</b> [Coach en route for Clyde crossed the Kawarau River, passed Gibbs Town] `Drove over Annie's Creek. And on till we came to within half a mile of Roaring Mag [sic. ?a mis-hearing] - Here is a natural bridge over the River Kawarau. ... A little below this bridge we crossed a Beautiful Mountain Torrent call'd Roaring Mag, and not inaptly ... <i>(Mar 21, 1874,Journal of a Visit</i>)

>  <b>HOLLOWAY</b> [Coach en route for Clyde crossed the Kawarau River, passed Gibbs Town] `Drove over Annie's Creek. And on till we came to within half a mile of Roaring Mag [sic. ?a mis-hearing] - Here is a natural bridge over the River Kawarau. ... A little below this bridge we crossed a Beautiful Mountain Torrent call'd Roaring Mag, and not inaptly ... <i>(Mar 21, 1874,Journal of a Visit</i>) [NOTES: 1875 (1874 Mar 21) Holloway Journal of a Visit]

>  <b>DOUGLAS</b> 'Twas sweet upon a Summers eve/To wander with my Nannie/ To hop across the Roaring Meg/ And climb up gentle Annie. <i>(Jan 29, 1891,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1891 Jan 29 Douglas in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 149]

>  <b>A TRAMP</b> ... trudging on ... up by the Molyneux; blessing `Roaring Meg' and sweating and swearing over the `Gentle Annie' <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,108</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> There are now several bonnie burns coming down the mountain sides to the river, the largest of which are the famous Roaring Meg and Gentle Annie; and a visitor ... may wash his colours of gold from almost anywhere while his wee wife boils the billy. <i>Ibid.</i> 53 ... Roxburgh and Cromwell are well behind, and I slow down ... to enjoy the sight of the water gushing from the spillway of the newly-erected power station at the Roaring Meg corner of the [Kawarau] Gorge. This heavy stream, which breaks so suddenly on a stranger from its very rough surroundings, got its name (according to early-day tradition) from the fact that Meg, one of the two girls on their way to a dancing saloon at Arrow, created such a fuss at crossing this turbulent water that vibrations of her voice loosened rocks on the mountain side to add still further to the roaring of stream and of Meg herself. There are different versions concerning the naming of this stream, and maybe some of this one is my own. ... A few miles further on the next stream to be crossed is the Gentle Annie, called after Meg's pal, who no doubt was a much quieter type of tourist than Meg herself. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,20</i>)

>  <i>(1959,Tararua,13/49</i>)

>  <b>McGILKL</b> Roaring Meg a raging river, perhaps near a Gebtle Annie mountain; possibly after legendary Irish landlady Maggie (Mag or Meg) Brennan of the Queenstown area who allegedly bellowed at customers and threw stroppy clients in the nearby creek; ref. James McNeish's Tavern in the Town; also the name of a Londonderry gun. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,93</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> This side-track crosses two old water races, Wessels'[sic], and Roaring Meg (the latter diverting water by a 30 metre tunnel from nearby Roaring Meg Creek) ... <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,64</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a raging river.

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> We passed, during our drive, three mountain torrents of different characters .. Weeping Lizzie, the Roaring Meg, and the Gentle Annie. You may miss seeing Lizzie or Annie, but you cannot escape Meg. She is a rumbling, raging, scolding stream ... <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,187</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> Roaring Meg a raging river, perhaps near a Gentle Annie mountain <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,93</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (personal nickname) [?186? 1870]

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> Gentlemen of first rank, William Downie Stewart observed, could be seen ... at Vauxhall [Gardens, Otago Peninsula] in the company of famous prostitutes, such as Roaring Meg and Black Susan. <i>(1980,Southern Gentry,101</i>)

### roaring up
 
 <i>n.</i> a severe reprimand [ca 1951 Miss G. Hall: (Letter) [? Central Otago] `A roaring up': a severe talking to, in the disciplinary sense: such as `I gave him a number one roaring up and sent him down the road (q.v.)' cf Part. Roar up: Speak abusively to; shout at Lower classes': from ca 1905 F and G; AC 1919]

### roaroa
 
 <i>n.</i> as roa [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  He said the Canterbury Museum received this bird from a collector on the West Coast of this Island, who states that the natives call this species Roaroa, and that a Maori from Bruce Bay informed him these birds were very valorous, and would well defend themselves against the attacks of dogs by kicking. <i>(Oct 15, 1870,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  The "roaroa," which is locally known as "the fireman," as far as Mr. Hansen knows, is not found on the eastern side of Preservation Inlet ... <i>(Mar 26, 1910,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  <i>Apteryx maxima</i> is described [from Nelson] as `a Kiwi, about the size of a turkey ...' The natives distinguish it as the Roaroa. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/15</i>) [NOTES: (Buller Ornithology)]

>  <b>WILLIAM DOCHERTY</b> 9 Ka-Ka Pos. [pounds]9. 2 dozen Kivi's skins [pounds]7:4:0. ... 6 Rowis skins [pounds]6. [549 1870] If the Roa-Roa is in the country, mark my words, I will find him. <i>(Oct 13, 1870,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1870 Oct 13 William Docherty in 1948 Von Haast Julius Von Haast 548, 549]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 61. ? APTERYX MAXIMA. Verr. Great Kiwi. Roa-roa. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,23</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> APTERYX HAASTI. (LARGE GREY KIWI.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Roa or Roaroa, and Kiwi-Karuai. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,371</i>)

>  59. <i>Apteryx haastii</i>, Potts. Roa-Roa. - Nothing is known of the breeding habits of this fine species. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,506</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>A. haasti</i>. - Roaroa, Kiwi, Karuai. South Island, in Alpine regions. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> <i>roa, roaroa,</i> a bird (Apterxy haastii), large grey kiwi: South Island kiwi. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the great spotted kiwi, Apteryx haasti

>  The roaroa (A. haasti) is another bird of "the kiwi kind. <i>(Apr 3, 1890,Otago Witness,00</i>) [NOTES: Check page number]

### roastery
 
 <i>n.</i> a place for roasting coffee beans

>  Having quit the part-time job I had at a coffee roastery in order to be available for all the great temping work that was going to come my way, I was most disappointed when two long, broke weeks later I received my first assignment... <i>(Sep 16, 2004,Dominion Post,D1</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>phr.</i> (derogatory reference)

### Rob's Mob
 
 <i>n.</i> supporters of Robert Muldoon

>  While acknowledging that the station [sc. Radio Pacific] has a "significant" audience, it panders, he says, to the prejudices of the vestiges of Rob's Mob. <i>(Apr 1998,North & South,71</i>)

>  Many of his [sc. Muldoon's] social views, such as on royalty, now seem quaint, while the hero-worshipping "Rob's Mob" is literally dying out. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  By contrast, National reaped a "Rob's Mob" harvest among Labours's social conservatives in earlier years... <i>(Oct 29, 2006,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  They took it to the woman, Rob's Mob. <i>(May 24, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  I neither liked nor admired him, and deplored his bullying behaviour; I thought he was a damned fool for enabling the notorious Springbok rugby tour of 1981; his constant references to "Rob's Mob" - his imaginary millions of adoring admirers - incensed me; but to call him evil is to go too far. He was just a snappy little bloke whom we made prime minister. <i>(Sep 1, 2011,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  It has a faint echo of Sir Robert Muldoon's "Rob's Mob" but without the deep-red tinge to its neck... <i>(Sep 22, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  First, [Peters] has pinched National's grumpy "Robs's Mob" vote with a more colourful stance on issues such as the Treaty of Waitangi. <i>(Jul 21, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A4</i>)

>  <b>JAMES</b> On his way to victory in 1975, Muldoon had recruited 'Robsmob' - 'ordinary citizens' from outside National's normal ranks. <i>(1986,Quiet Revolution, The,91</i>)

>  There are still some people who seem to pine for the days of Muldoon, pensioners mainly, the remnants of "Rob's Mob". <i>(Feb 1992,Metro,8</i>)

>  Activist members of the National Party, proudly identifying themselves as "Rob's Mob", flocked to hear their new Messiah. <i>(Nov 2, 1995,Times (London),23</i>)

### robber
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a piglet that takes over the teat of a sow other than its mother, often causing its mother's teat to dry off.

>  It is not sufficient merely to put the 'robber' back - it will find a way time and time again and if it should be away from its own mother for more than two days, the milk in the quarter at which he was feeding will dry-off. <i>(Jul 7, 1937,Weekly News,76</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ewe that takes the lamb of another.

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> The activities of 'robber' ewes, the influence of blustery weather, wet soil conditions, competition for available feed, the use of poorly trained dogs, the use of 'all-rounders' or huntaways instead of quiet strong-eyed dogs, are also contributing factors to this unsettledness. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,87</i>)

>  A ewe that takes the lamb of another: robbber. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high-consuming but poorly-producing dairy cow.

>  The Kaupokonui Directors, after inspecting and trying Preston's cow detector, a simple and cheap little machine used for the purpose of detecting the robber cows in the herd, have purchased the right to use and sell these machines at a low price to the suppliers. <i>(Nov 27, 1920,Hawera Star,9</i>)

>  Experience shows that no man knows, without testing, which of his cows are returning a profit and which are the 'robbers' or cows which don't yield sufficient to pay for their keep and milking. <i>(Oct 1, 1926,NZ Farmer,1421</i>)

>  One farmer who is testing his herd of thirty cows culled out six 'robbers'... <i>(Oct 29, 1927,NZ Dairy Exporter,26</i>)

>  Cull the robbers - once the unprofitable cows are detected, they should be culled. <i>(Sep 1, 1937,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

 <i>phr.</i> [(folk rhymes)]

 <i>n.</i>

### robin
 
 <i>n.</i> a) any of various small, plump, European birds with an orange breast. b) any of various similar but unrelated Australian birds, with scarlet or pink breasts. c) any of three similar but unrelated New Zealand birds. Also called toutouwai, katuhituhi. [AND name 1823] Miro australis, BUSH ROBIN, NATIVE ROBIN, NEW ZEALAND ROBIN, TOUTOUWAI 1. simplex (distinguish North Island and South Island Robins, and deal with them here: but only if epithet in text: give defin. that covers both races]

>  The boat dropped us at Motuara Island bird sanctuary near Ship Cove, where robins will eat from your hand and blue penguins nest in boxes, known as penguin pubs, supplied by DOC. <i>(Jun 1, 2004,Dominion Post,A17</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> The male & female of a small Thrush [[ed. fn] <i>Turdus minutus of Descr. Anim.</i> p.83; ... Now theYellow-breasted Tit ...] was shot, which we hitherto had taken for a variety of the small yellow legged Thrush, commonly called Robin by our Ship's-crew; ... <i>(May 3, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/269</i>)

>  (Queen Charlotte Sound) ... one [bird] in figure and tameness exactly resembles our Robin [ed. note [South island Robin, <i>Miro australis</i>.]] <i>(Feb 1777,Cook's Journals,3/806,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/806]

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> [see Kokaho [South Island Robin]] <i>(1846,Journal of an Expedition</i>)

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> [?S.I. Robin] we killed a robin. <i>(1847,Great Journey,42,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> We have a bird called the Robin but though like his British cousin in shape and habits his breast is black. <i>(Jun 9, 1853,Auckland Journals,140,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The lark is nearly identical and the quail is the same - the hawk the same - the robin has its counterpart here in a bird with a slate coloured head and throat with a canary coloured breast, to all intents and purposes it is a robin for all that; ... <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,44,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> - It is Sunday. We rose later than usual. There are five men sleeping in the hut. I sleep in a bunk on one side of the fire, Mr. Haast ... sleeps upon the opposite one; my bullock-driver and hut-keeper have two bunks at the far end of the hut, along the wall; ... The usual mutton and bread for breakfast with a pudding made of flour and water baked in the camp-oven after a joint of meat - Yorkshire pudding, but without eggs. While we were at breakfast, a robin perched on the table and sat there a good while pecking at the sugar. <i>(Apr 1861,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1861 April Butler's Note in 1919 Jones Samuel Butler - A Memoir 1/87 April, 1861]

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Killed a robin and three wrens; roasted them: the smallest joints I ever saw; . <i>(Apr 13, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Apr 13 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 406]

>  Petroica longipes. Totowai. Robin. Common. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  PETROICA ALBIFRONS, Gml. Totoara. Robin....PETROICA LONGPIPES, Less. and garn. Robin. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/60</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... to the end ... suspend ... a robin, or other small bird, newly killed; ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,32</i>)

>  The Robin (<i>Petroica albifrons</i>) is a very bold bird ... <i>(1872,TrNZI,4/212</i>) [NOTES: (Travers NZ Birds)]

>  <b>BULLER</b> This bird is the subject of an article - the Pitoitoi or Toutouwai of the natives and the `Robin' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,120,{1888 2nd edn 1/34} ed.</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Miro longpipes The large grey-coated <i>robin</i> - kinsman true/ Of England's delicate highbred bird of home, -/ <i>Ibid.</I.>2 /338 Robin ..... Miro longipes - of Lesson and Dr. Buller. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/21</i>)

>  28. <i>Petroeca macrocephala,</i> Gml. Yellow-breasted Tit or Robin, Miro-miro, Ngoru-ngoru [sic]... <i>Ibid.</i> 30. <i>Petroeca albifrons</i>, Gml. Robin, Toto-ara. - This delightful warbler is decreasing in numbers very rapidly ... <i>(Nov 1884,NZ Journal of Science,286</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>DUCHESS OF BUCKINGHAM</b> [At Bluff] saw some colonial robins; and also oyster birds, or `red bills', with black bodies and red legs and beaks. <i>(1894,Glimpses of Four Continents,127</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> The robin (<i>Miro albifrons</i>) is a constant companion in some localities, as far as the bush limit; ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,39</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Robin, n. The name, in consequence of their external resemblance to the familiar English bird, is applied, in Australia, to species of the various genera as follows:... The New Zealand species are - -->Chatham Island Robin - Miro traversi, Buller. North Island R. - M. australis, Sparrm. -->South Island R. - M. albifrons, Gmel. -->[NZ Dates 1866 Lady B. S. L; 1872 Domett] <i>(1898,Austral English,390</i>)

>  <i>Robi.in</i> (r&obreve.bi.&itilde.n), <i>n. [Add.] New Zealand robin (Zool.)</i>, the miro. See MIRO, 2. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [Birds South Westland] The New Zealand Robin .. <i>(1899,Mr Explorer Douglas,271,1957 ed.</i>)

>  I have paid special attention to the nests of three birds- the Native Canary (<i>Orthonyx ochrocephala</i>), the Robin (<i>Myiomoira albifrons</i>), and the Tomtit (<i>Myiomoira macrocephala</i>). Sir Walter Buller says ... that the Blue Crow (<i>Glaucopis cinerea</i>) lays a single egg, and ... leaves the care of the chick to the to the Popokatea, or Canary (<i>Orthonyx albicilla</i>). <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/119</i>)

>  <b>FERGUSON</b> See here are met .../ The robin's chirp and lintie's trill,/ And mocking bird, with fantail heard,/ And parroquet's green clamouring shrill. <i>(1912,Castle Gay,60</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Toutou, ... Miro australis,</i> robin. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,518</i>)

>  Here and there we heard the distant call of the <i>tui</i> ... and when we `spelled', grey-breasted robins fluttered about us cheekily. <i>(1930,NZ Short Stories,19</i>) [NOTES: Arthur H. Adams]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> The robins, riflemen and tomtits tamed very soon, the robins being an easy first in this respect. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,108,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <i>robin</i> A small lovable bird; in England called `robin redbreast' Australian and New Zealand robins do not have red breasts. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),346</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> TOMTIT <i>Petroica australis Maori name</i>: Toutouwai. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,202</i>)

### robin-run-the-hedge
 
 <i>n.</i> cleavers

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> CLEAVERS (<i>Galium aparine</i>), also called lady's bed straw, goose grass, robin-run-the-hedge, and hairiff, is to be found on road sides and waste places in many parts of both islands. It is an annual, with sprawling stems up to five feet long often forming tangled masses. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,163,1967 ed.</i>)

### robust skink
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a relatively large nocturnal skink both longer and thicker than most; resident on islands off Bay of Plenty. [<i>Oligosoma alani</i> aka pre 2009 <i>Cyclodina alani</i>]

>  <b>Jenny Jones</b> The robust skink is one of the largest of our New Zealand skinks. It has a SVL [snout to vent length] of 140 killimetres and can weigh up to 60 grams. <i>(2002,Lizards,31</i>)

>  Robust Skink <i>Cyclodina alani</i> Occurs only on a few offshore islands North East of the North Island. Possibly the largest New Zealand skink it is forest dwelling and strongly nocturnal. <i>(Aug 7, 2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.reptilepark.co.nz/nzlizard.htm]

>  The aptly named robust skink.., thickset in all its features, is the archetypal <i>Cyclodina</i>. <i>Ibid</i> 78 "The rare Whitaker's skink and the robust skink have been the main focus of the relocations, with the result that the future of these species is probably brighter now than at any time in the past 1000 years," says Towns. <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 47 pp 67, 78</i>)

### Roc
 
 <i>n.</i> regional operating company [ORIGIN: acronym]

>  Telecom says plan to disband its four regional operating companies will not alter its Kiwi Share obligations... The Rocs are at the heart of the Kiwi Share - a statement enshrined in the company's articles of association when Telecom was sold to Bell Atlantic and Ameritech in 1990. It was an attempt by the then Labour government to protect the interests of the New Zealand public. <i>(Oct 5, 1992,Dominion Infotech Weekly,1</i>)

### rock
 
 <i>n.</i> any stone [(esp. schoolboys')]



 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet in the names of plants and animals see found on, around, or in the vicinity of rock or rocks AKEAKE, ANISTOME, BLUEBELL, COTTONPLANT, CUDWEED, DAISY, FERN, FORGET-ME-NOT, KOROMIKO, LILY, GROUNDSEL, MAHOE, SNOWBERRY, SOW-THISTLE; FERN, SPLEENWORT; TREE-DAISY -->of animals [aj or attrib; see also oyster, perch, trout, wren, rock-shell,] (See [cod](../C#cod),[lark](../L#lark),[ling](../L#ling),[lizard](../L#lizard),[lobster](../L#lobster))



 
 <i>n.</i> bedrock [(goldminers') see AND]



 
 <i>v.</i> to throw rocks (stones) at [ca 1935 eg Look at Jerry rocking his poor old dog.(schoolboys')]



 
 <i>v.</i> to speed up, to intensify (an action) [ca 1945 We rocked the grog along all night, and Christ! were we sick in the morning! cf P: Rock in: Accelerate or intensify; esp. rock it in! hurry up: Austr.: 20c. B. 1942.]

>  <b>GASKELL</b> [To a girl singing slowly and sadly] Rock it along. ... She started off sadly again but we wouldn't let her. We rocked it. <i>(1947,Big Game,33</i>)

### rock borer
 
 <i>n.</i>

### rock camp
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an overhang or large shelter rock used by musterers, hunters and stock for shelter.

>  <b>CURTIS</b> We lost no time in heading for our rock camp and by the time we reached it the wind had dropped and the snow was falling. <i>(1996,Beneath the Mountain Mist,67</i>)

### rock cod
 
 <i>n.</i> a (usu. alternative and often little used) name for various fishes of coastal reefs

>  (Black Stewart Island) It was no unusual event ...to land ... Moki, <i>Latris ciliaris;</i> Trumpeter, <i>Latris hecateia</i>; Blue cod or Rock cod, <i>Percis colias</i>; and Red cod, <i>Lotella bacchus.</i> <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>)

>  Some hapukas weighed 80lb., and there were many trumpeters and rock-cod. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/379</i>)

>  (Journal Capt Robertson) rockcod, barracouta ... abouke. [poss. blue cod: longer cit.]] <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/30/69</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The bream, ling, gurnard, herring, sole, rock-cod, and a species of mackarel, are all in abundance, as are also many fish unknown elsewhere [than Wellington]. <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),49</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> Fish are plentiful, herrings (a large kind not so good as English), Rock Cod, a sort of Whiting, moka, small soles, Flounders, Eeels [sic], sometimes Habouka a large fish (which I tasted at Capt Simeon's) something between Cod and Grey Mullet, but not so good as either. <i>(1853,Journal,1/158</i>)

>  <b>DRURY</b> FISH [MET WITH IN PELORUS]. Rock Cod - Pakiri of the natives, red and brown, or black, very numerous. <i>(1855,Sailing Directions,67</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The best [sea fish] are the Hapuka ... a sort of mackerel, and some mullet, rock-cod, ... and snapper. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/122</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Many on board are busily employed in fishing for rock cod, 1 ft. and 1[half] ft. long. <i>(Jul 22, 1853,Traveller under Concern,124,1973 ed.</i>)

>  Many ... varieties of sea-fish are caught round the coast, such as barracouta, groper, rock-cod, ling, flounders, soles, gar-fish, and others, all which are plentiful ... <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The,32</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 38. ROCK COD. ... This (<i>Percis colias</i>) is the Coal Fish of Captain Cook and Blue Cod of the settlers in the South, and the Pakirikiri of the Maoris. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,113</i>)

>  <b>SENIOR</b> Amongst the sea-fishes fit for food there are ... aua, rock cod, wrasse, and patiki; ... <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,288</i>)

>  The fish for eating are hapuka, <i>Oligorus gigas</i>; rock cods, <i>Percis colias</i>; tarakihi, <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/198</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  Rock-cod or Blue-cod - <.I>Percis colias.</i> NUMBER OF FISH RECORDED - 6,286. LOCALITIES - Reported from all the stations <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/209</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  ... hundreds of young red-cod, rarii (<i>Lotella bacchus</i>), and what I believe are young rock-cod, or kokopu or rawaru (<i>Percis colias</i>). The red-cod were from 3 in. to 4 in. in length, and the rawaru, or, as the natives here call them, `toitoi' or `panepane', from 2 in. to 6 in. long. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/319</i>) [NOTES: (Mair Fish in Piako River)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>kopukopu ... 1. Percis colias, rock-cod. = rawaru, pakirikiri. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,162</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> For instance the Rock-cod <i>(Parapercis colias)</i> a comparatively rare line fish in Canterbury waters, has spiny-rayed fins ... <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,200</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 200]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> This [Blue Cod] is the fish known to Captain Cook as the `Coal-fish', and it has frequently been referred to by older authors as the `Rock Cod'. It was known to the Maoris as `Rawaru' or `Pakirikiri'. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,141</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (obsolete) hapuku

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>COD, ROCK</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Lotella rhacinus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Rock Cod; Kelp Cod. (c) Other common names: Parrotfish; Soldiers, Kelp, Kelpies, Moreporks, Marblefish, Maori Chief, Wrasse, Bourbon, Guffy, Cloudy Bay Cod, Rasp. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,18</i>)

>  English Name Rock Cod Native Name Whapuka <i>(1849,NZ Journal,9/125</i>) [NOTES: (quoting Anglo Maori Warder Oct 5)]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The `hapuku' or rock cod as it is called by the whalers - the finest fish of these seas. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,131</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> ... whapuka or rock-cod. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> a dark purplish-brown morid cod of coastal reefs. Also called Cloudy Bay cod, kelp cod, and (inappropriately) southern hake.

>  <b>WEEKES</b> The emigrants have been engaged in fishing over the vessels side [in Cloudy Bay] with a hook and line, catching a number of ling and rockcod <i>(Mar 24, 1841,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Mar 24 Weekes Jnl in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 39]

>  A specimen of this rather rare fish [<i>Physiculus (Lotella) rhacinus</i> (Forster)] was captured off Cape Saunders early in 1924, and kept in captivity for several months. Before its death my curiosity was aroused by one of the staff calling it a `rock-cod' and stating that that was the name used in the north even where the `blue-cod' <i>Parapercis colias</i> was also caught. <i>(1925,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/369</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> (Forster). ROCK-COD or SOUTHERN HAKE <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,23,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> (Forster). Rock cod. <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/405</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> ROCK COD or SOUTHERN HAKE <i>Lotella rhacina</i> (Bloch and Schneider) Up to 1891 this fish was universally known to scientists and fishermen as Hake, then for some unknown reason E. R. Waite (<i>Records of the Canterbury Museum</i>, Vol. 1, No. 3) transferred the name Hake to <i>Merluccius gayi</i>, with Whiting as a second alternative common name. ... The most common name among Otago fishermen was Rock Cod, because this fish favours a rocky habitat. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,172</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> ROCK COD ... <i>Other names: Lotella rhacinus;</i> Cloudy Bay cod, southern hake. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,210</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>COD, ROCK </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Lotella rhacinus</i> . (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Rock Cod; Kelp Cod. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,18</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Rock cod Lotella rhacinus</i> ... Common in rocky habitats around New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,59</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Simon made the acquaintance of barracouta, ling, trumpeter, and rig, and red cod, kelp cod, and rock cod. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,251</i>) [NOTES: -]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Pseudophycis bachus</i>, red cod (q.v.).

>  Rock Cod, or Red Cod, is very common, occurring in the Harbour in large shoals ... <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/486</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i> a rockfish, <i>Acanthoclinus quadridactylus</i>

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> ROCKFISH ... <i>Other names: Acanthoclinus quadridactylus</i>; rock cod, butterfish; taumaka (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,283</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>ROCKFISH</i> (Rock cod) <i>Acanthoclinus quadridactylus </i>... The rockfish is a small elongate species, often called rock cod by small children exploring tide pools. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,209,1984 ed.</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> prison [(prisoners')]

>  <b>McGILL</b> rock college prison. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,93</i>)

### rock fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cheilanthes humilis</i> (formerly <i>sieberi</i>).

>  (And) <i>Cheilanthes Sieberi </i> <i>(Jul 1920,Victorian Naturalist</i>)

>  <b>DOBBIE</b> (And) <i>(Nov 21, 1921,Letters</i>)

>  <b>BROWNSEY & SMITH-DODSWORTH</b> <i>Cheilanthes humilis. </i> <i>(1989,NZ Ferns & Allied Plants</i>)

>  In dry rocky habitats away from the sea, especially on the eastern side of the South Island, another characteristic assemblage of ferns can be found. This includes the rock fern (<i>)Cheilanthes sicheri</i>), woolly cloak fern (<i>C. distans</i>), blanket fern (<i>Pleurosorus rutifolius</i>), and <i>Pellaea callidirupium</i>. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/73</i>)

### rock gurnard
 
 <i>n.</i> = sea perch

>  <b>WILSON</b> Most gurnards are brownish-red but larger specimens are often bright red on top and white below. The Stargazer is sometimes known as the Mud Gurnard, River Gurnard or Mud-Grubber Gurnard but is in fact no relation. Similarly the Sea Perch is sometimes called a Rock Gurnard - <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,118</i>)

RENGA(-) LILY <i> Arthropodium cirratum </i> see also RENGARENGA ... [104] <i> Arthropodium cirrhatum (The Curled Arthropodium) </i>... Usually known as the Rock-lily. [(Renga-lily)]. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,100,1940 , p. 104 ed.</i>)

>  (And) <i> Arthropodium cirratum </i> rock lily <i>(1916,Official Trade Register of Nurserymen</i>)

>  <i> Arthropodium cirratum </i> rock-lily <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology</i>)

>  <b>DUNCAN & DAVIES</b> ... (And) <i> Arthropodium cirratum </i> rock lily <i>(1925,NZ Native Trees</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i> Arthropodium cirrhatum </i> (Forst.f.) R.Br. Common name: Rock lily Maori names: Rengarenga, maikaika, maika ka <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,63</i>)

>  Then there's the rock lily or rengarenga (Arthropodum <i>cirratum</i>, from the asphodel family) and Cordyline <i>australis</i>, our beloved cabbage tree, which is also known as the palm lily, probably from its great panicles of heavily-scented bloom. <i>(Mar 31, 2000,Dominion,18</i>)

>  On the rocky knoll near the middle of the [Karewa] islaet the rengrenga, or rock-lily (<i>Arthropodium cirratum</i>), grows in profusion <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/187</i>)

### rock lobster
 
 <i>n.</i> the marine crayfish Jasus verreauxi and J. edwardsii; a trade name for (esp. export) crayfish tails

>  <i> rock lobster n </i> 1 : SPINY LOBSTER 2 : the flesh of the Cape crawfish esp. when canned or frozen for use as food <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1966</i>)

>  <i>(1969,NZ News,5/3</i>) [NOTES: [crayfish under new regs]]

>  <b>BURTON</b> New Zealand crayfish are usually marketed in the United States as `rock lobster'. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,66</i>)

>  [species name] rock lobster .. [scientific name] <i> Jasus edwardsii </i>.. [other name(s)] crayfish, crayfish spiny, crays red, lobster red, red crayfish, spiny rock lobster <i>(1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <i> rock lobster </i> NZ kind of crayfish. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,660</i>) [NOTES: cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 998]]

>  (`Lament for a Crayfish') A thoroughbred crustacean,/ A well-bred cray, 'tis true,/ Yet Standards put a tag on me,/ `Rock-lobster', Sir, to you!' <i>(1979,SANZ in Reverse,13</i>)

### rock melon
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LUSH</b> Blanche bought 8 fine *rock melons for 4/0... Blanche has reduced 6 of them into jam. <i>(Mar 16, 1871,Journal,105</i>) [NOTES: 1975 edition]

>  At sales during the week prices ranged as follows .. rock melons 3s 6d doz. <i>(Mar 10, 1900,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  GARDEN SEEDS, ex Spray. - On SALE by the Undersigned, TEN PACKAGES, each containing the following Varieties of NEW GARDEN SEEDS .. Rock Melon, Water Melon ... <i>(Jul 31, 1852,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,89</i>)

>  Melons, Rock or Musk. It does not pay to sow melon seeds too early. <i>(1949,Brett's Gardening Guide,87</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The Rock Melons can be hollowed out and used as a receptacle for fruit salad. <i>(1991,Grow Your Own Fruit & Vegetables,86</i>)

>  Rock melons were offering today at 4s a case. <i>(Feb 26, 1910,Evening Post,8</i>)

### rock oyster
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Saxostrea glomerata</i> (See [also Auckland rock oyster](../A#also-auckland-rock-oyster))

>  <b>BURTON</b> The New Zealand rock oyster lives at the tide marks on rocks and wharf piles in the northern half of the North Island. ... Compared with the plump and juicy Bluff oyster, the rock oyster seems a rather withered specimen, ... <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,60</i>)

>  ... the sand banks were well store'd [sic] with Cockles and clams and in many places were Rock Oysters 2 [fn2] <i>Saxostrea glomerata</i> <i>(Nov 11, 1769,Cook's Journals,I/197,1955 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> A long-boat full of rock oysters, too, were brought aboard of us at one time, ... <i>(Nov 11, 1769,Journal,105</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> Q. What sort of fish did you obtain at New Zealand? A.A great variety and many of the fish are very good. There are snappers (schnapper), mullet, bream, soles, eels, and a great quantity of cockles, and some rock oysters. There is likewise abundance of lobsters, or a fish between a lobster and a craw-fish. <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/550</i>) [NOTES: 1821 May Ensign McCrae's Evidence before Commissioner Bigge in 1908 McNab Historical Records]

>  Whilst it was being prepared I strayed along the beach knocking off rock oysters (native name tio). <i>(1940,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement,119</i>) [NOTES: c1846 Weekes My Island in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Ngakihi</i>, ... rock-oyster. <i>(1892,Dictionary of the Maori Language,110</i>)

>  The rock-oyster is found over a large area on these coasts, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,5</i>)

>  Rock oysters are found around Auckland and northwards principally ... In the straits between the Bluff and Stewart Island large beds exist of the flat, or mud oyster. <i>(1913,Country Work,25</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Ngakihi, Ostria glomerata, rock-oyster.</i> [1957/228 <i>Saxostrea glomerata</i> ] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,265</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When I was a child [c1840s] I gathered rock oysters at Waikouaiti .. . <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,49</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Rock oyster .. .. tio, tio para, tio-repe .. .. <i>Crassostrea glomerata </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> fn. see <i>Stewart Island Oyster. </i> <i>(1962,Banks Journal,1/431</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Both rock and mud oysters are found all round New Zealand, but only the North Island rock oyster (restricted to the Auckland district) and the Foveaux Strait mud oyster (not confined to Foveaux Strait) are taken commercially. Our three main oysters are <i>Ostrea hefferdi</i> (southern rock oyster), <i>O. sinuata</i> (mud oyster, ... ), and <i>Saxostrea glomerata</i> (northern rock oyster). <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,79</i>)

>  The rock oyster is native to New Zealand, but similar to the Sydney rock oyster. ... The rock oyster is also known as the Auckland rock oyster. <i>(May 1985,Metro,47/28</i>)

>  [species name] oysters rock .. [scientific name] <i>Saxostrea glomerata</i> .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Rock oyster Saccostrea glomerata</i> ... Native to New Zealand, but very similar to the Sydney rock oyster. ... Once present ... around the northern North Island ... but has now been largely replaced by the Pacific oyster. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,164</i>)

>  The North Island rock oyster is preferred by many people. <i>(Feb 24, 1909,Otago Witness,57</i>)

>  It is useless to tell the people who know about the Rock Oyster and its merits; but those who have never tried them should do so and discover their nutricious [sic], stimulating qualities. <i>(May 8, 1914,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Rock Oysters - 4d per dozen. <i>(Jun 30, 1868,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> These rock-oysters are very small, but deliciously flavoured; ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,89</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> The rough corrugated shelled rock oyster ... are very abundant in places ... <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,134</i>)

### rock shelter
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf Theo Schoon Maori rock shelter]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Rock-shelter, n. a natural cave-dwelling of the aborigines.[1891 <i>(1898,Austral English,392</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an overhang or rock camp, used by musterers, hunters and stock for shelter.

### rock snot
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Didymosphenia geminata</i> [Internet search Feb 2006: also used N Amer (Missouri), Austral] (See [didymo](../D#didymo),[river snot](../R#river-snot))

>  There are fears that <i>Didymosphenia geminata</i> - known as "rock snot" - will choke waterways. <i>(Sep 30, 2005,Dominion Post,A5</i>)

>  The spread of "rock snot" algae in South Island rivers is a foot-and-mouth-level emergency that could devastate New Zealand's clean, green image, environmental scientists say. <i>(Oct 1, 2005,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

>  Known as "rock snot", didymo forms slimy, thick mats on riverbeds and rocks. <i>(Sep 29, 2005,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (informal) a child molester

### rock spike
 
 <i>n.</i> component of post rammer

>  Post rammer, four way tilt, side shift, collapsible mast, rock spike, had little work, $10,500 + GST. <i>(Nov 29, 2003,Hawke's Bay Today,C15</i>)

### rock trout
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Galaxias alepidotus</i>, a freshwater fish, often a kokopu, one of a number of misnamed `trout'. (See [kokopu](../K#kokopu))

>  It is interesting to note that this fish belongs to the same family (Galaxiidae) as the common kokopo, or New Zealand rock trout, and the inanga (the parent of the New Zealand whitebait), but has been placed in a separate genus (Neochanna) on account of the absence of the paired ventral fins, which characteristic occurs, on the contrary, in all species of the genus Galaxias. <i>(Oct 4, 1930,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 61. <i>Galaxias alepidotus.</i> - Cuv., ... Named by the natives of Dusky Bay `He-para,' and by Cook's sailors `Rock-trout.' [Williams 262 <i>Para</i> a fresh-water fish similar to a <i>ko[o]kopu.] </i> <i>(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/219</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach NZ 2/219]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> Also, the settlers of at least some parts of New Zealand have dignified the larger kinds [of <i>Galaxias</i>] with the name of `Trout', or `Rock Trout.' <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,128</i>) [NOTES: 1867 Nov `Ann. and Mag. of Natural History' (Dr.Gunther[u-umlaut] in 1872 Hector Edible Fishes 128]

>  Also, the settlers in at least some parts of New Zealand have dignified the larger kinds [of Galaxias spp, kokopu] with the name of `trout' or `rock-trout' <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/317</i>) [NOTES: (Hector)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Panako - The collector [sc Beattie] asked a Maori about this fish, as it was a new name to him, and received the reply: `The panako is the rock trout, and is nearly extinct. It looks like the kokopara in shape, but it is black in colour.' <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,63</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> MINNOW or WHITEBAIT (INANGA) <i>Austrocobitis attenuatus </i>(Jenyns) ... For a long time it has been known to the Maori and to Europeans that our New Zealand Whitebait is the young of a native freshwater fish commonly known as Minnow, or Inanga or Hiwi to the Maori, and erroneously called a Trout. ... The early settlers called it Maori Trout, Native Trout, Rock Trout and Minnow. But it is not a Trout. Nor is it a Minnow, through it resembles the English freshwater Minnow. While the name Trout is not correct, it is and will always be known as such by farmers and naturalists. In the Bay of Plenty, Whitebait is known to the Maori as Porohoe. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,118</i>)

### rock wren
 
 <i>n.</i> a small bird found amid rocks and streams of the Southern Alps.

>  [Barrington] also dined on kakapo, white-faced herons, kaka, weka, robins, blue ducks, rock wrens - "T'was the smallest joints I ever saw," he later wrote of the tiny perching bird - but is most famed for eating a rat on one of his many desperate and perilous journeys across the Southern Alps. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),48</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 16. XENICUS LONGPIPES. Gml. Rock Wren. ... 17.XENICUS GILVIVENTRIS. Pelz. Rock Wren. <i> X. Haasti,</i> BULLER. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,7</i>)

>  <b>BUL;LER</b> XENICUS GILVIVENTRIS. (ROCK-WREN.) ... [South Island only] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,117,1888 2nd edn 1/111 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The remarkable small rock wren (<i> Xenicus gilviventris </i>), of which I had obtained several specimens previously, was also found to enhabit [sic] the large taluses of debris ... on the sides of the high mountains. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,146</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I never saw the native quail, the cuckoo nor the morepork in the Central [Otago, c1860s], but I saw the sparrow-hawk, the rock wren, and plenty of hawks and pihoihoi (ground larks). <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,121</i>)

>  Amongs[t?] the creepers there are the bush-wren, the rock-wren, the rifleman and the yellow-headed native canary. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,195</i>)

>  <i> Xenicus gilviventris </i> Pelz. Rock-wren. On the authority of Mr. E. H. Atkinson and Major R. A. Wilson, and the writer, this easily overlooked species is recorded from three localities in the Southern Alps. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/88</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  ROCK-WREN <i> (Xenicus gilviventris).</i> - Always rare in this district [sc. North Canterbury]. <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,9/187</i>) [NOTES: (Hope)]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> THE ROCK WREN <i>(Xenicus Gilviventris)</i> [title] [in Westland National Park] <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,143</i>)

>  I have ... seen only one Bush Wren, two Rifle Wrens, two Saddlebacks, and no Rock Wrens [in Nelson]. Notes (Buller) <i>(1893,TrNZI,25/59</i>)

>  The rock-wren and the bush-wren resemble each other generally in the colours of their plumage, but the rock-wren is much rarer. <i>(1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  Whilst up on the barren country, my heart rejoiced to see a fine colony of rock wrens <i>(1935,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,4/107</i>)

>  <i> rock wren n 2 </i>: a small short-tailed passerine bird<i> (Xenicus gilviventris) of New Zealand </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1966</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> ROCK WREN <i> Xenicus gilviventris </i> <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,194</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ...one of the primitive New Zealand wrens, the rock wren <i> (Xenicus gilviventris).</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 3/689 WREN, ROCK (<i> Xenicus gilviventris </i>). This bird is found in the South Island on exposed slopes of the Southern Alps, among rocks above the forest line. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,1/207</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> The green rock wren, <i> X[enicus] gilviventris </i>, lives near rocky and stream beds in the Southern Alps. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,277</i>)

>  <i> 241. Xenicus gilviventris </i> Pelzeln, 1867 <i> Rock Wren </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,64,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i> Rock Wren Xenicus gilviventris </i> PELZELN, 1867 ... European explorers discovered the rock wren in the Southern Alps in the 1860s. The Maoris have no name for the bird and even now it has little contact with people. [Confined to South Island.] <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,266</i>)

>  Rock wrens in Fiordland prized kakapo feathers above all others for their nests, and this passion was exploited by searchers to ascertain if kakapo were in an area <i>(Mar 2002,NZ Geographic,56/39</i>)

>  Another rare bird which has been seen is the beautiful little rock wren. <i>(Sep 7, 1942,Evening Post,4</i>)

### rock-hop
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Sneaking along, he rock-hopped a small stream. <i>Ibid.</i> 128 When I reached the creek I rock-hopped my way down it . <i>(1982,Wild Pig,75</i>)

### rock-hopper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> ROCK-HOPPING One of the most exciting, dangerous and rewarding types of fishing is undoubtedly rock-fishing. The rock-hopper gets added spice in his fishing by his sometimes death-defying attempts to get to the best possie and also to retrieve the fish from a low ledge before the next wave. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,183</i>)

### rock-hopping
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Our first day began at 8am, with four hours of rock-hopping up the north branch of the Hapuku River to reach the foot of the climb. <i>(Nov 23, 2002,Dominion Post,F12</i>)

>  And I've been doing quite a lot of rock-hopping. <i>(Feb 3, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>THOMSON</b> [Photo caption] Rock-hopping to keep dry feet dry. The creek bed is typical of Canterbury high-country streams. With experience one rivals the mountain goat. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,XII</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,183</i>) [NOTES: ROCK-HOPPING One of the most exciting, dangerous and rewarding types of fishing is undoubtedly rock-fishing. ### rocker
 
 <i>n.</i> [(goldmining)]

### rockgas
 
 <i>n.</i> compressed propane gas (LPG). [Jim writes: Proprietary but (?) in wider use cf. Zip. No dictionary reference. DEH adds: In OED without citation : rock gas, natural gas obtained by boring through rock. Webster's 3rd refers one to "natural gas" gas issuing from the earth's crust through natural openings or bored wells esp. any of various combustible gaseous mixtures that when in the dry state contain largely methane and in the wet state in association with petroleum contain also higher hydrocarbons ( as ethane, propane, butanes and pentanes)...]

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> "I use rock gas for the cooking and hot water"... They looked at the rock gas cylinder there. 'Rock Gas', it said on it, sure enough. <i>Ibid.</i> 136 He used one of the rock gas rings which never gave any trouble, and stood shivering as he waited for the jug to heat. <i>(1986,Countless Signs,132</i>) [NOTES: From Owen Marshall (1982) Body and Soul]

### rockling
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [ling](../L#ling))

 <i>n.</i> [fish]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i> Rock-ling </i>, n. a marine fish. The Australian R. is <i> Genypterus australis </i> Cast1n., family <i> Ophidiida.</i> The European R. belongs to the genera <i> Onos </i> and <i> Rhinonemus </i>, formerly <i> Motella </i>. Of the genus <i> Genypterus </i>, Gunther says they have an excellent flesh, like cod, well adapted for curing. At the Cape they are known by the name of `Klipvisch,' and in New Zealand as <i> Ling </i>, or <i> Cloudy-Bay Cod. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,392</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i> Gaidropsarus novae-zelandiae </i>.[sic: no names] <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,24,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i> rockling n </i>, pl <i> rocklings also rockling [rock + ling]</i> 1 : any of several small rather elongate marine cods (family Gadidae); esp : a fish (<i> Gadus mustela </i> or <i> Motella mustela </i>) that is common in tide pools along European coasts and has four barbels on the snout and one beneath the chin - called also <i> five-bearded rockling 2 : an Australian and New Zealand fish (genus <i> Genypterus </i>) of the family Ophidiidae <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1966</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> LING [<i> Genypterus blacodes </i>] ... Also known as rock ling (Australia) and hokarari and hoka (Maori). <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,36</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> The other gadid (true) cod in New Zealand is the rockling, <i> Gaidropsaurus novaezelandiae </i> a small pinkish brown, eel-like fish with one chin and two snout barbels, found under rocks in tide pools and shallow water. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,56</i>)
