### Rogernome
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Rogernomes are set to abolish import licensing, lower tariff barriers and level the playing field. <i>(Oct 8, 1993,Independent (Auckland),10</i>)

>  [Hdg] Rogernomes may no longer be prophets in their own country but there's still money to be made exporting that expertise <i>(Oct 8, 1993,Independent (Auckland),27</i>)

>  The Rogernomes of 20 years ago, like aging men who still use Brylcreem [sic], are pitifully trapped in a timewarp. <i>(Sep 10, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  But even many post-Rogernomes, typically trained in the ideal of the US economy, think we are too small and too distant to survive. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  The Rogernomes have failed to capture the hearts and minds of the young, just as they failed to persuade the rest of us to change their views. <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  The problem was the selfishness, stupidity and blinkered social and political vision of the Rogergnomes. <i>(Jun 29, 1992,Dominion,6</i>)

>  You even get the odd cadaver still who claims to be "value free" like some ghost Rogernome. <i>(Jul 18, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  Dunne is a surviving Rogernome whg now boasts that he left the Labour Party when it attempted to disown its neoliberal past, a position forced upon it by sheer electoral logic. <i>(Aug 10, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  It would not surprise me if every dyed-in-the wool Reaganist, Bushian, Rogergnome and royalist in Aotearoa-New Zealand hasn't had a coronary over Helen Clark's proposal for a constitutional review ... <i>(Nov 17, 2004,NZ Herald,A16</i>)

>  But Rogernomes, having little respect for intellectual quality inconsistent with their ideology, penalised those who identified the flaws in their approach, the struggle collapsed, and we end up with shallow meaningless "research" such as that released by Act. <i>(Feb 7, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

>  .. along with thousands of Labour members who felt similarly betrayed by the policies of the fourth Labour government - fought a long and bitter retreat against the relentless advance of the Rogernomes. <i>(Jul 2, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### Rogernomical
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  For them [sc. the middle class] there is the growing feeling of having been cheated, of having been led to expect (by social conditioning, by Rogernomical hype) that things would be better for them. <i>(Aug 7, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  [Lange] caught the real public mood again by slowing down the Rogernomical juggernaut before it left the planet entirely. <i>(Aug 27, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  The former leader writer for the <i>New Zealand Herald</i> is never far from the surface, nailing the genesis of these tracks to the "merciless" Rogernomical reforms of the 1980's, which required farmers to diversify to make ends meet. <i>(Jul 28, 2009,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  The former leader writer for the <i>New Zealand Herald</i> is never far from the surface, nailing the genesis of these tracks to the "merciless" Rogernomical reforms of the 1980's, which required farmers to diversify to make ends meet. <i>(Jul 28, 2009,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

### Rogernomicise
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  They [cool TV newsreaders] were symbols of a benign paternalism that paraded the daily doses of murder, mayhem and Muldoonism past us and wordlessly reassured us: `There, there, it won't really hurt you. ...' Whatever did happen to that old comfortable world; has it all been Rogernomicised out of existence? Happily no. What we used to call social justice and now term wimpishness still lives. <i>(Nov 7, 1986,National Business Review,11</i>)

### Rogernomicist
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Sure, this is time-warp thinking, and ignores the fact that (a) Labour is as Rogernomicist as ever economically and even, arguably, fiscally and (b) National and Act's policies are more similar than they are different. <i>(Oct 8, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),P 19</i>)

### Rogernomics
 
 <i>n.</i> a label applied to the Lange Labour administration's policies of organisational and economic reform, associated with the name of its first Finance Minister, the Hon. Roger Douglas. [also attrib.]

>  remember those lovely comfy far-off Rogernomics days? <i>(Oct 14, 1993,North & South,22</i>)

>  If the dreaded fiscal wolverines of Rogernomics and Ruthenasia did us one big favour, it was in systematically taking the ambush and mystique out of the Budget process. <i>(Jun 5, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  A woman walks by a row of civil-suited servants, on her back a hairy toy monkey with a sign on its back simply stating ROGERNOMICS. <i>(Jun 14, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  Although most of the times of "stagnation" were in the first 100 years, two long recessions have occurred in the past 50 years: the 10 years or so that followed the wool-price crash in 1966, and the seven or so years of the Rogernomics recession from 1986. <i>(Oct 30, 2010,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  Welch identifies the princpal achievements of Clark: a rebalancing of her Rogernomics inheritance; leaving a fractious and close-to-collapse Labour Party considerably stronger than she found it; significant progressive social policies ... <i>(Aug 15, 2009,Listener (New Zealand Listener),40</i>)

>  Labour leader Helen Clark is questioning the appointment of one of the "high priests of Rogernomics and Ruthanasia" as the new head of the Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet. <i>(Oct 13, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

>  The new difficulty arose when the Crown proposed to transfer them to the flagships of Rogernomics, the State Owned Enterprises (SOEs). <i>(Jun 13, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  Rogernomics kicked out the props from agriculture and a huge make-believe edifice came tumbling down. <i>(Jun 13, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  They were stopped midstream by the cuppa tea fellow and prevented from instituting the Rogercare side to Rogernomics. <i>(Apr 14, 1996,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

>  The arrival of Rogernomics convinced me that political life had gone well beyond a joke. <i>(Dec 11, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  First examined by a working group in 1985, the Electricity (Hazards from Trees) Regulations have lurked in the bureaucracy and the lower reaches of various ministers' in-tray through the Anzus stand-off, Rogernomics, Ruth Richardson's Mother of All Budgets, the America's Cup win, the advent of MMP and the turn of the millennium. <i>(Jan 30, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

>  They speak of a time before Rogernomics and Ruthanasia, when issues were clear-cut, and people still cared enough to put their bodies on the line for something bigger than themselves. <i>(Sep 13, 1996,Independent (Auckland),18</i>)

>  We hear a lot these days about the so-called "Rogernomics", a name derived from the so-called "Reagonomics", which in turn is shorthand for "supply-side economics". Both these and "Thatchernomics" ultimately derive their inspiration from the dreaded "Friedmanite monetarism" which does not.. have anything to do with a hectoring American economist who is keen on the works of Monet, but instead has everything to do with a hectoring American economist who has, over the last 10 years, thrown millions of people out of work throughout the Western world. <i>(Jun 15, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  Immigration consultancy is a whole new industry, spawned of Rogernomics and its attendant philosophies of deregulation. <i>(Nov 19, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  You are, in fact, an entrepreneur, one of the vibrant new species expected to come away in a carpet of spring green after the bushfire of Rogernomics has razed the old and untoward. <i>(Nov 19, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  In 1995 Kelsey's book systematically challenged that which we have come to call Rogernomics, or could call Prebblemania, and woke us from the daydream of the New Zealand success story. <i>(May 24, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),50</i>)

>  In 2002, Colin James argued that Helen Clark's government was operating during a period of consolidation, smoothing out some of the most glaring social inequalities created by the 1984-92 policy revolution, while consolidating the key economic changes of Rogernomics and Ruthanasia. <i>(Sep 11, 2005,NZ Herald,Herald on Sunday 4</i>)

>  Take "Rogernomics". for instance. The term was born on February 23, 1985, as a headline on a <i>Listener</i> interview with Roger Douglas. The following week it was picked up by Bernard Lagan in the <i>Dominion</i> and went on from there to enter the language as a term for mad dog monetarism, Kiwi style. <i>(Apr 3, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  The worst of these misbegotten schemes was Rogernomics, which empowered a handful of selfish and cunning manipulators to amass vast sums of money in the misguided belief that they would make some of it available to lesser mortals. <i>(Jan 28, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),83</i>)

>  I found myself thrown once more against the aforementioned forces of cultural darkness, a truly awful coalition led by Mammon and the dreaded Roger Nomic, supported by Low Common Denominator, Market Madman and Treasury Philistine. <i>(Jul 16, 1992,Dominion,12</i>)

>  It is basic to the Rogernomics creed that political action be decisive and as far as possible irreversible. <i>(Jun 1992,Metro,76</i>)

>  Echoes of Rogernomics, which split left and right factions of the Labour Party, have been reverberating through the High Court at Auckland this week. <i>(Jun 10, 2003,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  <b>JAMES</b> At the heart of the discontent was "Rogernomics" - the popular word for the economic approach of Roger Douglas. Rogernomics deregulated where Muldoon had regulated, turned over to market forces those whom Muldoon had subsidised, cut taxes where Muldoon had raised them, subjected employers and unions to the painful wage discipline of controls on the money supply, exposed Muldoon's State departments to the market by turning trading operations into corporations, and requiring some other departments to earn part of their keep by selling their services. <i>(1989,Rogernomics,1</i>)

>  What transformed the two major parties into the activist-based organisations they are today? Rogernomics and Ruthanasia. <i>(Aug 18, 2006,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  By the time Rogernomics and Ruthanasia had run their course, New Zealanders had lost control of their finance sector, most of their news media, and much else besides. <i>(Sep 3, 2010,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  If you doubt this, consider the electorate's rejection of Rogernomics in 1990, and Ruthanasia in 1993. <i>(Feb 3, 1995,Dominion,8</i>)

>  A second doubt is that the first seven years of the Rogernomics regime were disastrous, as the accompanying table shows. <i>(Mar 12, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  Mike Lee sees these ideas as another example of "Roger politics", the phrase coined by Auckland University's Professor Richard Mulgan to describe, as Lee puts it, "the erosion of democratic organisations or their abolition - such as harbour boards, power boards, hospital boards - and the consequent diminishing of public involvement." <i>(Apr 27, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

### Rogernomist
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Labour's Rogernomist backbones were ill-wished away and, unable to be appeased, the NewLabour dissenters were waved off crisply. <i>(Dec 9, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### rogue
 
 <i>v.</i> (with reference to peas) [also roguing verbal noun (below)]

>  <b>WALLIS</b> Occasionally a woman would pick something up from her table and toss it into a tin can. `Roguing peas,' said Laker. ... He had heard of the practice of roguing. In any given crop of peas it was inevitable there would be some foreign strains, some rogues, which had to be removed if the seed was to be used to grow canning peas. Some of the roguing ... was done in the paddock before harvesting ... <i>(1963,Point of Origin,38</i>)

### rogue and villain
 
 <i>n.</i> a shilling [rhyming slang]

>  I plonk 10 rogue and villains on its I suppose, home it rolls and I'm winning a crayfish. rogue and villain....shillin(g) <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### rogue ram
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a ram that escapes from an enclosed area to impregnate ewes.

>  Unfortunately a rogue ram had joined some of these ewes early and 24 of the control ewes and 22 of the treated ewes had lambed. <i>(Jul 2004,Country-Wide,39</i>)

### roha
 
 <i>n.</i> a stingray

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SHORTTAIL STINGRAY ... <i>Other names: Dasyatis brevicaudatus</i>; stingray, stingaree; whai, pakaurua, oru, roha (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,192</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> STINGRAY, SHORTTAIL Dasyatis brevicaudatus: ... Also known in New Zealand by the Maori name whai, pakaurua, oru and roha. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,201</i>)

### rohe
 
 <i>n.</i> area; boundary; district; land; zone [ORIGIN: Maori, [Ma. <b>rohe (-a)</b> margin, territory, outfield, (sport), hand net] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 222. *Judge Durie thought this was a neo-Maori-ism, I understand]

>  Services are to be delivered throughout the rohe of Mataatua and as such the co-ordinator will need to demonstrate ... <i>(Jan 18, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,15</i>)

>  The bill [the Taranaki Whanaui ki Te Upoko o Te Ika) Claims Settlement Bill] outlines the key elements of the rohe. The group's rohe is defined as conforming to the boundaries of the Port Nicholson Block. <i>(Dec 24, 2008,Dominion Post,B9</i>)

>  A defining feature of the Ngai Tahu settlement was the "bolt-ons" - a deferred selection process that allowed the tribe 12 months to buy selected Crown assets, and a perpetual right of first refusal on every surplus Crown asset in its rohe, or tribal area. <i>(Feb 10, 2001,Dominion,28</i>)

>  Ngati Wai numbers about 7000. Its rohe stretches from Cape Brett in the north to Pakiri and takes in coastal islands including the Poor Knights. <i>(Dec 13, 2005,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  <b>Michael King</b> The country as a whole was divided into a notional network of rohe over which particular iwi and hapu held mana whenua or authority over the land and its resources. <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,71</i>)

>  Mana Wahine means the empowerment of women and that could be within their home, their workplace, their community and their rohe. <i>(Apr 20, 2007,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

>  The comments going around the rohe were, "I hope the people who've got it don't return it." <i>(Oct 17, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  The 1870-72 Urewera expeditions were driven by a vengeful Colonial Government (also known as the Kawanatanga), desperate to track down and destroy Te Kooti Arikirangi Te Turuki and his Ringatu guerilla force then regrouping within the boundaries of the Tuhoe tribe's forested rohe. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

### rohe moana
 
 <i>n.</i> a customary seafood-gathering area [ORIGIN: Maori, =territorial waters (P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 223); different sense here]

>  The customary food-gathering /rohe moana follows the coastline ... <i>(Oct 11, 2006,Wairarapa News,36</i>)

>  .. the Kairakau Lands Trust .. applied to the Minister of Fisheries for mataitai reserve within the boundaries of the Kairakau Lands Trust rohe moana. <i>(Nov 4, 2006,Dominion Post,C15</i>)

### Rohe Potae
 
 <i>n.</i> King Country [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>HUTT</b> I... will send a message to my people... to say that the word of our fathers is to stand, and that no liquor shall come into the Rohe Potae." <i>(1991,Maori & Alcohol,103</i>) [NOTES: See <i>Evening Post</i> 8 June 1910 p. 3, quoted in Skerman <i>The Dry Era... </i> MA thesis Univ. Auckland, 1972, p. 131, fn. 70]

>  The Ringatu leader fled into the headwaters of the Waiau River country, now ultimately intent on making for the King Country (the Rohe Potae still held by the powerful Tainui Confederation under King Tawhiao Te Wherowhero)... Their simple act of wading ashore onto the western banks of the independent Te Rohe Potae effectively ended the complex New Zealand Wars. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

### rohi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [roi](../R#roi))

>  <b>KOHERE</b> Even today, the Maoris of Wairoa regard rohi (loaf, or baker's bread) as the cause of a tribal calamity. <i>(1951,Autobiography of a Maori,52</i>)

### rohito
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> A very similar wood [to the Kahikatoa] exists to the southward, called Rohito, of which carved boxes, for holding small trinkets and feathers, are made by the people. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/395</i>)

### rohutu
 
 <i>n.</i> a native shrub of the myrtle family. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  More than sixty kinds of wood can be utilised for various purposes. .. There yet remain to be cited the following kinds, which, although little employed at the present time are notwithstanding very serviceable. The rohutu (myrtus bullata, Hooker), employed in cabinet work ... <i>(May 25, 1865,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  A small settlement on the Wata-roa is Rohutu, (the name of a small tree, myrtus obcordata). <i>(Jun 17, 1908,Grey River Argus,1</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> As the brush-like flowers suggest, rohutu shares the myrtle family with rata, pohutukawa, feijoa and guava. <i>(2009,Which Native Forest Plant?,43</i>)

>  They were joined by companion plants - such as <i>Myrsine divaricata</i>(weeping matipo), <i>Lophomyrtus obcordata</i> (rohutu), <i>Coprosma propinqua</i> (swamp coprosma), and <i>Olearia virgata</i> (swamp tree daisy) - to re-create an entire plant community and ultimately a self-sustaining rare plant population. <i>(Oct 2003,Footnotes,No. 20/7</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Rohutu, <i>Col[enso]. Myrtus pedunculata. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  ... the curious red fruit of the Titoki, or Titongi, (<i>Alectryon excelsum</i>); the fruit of the Tutu-papa (<i>Coriaria thymifolia</i>); of the New Zealand Bramble, Tataramoa, (<i>Rubus australis</i>); of two of the New Zealand Myrtles, the Ramarama (<i>Myrtus bullata</i>), and the Rohutu (<i>M. pedunculata</i>); of several species of Coprosma, - particlularly the Karamu (<i>C. lucida</i>), and <i>C. robusta</i>), of the Papaauma (<i>C. grandifolia</i>), and of the two littoral species, Taupata (<i>C. retusa</i>), and Tataraheke (<i>C. acerosa</i>); of the Koropuku (<i>Gaultheria depressa</i>); ... and of the Kareao, or Pirita, (<i>Rhipogonum parviflorum</i>). <i>Ibid.</i> 1III/50 (known hard woods.) The Mairetawhake, (<i>Eugenia Maire</i>); the Rohutu (<i>Myrtus pedunculata</i>), ... The Maire (<i>Santalum Cunninghamii</i>) ... the Manoao (<i>Dacrydium Colensoi</i>), a small hard-wooded pine ... <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Myrtus pedunculata Tohutu <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  [Native name] Rohutu .. [Settlers' name] Rohutu .. [Family] Myrtaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>FEATON</b> MYRTUS OBCORDATA.<i> (Hook.F.) </i>...The `ROHUTU'. This myrtle is common to both Islands. <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Rohutu [Maori name] .. Rohutu [Settlers' name] .. Myrtus obcordata .. Shrub <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,167</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> I also found a species of <i>Myrtus</i>, a small slender tree bearing orange-coloured juicy berries ... [The natives] call the tree rohutu. [fn] <i>Myrtus obcordata</i>, Rohutu <i>(1894,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1894 Colenso Excursion Nth Isd in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 29]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Myrtus pedunculata (The Pedunculate Myrtle)</i> ... Maori name <i>Rohutu. </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,288,[1940, p. 305] ed.</i>)

>  <i>Myrtus obcordata</i> Hook. f. .. Rohutu. <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/144</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> The ROHUTU (<i>M[yrtus] obcordata</i>) is more widespread [than RAMARAMA, M. bullata] ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,230,1929 ed.</i>)

>  ... a jungle of pepperwood (<i>Pseudowintera colorata</i>), rohutu (<i>Neomyrtus pedunculatus</i>), or the crown fern (<i>Blechnum discolor</i>) does not represent regeneration ... but marks areas of extreme modification. <i>(1971,VUWTC'71,55</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Lophomyrtus obcordata</i> (Raoul) Burr. Maori name: <i>Rohutu</i> ... This shrub, and the closely allied <i>Neomyrtus pedunculata</i> (Hook.f.) Allan are found throughout New Zealand. Both are known as `rohutu', and it is possible that both were used for medicine. <i>Lophomyrtus obcordata</i> was known as <i>Myrtus obcordata</i> (Raoul) Hook.f. and <i>Neomyrtus pedunculata</i> was known as <i>Myrtus pedunculata</i> Hook.f. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,72</i>)

>  ROHUTU/ <i>Myrtus pedunculata</i>/ Shrub reaching 5m. <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,10</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Neomyrtus pedunculata</i> ROOHUTU ... Shrub or small tree up to 6 m ...(=<i>Myrtus pedunculata</i>). <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,61</i>)

>  758 Lithograph of rohutu foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,31</i>)

### roi
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Ar|oi, s. Fern-root. <i>Ibid.</i> 203 R|oi, s. Fern-root: <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,136</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The rourou, or plain, was covered with the<i> roi</i>, or fern, and the ever-flowering kaikatoa [a2-acute], which emitted a fragrant odour. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/397 The principal sustenance, anterior to the time of Cook, was the <i>roi,</i> or fern-root, answering the same purpose that bread does with us. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/293 The<i> rohi</i>, or fern, flourishes in an infinity of species - upwards of sixty distinct varieties have been noticed by botanists. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/105</i>)

>  We were regaled with Rohe (Fern root). <i>(Apr 8, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,343,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Roi, Col[enso]. Pteris aquilina</i> (root of). <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> 971 [<i>Pteris aquilina. Linn. var. esculenta, Hook.f.</i>] <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,971</i>)

>  Certain places became noted for `roi,' the edible rhizome. <i>(1915,TrNZI,47/79</i>)

### rokeroke
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of potato [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> R|oke r|oke; A species of the potatoe. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,203</i>)

### roki
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> fern-roots (roki) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/97</i>)

### rolie
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Dr John Dobson, a drug clinic consultant ..., says flunitrazepan, marketed as Rohypnol, is supposed to be a sleeping tablet. But it has become a favourite with pill popping addicts, and `rolies' as they are known, sell on the street for up to $7 each. ... `It's the drug that druggies use.' <i>(Apr 30, 1989,Dominion,3</i>)

### roll
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was an unfortunate day, cold S.W. showers falling and a great roll came up before we got to the ship. <i>(Aug 30, 1877,Chudleigh Diary,261,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [(farmers')]

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> It is difficult to imagine how these two men could skirt, roll, class and carry away the 1600 or so fleeces shorn in a day. Two pressers were paid 1/- a bale. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,89</i>)

>  Commenced shearing at five a.m. I was picking up and rowling [sic] the fleeces <i>(Jan 30, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,75,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Regarding the respective values of the clips, it is hardly necessary to state that fleeces from March-shorn sheep were not able to be rolled. From them only stained wool and bellies were sorted out ... A star bale sold at a crutching sale made 101/4d., while a fadge of the same wool pooled later made 121/4 pence .. <i>(1929,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,10/326</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> state of the sea where large swells are present

>  <b>HARGREAVES</b> When it's got a big roll on it's no good for fishing round the point, it's too rough. But that's good for all the surfers. <i>(1998,On the Next Tide,27</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [shearing; ca 1920 (W.H.B. Orsman) I'm rolling this season. Not so hard as pressing]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Meanwhile the `pickers up' were busy gathering up the fleeces as they fell from the bereft sheep and carrying them to the sorting table, where they [173] were stripped of the `pieces', which were thrown aside, while the best of the fleece was quickly classified, rolled together, and deposited in a kind of bin, according to its quality, whether `clothing' or `combing.' <i>(1881,Waitaruna,172</i>)

>  I then muster for Brown and Engst shears last. I put Manuel and Lyne on to shear to. ... I press. The general hand Hobbs picks up and rolls fleeces. <i>(Dec 9, 1884,Chudleigh Diary,331</i>)

>  I am to keep and pay a cook ... 1 roller who expects to roll 1000 fleeces a day ..., one fleece boy at 5/- per diem, 2 press men .. <i>(Nov 5, 1904,Chudleigh Diary,425</i>)

>  J.Dix offered to take the responsibility of the shearing at Taupeka. He would bring 4 good shearers and a cook and roll and sort himself. <i>(Dec 7, 1906,Chudleigh Diary,438,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> [Helping press wool] was only one of the many ways I was glad to put in a week-end, if it was not skirting and rolling for some good friend. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,18</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A steam whistle ... blew for the commencement and finish of each run. The wool tables for rolling and skirting were in the centre of the woolboard. Close by stood the classer's table, where he sat and classed each fleece before it was carried by a shed hand to its respective bin. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,113</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [criminal slang]

>  <b>JENKYNS</b> To `roll drunks' was to frequent drinking saloons, to follow any man who left drunk, roll him in the gutter, and rob him .. <i>(1892,Hard Life,165</i>)

>  Kauhata held Dempsey while Nathan went through Dempsey's pockets. Nathan from the dock said [however] that he had not `rolled' Dempsey. <i>(Jul 30, 1963,NZ Truth,5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [fight, beat up]

>  Lucas had told him of a fight he had had ... He said he had rolled a guy .... Williams said the expression `rolled a guy' meant belted him up. <i>(Jul 22, 1963,NZ Truth,3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> roll one's own, roll (oneself) one (cf roll-y-own n; the makings) [roll your own roll your own (cigarettes)] (See [roll your own](../R#roll-your-own))

>  But then Charlie would have rolled himself one and looked up at the hills and pretended he didn't hear. <i>(1952,Arena,31:2</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I said if he liked he could take my tin and go roll himself one. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,171</i>)

>  Roll your own Cigarette while you stroll. <i>(Jul 1, 1916,NZ Truth,9</i>)

>  "He wouldn't give his own mother a roll." <i>Ibid.</i> 35/1 Acie rolled the coarse spring rolls of hair with a paper and lit up. <i>Ibid.</i>35/4 "If we play it right they'll give us a roll." <i>(Dec 29, 1965,Weekly News,35/2</i>)

>  <b>COWIE</b> At least half the cigarette smokers [of Christchurch] `rolled-their-own' <i>(1937,New Zealand From Within,13</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> While he rolled himself one he looked across the flat ... - <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,111</i>)

>  <b>CHERRILL</b> I mostly roll my own cigarettes down home. <i>(1950,Story of a NZ Sheepfarm,40</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [(? goldminers', ? nonce)]

>  The reef ... was very flat-lying, and appeared to `roll' with the contour of the country. <i>(1912,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.15 (NS) [p?]</i>) [NOTES: (Bell and Fraser, Waihi-Tairia)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a rolled cigarette,a roll-your-own

>  <i>(1952,Arena,31/2</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),167</i>) [NOTES: 1953 Middleton in 1966 Short Stories 167 [?187]]

>  <i>(Dec 29, 1965,Weekly News,35/2</i>) [NOTES: [OEDS]]



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [wool handling] to roll up a fleece after skirting.

>  The fleece-picker takes the fleece by the hind legs, and gathers it towards him; takes it to the rolling table, and throws it out with the outside of the fleece upwards; the roller skirts it, rolls it up tightly, and hands it to the classer, who puts the fleece in the proper bin. <i>(Nov 12, 1902,Otago Witness,74</i>)

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> Commenced shearing at five a.m. I was picking up and rowling [sic] the fleeces. <i>(Jan 30, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,75</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> The best of the fleece was quickly classified, rolled together and deposited in a kind of bin. <i>(1881,Waitaruna,173</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> It is difficult to imagine how these two men could skirt, roll, class and carry away the 1600 or so fleeces shorn in a day. <i>(1982,Blue Cliffs,89</i>)

>  ROLLING FLEECES. - An experienced person should always be employed to roll up wool. <i>(Nov 11, 1865,Otago Witness,16</i>)



 
<b>7</b> <i>n.</i> to defeat in sport

>  [Hdg] Helter-skelter All Blacks roll South Africa... It was the All Black's first World Cup win against the Springboks... <i>(Sep 2003,Sunday Star Times,A1</i>)

### roll in
 
 <i>v.</i> turn in

>  <b>STRONACH</b> About nine o'clock we rolled in, after putting our gear where it could be easily found in the dark. The ground was hard. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,10</i>)

### roll off
 
 <i>v.</i> (of barrels of oil) [(whalers') 1839] (See [cf. stow down](../S#stow-down))

### roll one's swag
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [farm work] to move to another location or when mustering, to pack and move to another mustering hut or camp.

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Got my cheque for L3 then rolled my swag and put it on my back. <i>(Aug 8, 1912,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. we had no option but to roll our swags and move on. .. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,7</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> They'll be rolling swags in the bunkroom, fixing the pack and saddles too ... <i>(1997,Different Worlds,23</i>)

### roll the knot
 
 <i>phr.</i> [(obs. musterers')] (See [cf roll n](../C#cf-roll-n))

>  <b>PETER NEWTON</b> `Roll the knot means to roll one's swag - musterers on the bigger places shift camp as the muster progresses and as their `gear' consists of blankets and a change of clothes, this is rolled into a ...bundle for loading onto the packhorse whenever they shift camp. <i>(Apr 21, 1952,Letter</i>)

### roll up
 
 <i>v.</i> to attend, turn up

>  The band commences operations by playing an overture, as a notice, I presume, for the public to "roll-up and be in time," and this is a signal to the miners, who are camped in large numbers at the back of the town, to strike up also. <i>(Sep 26, 1865,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  The members of the Tukapa Football Club are requested to roll up for practise [sic] on Friday morning at 9.45 o'clock. <i>(Apr 12, 1900,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>FARRELL</b> `The miners all rolled up to see the fun.' <i>(1887,How He Died,26</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> to gather, to assemble. <i>(1898,Austral English,393</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> to roll up at a meeting, [etc] these are phrases which came almost as easily to the New Zealand tongue as to the Australian. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  roll up v.int. to attend a meeting, whence ~ n. the attendance at a meeting. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  roll-up v. arrive; n. informal game of bowls. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),17</i>)

>  roll up ... NZ & Austral. sl. congregate or assemble, colloq. arrive in vehicle or on scene; roll-up NZ & Austral. sl. assembly, meeting. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1000; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 422]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i>

>  ... the latter is always skirted and classed, whereas the black fleece was rolled up just as taken from the sheep's back, the belly-pieces being also left in. ... For example, white wool divides, when skirted by the roller at the wool-table, thus: Belly pieces, say, 6d. per pound; first pieces, 8d. per pound; ... fragments or locks, 4d. per pound. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/196</i>)

### roll your own
 
 <i>phr.</i> [aka roll-your-own, roll one's own, roll his own, roll her own. NB active and passive verbal use, also adjectival]

>  Prize Crop. For Every Man who Rolls his Own. <i>(Sep 15, 1939,Dominion,13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> an illegal activity, specific. smoking [(adolescents') 1984 16-17 F E3 Pakuranga Coll. 33 ROLL UP [M4 F4]]

### roller
 
 <i>n.</i> [criminal slang]

 <i>n.</i> Farming: in sheep-shearing, one who rolls the shorn fleeces. [[? transfer to WOOL-ROLLER] [1890's see shearing shed hands]]

>  .. the latter is always skirted and classed, whereas the black fleece was rolled up just as taken from the sheep's back, the belly-pieces being also left in. ... For example, white wool divides, when skirted by the roller at the wool-table, thus: Belly pieces, say, 6d. per pound; first pieces, 8d. per pound; ... fragments or locks, 4d. per pound. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/196</i>)

>  <b>SWANTON</b> There is the wool-classer with his assistant rollers who number five or six. <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,96</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> At shearing time [at Benmore, 1892] about 55 men were employed in the shed: 28 to 30 shearers, 2 penners-up, 4 pickers-up, 2 broomies, 6 rollers, 1 classer, 1 fleece carrier, 4 piece sorters, 3 pressers, 1 bale brander, 1 butcher and brander. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,208,1954 ed.</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (the making of) a roll-your-own cigarette

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) of shearing, the bundling of wool fleeces for pressing.

>  <b>PERRY</b> There are many ways of rolling, but two common ways are as follows:- (1) First throw in the neck so that it will rest on the shoulder and then turn in the two sides to the middle back. Commencing at the tail, roll the fleece towards the neck. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,123</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> The wool tables for rolling and skirting were in the centre of the woolboard. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,113</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> I had learned quite a lot about shed work when we work at Kekerengu and was enjoying penning up, fleece picking, skirting and rolling when Whit asked if I would like to have a go. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,51</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> Rolling. - When the wool is opened at the sale it is in the best interests of the seller if the best and most attractive part of the fleece, viz., the shoulder, is exposed to view. ... There are many ways [123] of rolling, but two common ways are as follows:- (1) First throw in the neck so that it will rest on the shoulder and then turn in the two sides to the middle of the back. Commencing at the tail, roll the fleece towards the neck. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,122</i>)

>  4. Rolling [heading] There are several methods of rolling a fleece. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,708</i>)

### rolling road
 
 <i>n.</i> [kauri logging]

>  As I went along this creek, I was struck by the great number of rolling roads crammed full of logs - piles of them heaped in the creek whereon the roads abutted; at least about two thousand logs are now awaiting a long desired fresh. <i>(Dec 31, 1862,Daily Southern Cross,9</i>)

>  We went in by the muddy rolling road, all overgrown with white clover and grass, hearty aliens which it would have greatly astonished Captain Cook to find on the banks of the new Thames. <i>(Apr 1, 1902,NZ Illustrated Magazine,60</i>)

>  The high road, the rolling road, that leads unto the Hutt, / is under Atkinson's control and free from hump or rut. <i>(Sep 30, 1916,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> When growing at any considerable distance from a creek or river it is necessary to form rolling roads. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,150</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> An article in the Building World, 1889, had this to say: `Logging the Kauri in New Zealand is a rather exciting occupation - that is by the `rolling road' method of extraction ...' <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,172</i>)

>  Flotation is the method most commonly used in the kauri districts ... After the trunks are cut into lengths a `rolling-road' is formed to the nearest creek. All the scrub is closely cut down for a width of from twenty-five to thirty feet ... to obtain an approximately even surface, inclined towards the creek. The logs are forced along this road by timber-jacks, which the bushmen use with remarkable skill, and are impelled with a rapidity which is simply astonishing to persons unused to the process. If the creek contains plenty of water the logs float to the nearest booms without further trouble ... <i>(1894,NZ Official Yearbook,331</i>) [NOTES: (T. Kirk)]

>  <b>REED</b> Hochstetter in 1889 and Kirk in 1874 [Report to Parliament], refer to rolling roads. Kirk describes them as `broad tracks, thirty to forty feet wide, in which every advantage is taken of the natural incline of the surface, and from which all trees have to be removed, the stumps cut level with the surface, inequalities roughly levelled, and large holes filled up. The logs are forced along these roads by `timberjacks' until they reach the water. In this way the logs are moved with great ease. ...[D.H.McMillan] Rolling roads were formed by laying three rickers or other small trees lengthwise to the line of road, and logs were placed crosswise on this road. There might be thirty or forty or more in a fleet. Three men with timber lacks usually operated this job. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,170</i>)

>  ... what was called the rolling road ... was anything up the 30 feet wide, with all the stumps cut down to ground level ... A lot of loose skids would probably be laid on the ground running in the direction the log would travel. The logs were then rolled along the road with jacks, usually two men to a log. <i>(1951,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: [ca -1914] 1951 H.S. McCo[?a]rroll The Days of the Kauri Bushmen [Radio 4: TS]]

>  But before this [treefelling] has been done rolling-roads have been formed, or tramways laid, involving the vast destruction of arboreal growth ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/16</i>)

### rolling table
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) wool table in a woolshed, on which fleeces are rolled before binning.

>  On turning round he beheld Mr Boyd attired in blue dungaree overalls, he having been engaged assisting at the rolling tables. <i>(Dec 20, 1894,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  There are many so-called wool-classers whose experience is limited to a few years at the rolling-table. <i>(1883,Brett's Colonists Guide,215</i>)

>  The fleece-picker takes the fleece by the hind legs, and gathers it towards him; takes it to the rolling table, and throws it out with the outside of the flecce upwards; the roller skirts it, rolls it up tightly, and hands it to the classer, who puts the fleece in the proper bin. <i>(Nov 12, 1902,Otago Witness,74</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> Fleece-picking. - As soon as a shearer has taken the fleece off and let the sheep go through the port-hole to the counting-out pen, the picker gathers the fleece up in such a manner that she or he can on getting to the rolling table, throw it out with the breech part at the right end. <i>(1913,Country Work,14</i>)

### rongoa
 
 <i>n.</i> remedy, medicine; traditional Maori healing [ORIGIN: Maori, aka rongoa Maori; includes attributive usage]

>  Rongoa encompasses traditional herbal remedies using native plants for such disorders as stomach complaints, bruises, toothache, colds and sore throats as well as traditional massage and prayer. <i>(Jan 22, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A5</i>)

>  He calls rongoa mumbo jumbo because it combines herbal remedies delivered within a Maori cultural context and that includes prayer. <i>(Jan 30, 2006,NZ Herald,A16</i>)

>  He calls his mixture rongoa, Maori for medicine, and administers it in one or two teaspoonful lots. Up to 20 ounces of cannabis produce about five litres of milk. <i>(Jun 20, 2004,Sunday Star Times,A7</i>)

>  Rongoa [traditional medicines] were stored in unlabelled paper bags. No sterilising equipment was available for medicine preparation and the rongoa were made in a garage overhang using aluminium pots and water from a garden tap. <i>(Nov 4, 2003,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> Stacked against one of the walls of the tent are the rongoa and the herbal pharmacy that Paraiti draws on for her work. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,251</i>)

>  Hohepa Kereopa could not read and write as a youngster, instead committing to memory traditional Maori medicine and healing methods called rongoa Maori... After learning from his many aunties, Mr Kereopa is recognised as the foremost Tuhoe rongoa expert. <i>(Jul 28, 2001,Dominion,9</i>)

>  There is much evidence on Maori rongoa and many of those that have used it swear by it. <i>(Feb 14, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,2</i>)

>  He [Hohepa De La Mere] is devoted to not only treating people, but to passing on his knowledge of rongoa maori. <i>(May 10, 2005,Dominion Post,TV week news,  P4</i>)

>  Hohepa Kereopa could not read and write as a youngster, instead committing to memory traditional Maori medicine and healing methods called rongoa Maori... After learning from his many aunties, Mr Kereopa is recognised as the foremost Tuhoe rongoa expert. <i>(Jul 28, 2001,Dominion Post,9</i>)

>  Te Whare O Kenehi workers Margaret and Tom Tuakana will be showing walkers the wide range of plants available in the Rotorua region that can be used in traditional Maori medicine (rongoa) and how to collect them without harming the environment. <i>(Sep 15, 2011,Daily Post (Rotorua),A004</i>)

>  Their clinic is a registered organisation and they provide a variety of free services including traditional rongoa, mirimiri (Maori massage) and other health checks. <i>(Sep 15, 2011,Daily Post (Rotorua),A004</i>)

>  Teresa Wall, the minstry's deputy director-general of Maori health, told a newpaper earlier this year that "the mere fact that people use rongoa and go back to the practitioners is testament that the services they are given are contributing to their wellbeing". <i>(Jun 21, 2009,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  Today there are about 30 rongoa clinics around the country, 15 of them funded by the Health Ministry to promote wellness through massage, prayer, spiritual conselling and cultural support. <i>(Jun 21, 2009,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

### Rongopai
 
 <i>n.</i> gospel, religion

>  The words <i>Rongo pai</i> are those used by the missionaries for the gospel, as to signify "good news," ... <i>(Jun 10, 1865,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,4</i>)

>  The speeches were all to the same effect. The substance was this - That Mr. Turton was one of those who had brought them the good tidings ('rongo pai') of the Gospel, and was now forsaking his work to teach them the law; that they could not understand or reconcile this, or as some termed it, worshipping Mammon. <i>(Aug 5, 1859,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  He would carry his "Rongo Pai" to Poverty Bay, and then on to the East Cape. <i>(Jul 26, 1884,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> The old tohunga Maori, schooled in the ancient religion, were the first to accept Pai-marire; they were astute enough to recognize that by adopting it they would secure the ancient ascendancy of their class over the people which the Rongopai had impaired but not destroyed. <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads,133</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> He was a middle-aged man when the missionary impulse set him seeking a field beyond his tribal district, in the cause of the new religion, the Rongo Pai, and the reconciliation of old enemies. <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,53</i>)

>  rongopai, Gospel (rongo, to hear or something heard; pai, good, good news). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### rongotea
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of wheat. [ORIGIN: Maori]

In Medium to high yiled potential areas, the choice of autumn sown cultivars is between Rongotea and Kopara. <i>(May 1982,NZ Journal of Agriculture,22</i>)

>  Tiritea was bred from the dame parental combination that gave rise to Rongotea .. Tiritea resembles its sister line, Rongotea, in height and maturity. <i>(1982,NZ Journal of Agriculture,23</i>)

### roody doody
 
 <i>n.</i> probably an early spg (as KOW-ROWDY-DOWDY) of Maori ruriruri, Williams 352 Ruri, ruriruri ... Song, ditty, generally of an amorous nature, accompanied by gestures.: and English dialect rowdy-rowdy. kow- = ?Maori ko[o] sing; resound; a shout; or perhaps ko a particle. [cf OED rowdy-dowdy `characterised by noisy roughness.' (1882); EDD Rowdy-dowdy: Sc. Ches. a merry-making.]

>  <b>Markham</b> The women will sit on a heap of potatoes or log, and do what is called "Roody Doody" [ruriruri]. [Footnote. The word is defined in Williams as a song; generally of an amorous nature, accompanied by gestures]. <i>(1834,New Zealand (Heaphy),50,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The [Maori] will sit on a heap of Potatoes or log, and do what is called a Roody Doody. [a kind of gossiping, impromptu recitative, accompanied by rhythmic dancing etc, of all the happenings around them - scandals about the pas, obscene or lewd observations to discomfort the spectators etc. They will often keep it up for hours - on board ships, with traders or sailors. - and work up sexual passion] <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>)

>  <b>JOHN BISCOE</b> ... they had only seen a few seal, it was thought these were only stragglers from some Rookery near at hand; ... <i>(Nov 22, 1831,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1831 Nov 22 Capt. John Biscoe Journal in 1982 Richards Whaling and Sealing 40 Also in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 418]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> This rugged part of the coast was formerly thickly inhabited by seals; and several spots, once their favourite resorts, called rookeries by the sealers, were pointed out by some of the crew ... <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,144</i>)

Briefing papers for Incoming Minister of Social Welfare, 1987. Wellington: Dept of Social Welfare.]

### rooster
 
 <i>n.</i> a male fowl; the main word in NZ (not cock)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> fellow, chap, bloke [OED back to 1785, 1949 April McKain, Fiords Expedition, A.L. Poole 1952 Aug 22 R.Fraser, Internal Affairs Wildlife Branch (R.Mason).]

>  [see quot. spruiker] <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in Italy,41</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> `Rooster' is another variant of `joker' [in high country slang]. <i>(1952,Land Uplifted High,162,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> The note invited me to see if I could make head or tail of this rooster. As far as Jim had been able to make out, his name was `Bloodywell Clarry' <i>(1960,Good Keen Man, A,43</i>)

>  GOLD is easy enough to find on the West Coast. Those in the know can stomp through the bush towards a creek worked by gold miners last century and virtually within minutes pan out a few flecks of the precious metal. Any rooster can go out and do that. <i>(Sep 20, 1987,Dominion Sunday Times,17</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> rooster a person, a genial usage often qualified by `old' or `odd'; eg Gordon Slatter's A Gun in My Hand: `He's not a bad sorta rooster.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,93</i>)

>  Talk of the pugnacity of the Gallic cock! Why that game rooster can't strut beyond his dunghill, clip his wings, and cock-a-doodle-doo, without very whelp of bluff old bully feeling bound in honour to show his teeth and bow-wow. <i>(Aug 20, 1859,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  He then turned and shouted to the group "Who is the rooster on the stand.[sic]" <i>(Dec 23, 1920,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  "And we say to this rooster, 'Right!' we say. 'What's the story about this debentured stock...?" <i>(Jan 26, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  <b>McGEE</b> Well, your average rooster just wants to know where his next square meal's coming from. <i>(1981,Foreskin's Lament,42</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> one who will not get up and dance (i.e. roosts in one place all evening, a once common occurrence in NZ dance halls).

>  <b>SMITH</b> The mayor in a happy mood / Told a funny tale or two. / When you want to grip the roosters / That's the best thing you can do. [F.N.] `Roosters' - those on the high seats near the door [of a dance or social hall] <i>(1946,Poetical Works,273</i>) [NOTES: [Greymouth]]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  `Blatant megaphones' and `roosters' who have been `got at' are the terms used to describe `highly-paid' officials of the Labour Federation. <i>(Jul 7, 1911,Maoriland Worker,18/9/2</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> In Canterbury [in the 1880's], men looking for harvest jobs perched along fences in country townships, and were called `roosters'. <i>(1980,Southern Gentry,121</i>)

### rooster's blood
 
 <i>n.</i> [WW2, Italy] (See [cf. purple death](../P#purple-death))

### rope
 
 <i>n.</i> specifically, the wire rope used to break logs out of the bush (cf ROPEY [ca 1950 R Gilberd Okaihau Letter A log can be hauled forward on `the rope', backwards on the `tail-rope' and the direction of pull can be varied by the use of a snatch block temporarily attached to a tree on one side or other of the log-way]

>  The shoe is connected with a steel wire rope about ?in in diameter, which is wound round the drum of the engine. A black rope on the drum, or the horse which usually accompanies the shoeman, takes the shoe into the place where the logs are. <i>(Sep 24, 1902,Otago Witness,72</i>)

>  Manufacturers of every description of WIRE ROPES. Round Ropes for inclined planes, Collieries, Mines, Railways, Ploughing, etc. <i>(Jan 20, 1890,Inangahua Times,3</i>)

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> Early haulers were built with one cable drum with a typical capacity of 400 metres (20 chains) of 25 mm (1") diameter rope - it was usually called rope even thought it was actually a wire cable. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,19</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to secure (an animal, usually a cow) with a rope (cf. leg-rope) [v (farming)]

>  After I had examined and roped up the heifer he came into the yard, and I said, "Father, this appears to be one of your heifers, here is a T on it and the top part of an S." <i>(Sep 21, 1861,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> Alfred roped up and milked Lilly. <i>(1854,Diary,4</i>)

>  .. or when after the second morning of roping up a heifer, he tries to do so alone, and the beast, after standing for five minutes almost within the bail, wheels round suddenly and drags a half-dry muddy rope, burning like a red-hot iron, through his fingers ... <i>(Mar 13, 1883,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  So the little brat of a beast was roped up, a sadde put on his back and a horse's headstall bridle put on his head, - the two making nearly a complete suit of clothing for it - and the pony was immediately "backed" and taken out for duty as a stock-horse. <i>(Nov 4, 1895,Timaru Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (See [cf. roping stick](../R#roping-stick))

>  MacCluchey came ... and branded etc. a lot of young horses. It is very hard work ropeing them. <i>(Nov 17, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,66,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> Harold with a long pole, had been roping'- that is, throwing the slip-knot over the heads of the cattle that required to be branded. .... He had successfully roped an animal. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,189</i>)

>  ~ v.t. to lasso.-ro'pable a. of cattle, wild and intractable. Generally, illtempered and intractable.-ro'ping pole n. a pole with a noose of rope at its end used for roping individual cattle in a mob. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,10</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a part of the neck or edge wool of a fleece.

>  If they've taken off too much wool with the necks, they are just as good as the fleece, but we have to give the 'ropes' a bit of special treatment. At the scour, someone had gone through the result of this sort of skirting a second time, separating the fleece from the 'ropes' which to anyone who has handled wool is a very descriptive term. <i>(Feb 13, 1963,Weekly News,39</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### rope swinging
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Kiwis have this thing about vertical rubber experiences. They can't wait to leap from bridges with an elastic band on their feet, fling themselves from aircraft with pros strapped to their back like copulating dragonflies, jump into underground rivers with rubber rings around their middles, "rap jump" - abseil down the outer walls of high-rise buildings - and "zorb", roll down hills inside transparent rubber spheres... Even tiny Takaka not only has bungee jumping but serious "swinging", the latest in fear fads involving a 60 mph, 50 yd flight over an abyss. <i>(May 11, 1997,Sunday Times (UK),6.5</i>)

>  The man was rope swinging, an adventure pastime similar to bungy jumping, when he fell from the Mangaweka Bridge. <i>(Dec 27, 2000,Evening Post,2</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> (informal)

### ropeable
 
 <i>adj.</i> violently angry, needing restraint, fit to be tied spg also ropable [*OED online: Austral & NZ Slang. *ca1945 Victoria Univ. College undergraduates; cf also punning corruption rapeable (of a girl).]

>  The mother, now "ropable" about the initial DNA results, admits that the teenager "more or less had no one." <i>(Jul 17, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  In fairness to the chief officer, he was pretty ropable. <i>(Oct 30, 1963,Dominion,12</i>)

>  Now Levin-based Swazi - which employs 70 people - is set to lose 93% of a $2 million Defence Force contract to cheaper China, and hard-talking Hughes is ropeable. <i>(2007,NZ Truth</i>)

>  [He]..suggested the father had been "ropable" to find the trio inside, but the father said that he was merely annoyed. <i>(Nov 30, 2005,Dominion Post,A6</i>)

>  "I'm ropable," said Graham... "There were 200 people here to vote for this motion and I'm furious." <i>(Apr 9, 1993,Salient,5</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Ropeable, adj. (1) Of cattle; so wild and intractable as to be capable of subjection only by being roped. (2) By transference: intractable, angry, out of temper. [1891] <i>(1898,Austral English,394</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Well, I must get back to the G1. He's absolutely ropable about the tanks. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,315</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Well, I must get back to the G1. He's absolutely ropable about the tanks. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,315</i>)

>  <i>(May 16, 1958,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21/3</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Silly old fool. I bet his old woman's just about ropable by now. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,206</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> In a wild rage. .in 4;`Rill's ropeable, someone left the gate open and there's a large variety of fowls in his garden. He's using some shocking language.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS],923</i>)

>  ropeable adj. of cattle, wild and intractable, able to be controlled only by roping; by transference angry, out of temper. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The word ropeable `angry' literally means `needing to be tied up' and is a cattle term that ha become widely current. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,148</i>)

>  The worker in the storehouse was `hot' ... The young bloke [his son] had completed [a security questionnaire] - but the father was ropable. <i>(Nov 2, 1966,People's Voice,3</i>)

>  ropeable, ropable adj. of cattle, wild and intractable, able to be controlled only by roping; by transference, angry, out of temper. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),17</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Oh, Dick. What a ropeable young idiot I've been. We should never have got maried like that.' <i>(1976,Pacific Star,131</i>)

>  ropable or ropeable ... 2. Austral. & N.Z. inf. a. angry. b. wild or intractable: a ropable beast. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1002</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 663; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 423]]

>  Hastings cowboy Brian Denton is ropeable. He is angered that the word cowboys is being used as a label for wild, reckless behaviour. Last week our ... headline was `Cowboy Truckies Licensed To Kill'. The story highlighted the frivolous approach of some truckies to the handling of dangerous goods. But Mr Denton ... reckons using the word cowboy the same way as slap-happy is to ride rough-shod over the reputation of [rodeo] cowboys. <i>(Apr 17, 1988,Sunday News,15</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> ropeable very angry; from need to rope up a difficult beast; eg `Their coach after the game was ropeable!' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,94</i>)

>  Ropei.a-ble (r&omacr.pi.&adot.-b'l), a. [See ROPE, v. t., 4. in the Dictionary.] Needing to be lassoed and thus brought under control; unruly; - siad of a horse or bullock. Hence, in a general sense, angry and excited; savage; - used also of persons. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)

>  Mr. R. M'Kenzie was in a particularly "ropable" mood last night, and all the efforts of all the members could not restrain the exuberance which prompted him to move motions to report progress at most inconvenient moments, to the chagrin of Ministers and members who wanted to get on. <i>(Sep 20, 1895,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  That the idea of the people being roped off at the reception, lest they should get within coo-ee of Royalty and the highly select, is making Wellington ropeable. <i>(Jun 15, 1901,New Zealand Free Lance,22</i>)

>  Your wife was not in the house at the time. You were ropeable. <i>(Mar 8, 1882,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  The Liberal executive was ropable - to them, it was another instance of the Alliance using the Liberals as a convenience. <i>(Sep 26, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Paraparaumu woman Lois McInnes said today she was "ropeable" after the Court of Appeal tossed out her challenge to the legality of the new photographic driver's licence. <i>(Apr 11, 2001,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  The following day the Minister became very angry, almost ropeable in fact, when he read a condensed account of the incident in the Hamilton paper. <i>(Feb 16, 1924,NZ Truth,12</i>)

>  When he came round he was ropable. <i>(Sep 13, 1913,NZ Observer,16</i>)

>  Dr Smith said he was "ropable" the Government intended to take historical land records from Nelson, Marlborough and the West Coast and store them in Christchurch. <i>(Sep 12, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  "You saw what they did? I was bloody ropable! I want their heads on a stake, the two of 'em!. <i>(Sep 30, 1999,Evening Post,18</i>)

### ropeboy
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Two youths who had come from England under engagement made some four months previously were started on that date - one as a rope-boy and the other as a trucker. <i>(Dec 6, 1913,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  An Italian has trucked in a West Coast mine for twenty years; he is still alone ... ropeboys just starting can feel cocky pride in shouting: `Good day, you --- rotten Skypoo bastard!' <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,6(3): 206</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers') Also Sep. p. 23]

### ropehead
 
 <i>n.</i> a hippie or dope-associated person [Heard on National Radio Spectrum programme (Deborah Nation), used of neo-hippies in Nelson]

>  He asked Mr Goff "whether he has received reports on which parties in this Parliament support the view generally enunciated by Mr Alexander, and which parties are out there supporting the ropeheads?" <i>(Oct 7, 2004,Dominion Post,A2</i>)

### ropeman
 
 <i>n.</i> [(loggers')]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> [I] had taken one of the first of Westland's mechanical log-haulers out to Bourkes Creek mill, ... Alf acting as ropeman. <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,70</i>)

>  WANTED. Hauler Driver, 12s; and Assistant Ropeman, 11s. Apply at once, to Seed Bros., Ltd., Mangaroa. <i>(Apr 30, 1914,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  It turns out (telegraphs our Dunedin correspondent) that the diver Hughes, who so pluckily rescued the Italian miner Varischetti in a West Australian mine on the 28th of last month, was at one time employed as a ropeman in the Roslyn Tramway Company's service. <i>(Apr 16, 1907,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Mr F. P. Welsh, labour agent of Masterton, requires a ropeman for log-hauler, good wages will be given to a thoroughly competent man; also married couple for station. <i>(Jul 22, 1904,Bush Advocate,2</i>)

### ropey
 
 <i>n.</i> a worker who manipulated a log-hauling rope [(bushmen's)] (See [rope n](../R#rope-n))

>  <b>GEORGE</b> Among the other employees there was.. the 'ropey' whose job it was to fasten a heavy steel rope to the log... <i>(1990,Ohakune,95</i>)

>  <b>REED</b> Immediately a log was drawn to the whim, the pole was released from the niche, whereupon the `ropey' pulled back the rope and hitched on another log. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,195</i>)

>  <b>MARRIOTT</b> ... a steam log hauler he would put up with, as long as the ropies would hook on to the tree he had felled and get it out of the way, ... <i>(1981,Life in the Gorge,21</i>)

>  <b>R. GILBERD</b> The breaker-out is assisted by a `ropey' whose duty is chiefly to carry the snatch-block. <i>(1951,Letter</i>) [NOTES: R. Gilberd Okaihau Letter]

>  <b>MAHONEY</b> This stopping, rope changing, and starting during a haul required the attentions of a ropey. He walked along within sight of the log, and pulled on the whistle cord at the necessary time. <i>(1998,Era of the Bush Trams in NZ, The,19</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> shy of, tetchy on the rope (See [cf. toey =stroppy](../T#toey-=stroppy))

>  <b>MUIR</b> ... it was hell's own job rounding the bastards up, I can tell you. God, she was a ropey outfit, that bloody dump. <i>(1960,Word for Word,253</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stockman's or shepherd's saddle with a rope attached to the horn with which to lead or carry stock.

### ropu
 
 <i>n.</i> a (Maori) group, company, society, or association [ORIGIN: Maori, also roopu] (See [also roopu](../A#also-roopu))

### rorerore
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BURTON</b> For preparing small meals in a fowlers' camp or fishermen's bivouac, the open hearth was used and the fish or birds might be grilled on sticks leaning over the glowing embers. Grilling was known as tunutunu or rorerore. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,1</i>)

### rori
 
 <i>n.</i> a shellfish, <i>Scutus antipodes</i> Fam. Fissurellidae [Name in Oct. 1992 was <i>Scutus breviculus</i>]

>  <b>HULME & MORRISON</b> When I lift the lid, the rori is undulating beautifully round and round in the steaming water, singing to itself. <i>(1989,Homeplaces,8</i>)

>  There is a proverbial comparison for a woman's lips when well tattoed; such are said to resemble a rori (Paramphorus australis); the plump black smooth and glossy mantle of this shell-fish appearing, when living, its whole length on both sides from under its narrow back shell, and turning up and enveloping its sides, no doubt originated the proverb. <i>(1881,TrNZI,13 : 71</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> How, cowed and servile, gnawing hunger makes the soul-less frame to stagger, when at dim eventide the reeling form oft seeks to eat the refuse rori, cooked and left by Pare-korau! <i>(1922,Maori Life in Ao-Tea,298</i>)

### rorio
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  And then, evoking the spiritual world, there was Richard Nunns with his extraordinary arrray of authentic, rare Maori wind instruments: a whalebone bullroarer, an albatross bone flute, a nose gourd and the rorio (mouth harp). <i>(Oct 31, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

### roro
 
 <i>n.</i> verandah

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> <i>(1963,Teacher,207</i>)

### rort
 
 <i>v.</i> to have sexual intercourse with (= root) [ca1935 Havelock schoolboys ca1949 Football Song : My Old Man's a Fullback etc My old man's a blacksmith, a blacksmith [etc] ... All day he wroughts iron, wroughts iron, [etc] At night he comes home and rorts me.]



 
 <i>n.</i> an act or bout of sexual intercourse; a woman regarded as a sexual object (= root) [ca1935 Havelock schoolboys]

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> Ian thought of Valerie Kemp. By God, what a rort! <i>(1976,Send Somebody Nice,127</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> act of sharp practice, swindle

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> WROUGHT (n) and (v) Trick, conspire, swindle. The wroughter, in British underworld parlance from about 1860, was he who manipulated the cards in the three-card trick. cf P: rorty adj. 1. fine 2. low: amorous: ca1893. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,256</i>)

>  (heading) No move yet on tax credit rort <i>(Aug 19, 2009,Press, The</i>)

>  Members of Parliament love a rort. <i>(Aug 3, 2002,Dominion Post,A14</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> something superior or excellent of its kind [(schoolchildren's)]

 <i>n.</i> [2 entries, rose anise]

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Angelica rosaefolia</i> Hook. Common name: <i>Rose-leaved anise<i> Maori names: <i>Koheriki, kohepiro</i> ... This coastal herb is found in the northern part of New Zealand. <i>(1981,NZ Mail,25</i>)

### Rose-Noelle
 
 <i>n.</i> a trimaran which capsized in 1989 leaving the four occupants to survive 119 days drifting at sea on the upturned hull before washing ashore on Great Barrier Island.

>  On September 30 four men were washed ashore on Great Barrier Island long after most had given them up for dead. They had spent 199 days living in their upturned trimaran, the 12.5-metre <i>Rose Noelle</i>. <i>(Nov 20, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>) [NOTES: check whether original says 199 or 119.]

>  John Glennie's inexperienced crew on the <i>Rose-Noelle</i> were to say later that they believed rescue was just over that horizon every single day of their 119-day drift on the upturned hulls. <i>(Aug 20, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),109</i>)

>  The story of the four men who survived four months in the upturned hull of the Rose-Noelle is one of endurance on an epic scale. <i>(Oct 11, 2000,Dominion,51</i>)

>  Four men found at the scene of a break-in on Great Barrier Island claim to be from the yacht <i>Rose-Noelle</i>, lost at sea four months earlier... The <i>Rose-Noelle</i> saga isn't the longest example of survival at sea - three others equal or surpass the trimaran's drift. <i>(Jul 16, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

### rosella
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep which has cast or lost most of its wool [(shearers')]

>  A sheep that has cast most of his wool before shearing, so is very easy to shear. R. is a shearers' word, probably brought from Australia. [1898 Morris Austral-English] gives two meanings for the word but not this one: (1) a sort of parrot; (2) a European who works bare to the waist (North Australian slang). Our word is probably derived from (2). ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Oct 28, 1933,Press, The,17</i>) [NOTES: Acland Grossary]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The `rosellas'; or easily shorn sheep ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,112</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ROSELLA: A sheep which has lost the wool off its belly, i.e., torn off in matagouri or scrub. A sheep which has lost most of its wool is known as a `rosella.' ] <i>(1949,High Country Days,193</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Rosella. 2. In Australia and New Zealand, since ca. 1910, it has been sheep-shearing s. for `a sheep bare of wool on the `points' (hocks, head, foreleg, etc.) and consequently very easy to shear,' Niall Alexander, communucation of Oct. 22, 1939. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1156</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> My catching pen happened to be filled just on smoko time. In it I noticed right near the front, one rosella (sheep bare of wool except for a few tufts around the neck, and several others that appeared to be bare-bellies. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,257</i>)

>  bare belly: A sheep with defective wool growth caused by a break in the fibre structure. This causes the wool to fall off the belly and legs and is more common in ewes. These sheep are sometimes called `Rosellas'. <i>Ibid.</i> 3/27 (Wallace Shearing Glossary) rosella: A sheep with wool falling out of it. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/21</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> We'd a bunch of Aussie shearers, and they come from New South Wales,/ They entertained the `rouseys' with a lot of fairy tales/ Of how they shore the jumbucks and rosellas from the hills,/ <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,49</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1910 Goldstone `The Man From Kaiveroo' in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 49]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep that has no wool around the neck area or a sheep that is particularly easy to shear, with wool that sloughs off.

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> When an old hand and a young one were sharing a catching pen, the former would let the learner in for the 'cobbler' or hard shearing sheep, at the end of every pen, and when the pen had been refilled, quickly catch and shear all the 'rosellas' or easily shorn sheep, before the unfortunate novice had finished his cobbler. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,129</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> I let him get his third sheep on the board, then grabbed the rosella, hacked the few tufts of wool off and dumped her out of the porthole. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,258</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> rosella: a sheep that has cast most of its wool before shearing. The shearer will usually leave it until last so that he can use it for timing his catch. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,67</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> <i>Notothlaspi rosulatum</i>

### rosin
 
 <i>n.</i> gum, especially kauri gum [In Taranaki in the 1930s, resin was always pronounced "rosin" (DEH)]

>  <b>ELDER</b> I should wish you to bring as much hemp as you possibly can, and such spars and timber as you may, with Mr. Hall, judge valuable. Pork, if it is to be obtained, and salt fish, rosin, or any other natural productions. <i>(1934,Marsden's Lieutenants,55,1934 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Marsden in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 55]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> New Zealand must be always considered as the great emporium of the South Seas, from its local situation, its safe harbours, its navigable rivers, its fine timber for ship building, its rosin, native flax, etc., etc., specimens of which I intend sending to the Society by this conveyance. <i>Ibid.</i> 210 There is here and there the root of a pine which has been burnt into the surface of the ground, and pieces of rosin, which have come from the pine tree, are lying on the ground in all directions. <i>(Sep 22, 1814,Letters & Journals,133,1932 ed.</i>)

### Ross Safety Belt
 
 <i>n.</i> a life-saving device

>  Any kind of entanglement or snag of the line could drag the swimmer under. It wasn't until 1948 that the Ross Safety Belt - a harness held together with a single pin - was designed by a New Zealander. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/38</i>)

### Ross Sea Region
 
 <i>n.</i> the region previously known as the Ross Dependency

>  The Ross Sea Region used to be known as the Ross Dependency, and is New Zealand's slice of the Antarctic pie... The report indentifies two key pressures on the Ross Sea Region environment. <i>(Mar 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 50/14</i>)

### rot
 
 <i>v.</i> disparage

>  He states a soldier was "rotting" a rifle, saying that it would not fire true, and was a "crookie". <i>(Nov 11, 1915,Feilding Star,2</i>)

### rot off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheep feet] to be afflicted with advanced foot-rot.

>  <b>WALLACE</b> In the plain language of the country people, the feet of animals suffering from the animals suffering from the effects of the ergot-poison are said to 'rot off.' The description, though rather brusque, is pretty nearly correct. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,307</i>)

### rotaflow system
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I had long heard of the herring-bone milking shed, but the rotaflow system.. was a new development... The Friesan cows walk into individual bails on the slowly revolving platform; the milking cups go on; each cow does a circuit; the cups are automatically removed; and then the cows receive a light spray of water which serves to nudge them out of the bail towards the yard. <i>(May 8, 1993,Dominion,17</i>)

### rotary
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rotary design of cowshed, where milkers are located in its centre. [ORIGIN: ellipsis] (See [also rotary shed](../A#also-rotary-shed))

>  A rotary would probably cost from $2500 to $3000 more than a herringbone. <i>(Jul 1971,NZ Dairy Exporter,9</i>)

>  Jobs Currently it is a semi self-contained unit milking 940 cows for 340 000 thru a new 54 bale [sic] rotary. The main farm of 260 ha will milk some 850 cows thru the 1 year old rotary. <i>(Jan 2002,Fencepost.com,4</i>)

>  Wing Point Farm is a new conversion milking 800 dairy cows through a 70 bale rotary. <i>(Apr 3, 2002,Dominion,18</i>)

>  He has sited the new shed on the same site as his previous rotary which now sits close by in the bull paddock. 'The bulls enjoy playing with it,' says Brian with a laugh. [rotary cowshed] <i>(Oct 31, 2002,Opunake and Coastal News,14</i>)

### rotary clothesline
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SLATTER</b> The high paling fences behind the house and the patch of lawn under the rotary clothesline ... <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,103</i>)

### rotary herringbone cowshed
 
 <i>n.</i> a shed similar to rotary turnstyle except that the milker is in the centre and the cows face outwards.

>  Rotary Herringbone cowshed. Similar to rotary turnstyle except that the milker is in the centre and the cows face outwards. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,39</i>)

### rotary shed
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also rotary](../A#also-rotary))

>  A lot has been written about the various types of rotary shed. <i>(Feb 1972,NZ Dairy Exporter,2</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> [Caption] During the 1970s the acceptance of intensive dairying methods.. resulted in the development of further ' production line' techniques such as this rotary milking shed. <i>(1983,Illustrated History of Taranaki,150</i>)

>  In his new rotary shed, the cows step onto a slowly turning platform and have the cups attached ... When the cows have completed the circuit the cups are removed automatically, enabling Karl to milk 600 cows singlehandedly in an hour. <i>Ibid.</i> 38 I have just watched the afternoon milking of 350 cows. It took about an hour in a rotary shed that has more in common with the Sydney Opera House than with John Scott's old herringbone shed... One at a time, the cows boarded a huge, slow merry-go-round capable of holding 66 animals and stood side by side like slides in a carousel, heads facing the centre. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/32</i>)

### rotary turnstyle cowshed
 
 <i>n.</i> a shed in which cows are milked on a rotating platform with the milker on the outside and the cows facing the centre.

>  Rotary turnstyle cowshed. Cows are milked on a rotating platform with the milker on the outside and the cows facing the centre. The cows step on to the platform and reverse off. Cups are put on from behind. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,39</i>)

>  When he played rugby, the drinking was "colossal". "We'd be at the pub and it would be like going around and around in a rotary cowshed.." <i>(Nov 14, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C5</i>)

### rotate
 
 <i>v.</i> (See [wouldn't it rotate you](../W#wouldn't-it-rotate-you))

>  Wouldn't it rotate you! Have to waste another half mug of water to get clean again. <i>(Dec 21, 1942,NZEF Times,II/78 : 17</i>)

>  I am rotated more than somewhat. <i>(Dec 21, 1942,NZEF Times,II/78 : 19</i>)

### rotation length
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the length of time and rate of movement of stock around a farm.

>  <b>RENNIE</b> The length of term the herd of cows spend in each paddock is called the rotation length. In summer/autumn the farmer may again 'lengthen' the rotation by grazing cows in the same paddock for two milkings. <i>(1988,Agriculture in NZ,32</i>)

>  <b>WHITE ET AL</b> The definition of rotation length is often misunderstood. The common definition relates to the proportion of the farm being grazed in one day. Rotation length describes the current rate of movement around the farm. <i>(1999,NZ Pasture and Crop Science,162</i>)

>  Remember that the farm area is fixed. It is critical not to reduce rotation length if you run out of feed. <i>(Jul 26, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,16</i>)

### rotational cross
 
 <i>n.</i> a crossbred produced after a period of crossing with a series of breeds used in a set rotation [(farming)]

>  Crossbred produced after a period of crossing with a series of breeds used in a set rotation. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,15</i>)

### rotational grazing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the moving of a constant number of stock from paddock to paddock, in order to control feed.

>  The more effective utilization of pastures is also facilitated by so-called `rotational grazing,' milking cows being followed in a field by dry and young stock or ewes. <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,28</i>)

>  The system of rotational grazing practised on so many dairy farms, should be even more popular than it is.. <i>(Feb 20, 1930,NZ Dairy Exporter,1</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Rotational grazing on hill country pasture has a detrimental effect on lamb weight, lambing percentage, wool weight and general thrift. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,61</i>)

>  <b>MACMILLAN</b> If the farm is well sub-divided you can stock up to several hundred animals per hectare and hold them on to the paddocks for only brief periods. This pattern of feeding stock is called rotational grazing or mob-stocking. <i>(1980,Land Development in the Chatham Islands,17</i>)

>  <b>BARNES</b> Rotational grazing was not much used by dairy farmers in the 1940s; the length of fencing seemed to many to make the idea uneconomic. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),99</i>)

### rotationally graze
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to move stock into several different grazing locations throughout the year.

>  To maintain good growth during this period, calves should be rotationally grazed from three weeks of age, so that a fresh grazing area is provided daily. <i>(Sep 1961,NZ Journal of Agriculture,231</i>)

>  Ewes and lamb are set-stocked from lambing until weaning. Ewe lambs are then rotationally grazed until mid-May, when they go on to kale. <i>(Jan 1965,NZ Journal of Agriculture,45</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> We mob-stocked and rotationally grazed, so disease spread. <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,160</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [rata](../R#rata))

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a baling machine that produces round bales.
