### Rotoehu Ash
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  The first day commenced near Matawai where a 6 m thick sequence (from Rotoehu Ash 50 kyr to Taupo Pumice 1800 yrs B.P.) is preserved in a road cutting on SH2 a few kilometers east of Matawai. ... At the top of Panikau Hill a thin but relative [sic] complete distal section is preserved and contains Rotoehu Ash through to Taupo Pumice. <i>(Jul 1984,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,65/17</i>)

>  Stratigraphic position: mud overlying Rotoehu Ash. <i>(May 1976,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,41/22</i>)

### rotoflow
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rotary design of cowshed.

>  Another shed is being built. A 50-bale rotor-flow rotary. <i>(Aug 8, 1996,NZ Farmer,20</i>)

>  Consistent fertiliser history, 36 bale rotoflow cowshed. <i>(Jun 23, 2001,Dominion,39</i>)

>  First farms with improvements like this are hard to find. 24 bale rotoflow cowshed, excellent racing and farm buildings. [rotor-flow] <i>(Oct 17, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,19</i>)

### Rotoiti Breccia
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  Howarth, R. Relative and absolute age of the Rotoiti Breccia formation. <i>(Nov 1976,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,42/26</i>)

### Rotokawa  Ash
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  Above the latter is a form of diatomaceous silt at the base of which is a thin layer Rotokawa Ash, undated but thought to be c.4000 years B.P. <i>(May 1975,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,39/35-6</i>)

### Rotokawau Ash
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  On field appearance and stratigraphy, the thin tephra was considered not to be an Okataina tephra [sic, small 't'] (Kaiharoa or Whakatane Tephra) but perhaps a tephra from Taupo or the basaltic Rotokawau Ash. <i>(Oct 1984,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,66/18</i>) [NOTES: Is this the same as Rotokawa Ash???]

### Rotomahana Mud
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  The upper part of the section comprises Taupo Pumice, Kaharoa Ash and Rotomahana Mud intercalated with beds of peat. <i>(May 1975,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,39/36</i>)

### Rotorainer
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [from trade name] a rotary spray irrigation plant.

>  They have decided on a low pressure Roto-rainer. <i>(Jul 14, 1983,NZ Farmer,31</i>)

>  The Roto-rainer itself has taken some getting used to. <i>(Aug 23, 1984,NZ Farmer,28</i>)

>  Irrigation planning and management experience (Rotar rainers [sic] and Gun.) <i>(Nov 18, 2000,Press, The,43</i>)

>  This property is one of the best of its type. The total area is spray irrigated by two Rotorainers. <i>(Nov 25, 2000,Press, The,64</i>)

### Rotorua Ash
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  The base of the section comprises ridges of pumice sand gravel mantled by Okareka Ash, Rerewhakaaitu Ash and Rotorua Ash now truncated by lake waters when the lake level was at an elevation of 284 m as against the present day elevation of 279.7 m. <i>(May 1975,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,39/35</i>)

### Rotorua smell
 
 <i>n.</i> a smell of hydrogen sulphide

>  [Hydrogen sulphide] can be detected as a `Rotorua' smell at one part in two million. <i>(Aug 1955,AJHR,H31A/15</i>) [NOTES: (Report of the Fumes Enquiry, Auckland)]

### Rotorua smelt
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Retropinna lacustris</i>

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Usually smaller [than the two-inch Southland smelt] is the Rotorua smelt, <i>R. lacustris</i>, which occurs naturally in cold as well as thermal lakes. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,209</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM399</b> Rotorua Smelt <i>Retropinna lacustris </i> Stokell <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 399]

### Rotorua tea cakes
 
 <i>n.</i> recipe

>  ROTORUA TEA CAKES flour ... baking powder ... butter to be rubbed in, 1 egg, and enough milk to moisten and drop on shelf. <i>(1908,Souvenir of All Nations Fair,41</i>)

### Rototuna waffles
 
 <i>n.</i> recipe

>  <b>BASHAM</b> ROTOTUNA WAFFLES ... flour ... butter ... milk ... salt ... baking powder ... 3 eggs beaten separately ... Have the irons hot ... cook a few minutes ... serve either hot or cold with golden syrup. <i>(1939,Aunt Daisy's Cookery Book,141</i>)

### Rotovegas
 
 <i>n.</i> Rotorua [ORIGIN: blend, Popularised by Craig Marriner's novel "Stonedogs" but according to Linda Clark and Marriner on Radio NZ Nine to Noon (23/7/02) it has been around and used for some time.]

>  Some predicted there would be a quickie divorce in Roto-vegas and another Alliance before the year was out. <i>(Aug 13, 2002,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  It's appropriate that Forestcorp's operational base is in Rotorua, known to some as Roto Vegas because of its glitzy tour <i>(Feb 11, 1995,Dominion,12</i>)

>  Here, at last, was star quality. Rotovegas star quality. <i>(Sep 11, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  <b>MARRINER</b> A day or so later, your Mr X.. found a need to kill two patched Black Power members and one associate, all hailing from your home town of sunny Roto-Vegas. <i>(2001,Stonedogs,349</i>)

>  Rotovegas News and Times <i>(Nov 9, 2004,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)

>  <i>Stonedogs</i>, by Craig Marriner... It's a real Kiwi story and it's set in Rotovegas. <i>(Feb 4, 2006,Time Out,4</i>)

>  Organising a weekend at Rotovegas, with a hot pool included. <i>(Feb 8, 2006,Viva,5</i>)

>  Summer in Rotovegas and it's hosing down. <i>(Feb 14, 2009,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  We've got Rotovegas. <i>(Jul 14, 2004,TV1</i>)

>  It takes place in an animal kingdom he knows well, the netherworld of drug dealing and gang banging in Roto-Vegas, before heading on a dark road-trip to murder. <i>(Jul 27, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Known as Roto-Vegas for its tourist kitsch appeal, Rotorua is quite a different kettle of trout... <i>(Sep 19, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),40</i>)

>  I'm from Rotorua, which is also known as "Rotovegas", I wasn't aware Dannevirke had the same sort of nickname! <i>(Oct 29, 2001,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: www.geocities.com/dannevirkefire/Feedback.htm]

>  Rotorua's CBD ban: Is banning ratbags from Rotovegas central an infringement of their human rights or a piece of inspired thinking? <i>(Aug 6, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A9</i>)

>  Chiefs take jackpot in Rotovegas spree [headline] That cued a rather confident and well lubricated gentleman sitting in the main stand at last night's venue to stand and roar: "welcome to Rotovegas you Bulls". <i>(Mar 23, 2008,Sunday Star Times,B5</i>)

>  But they have begun to call the place "Roto-vegas" for a reason -- the density of motels and gimcrack places is near the tipping point. <i>(Apr 4, 2004,Washington Post,P.01</i>) [NOTES: Steve Hendrix]

>  <b>Mary Wilson</b> A staff member who has been away on leave for a while came back today and he said Rotovegas was the name of a country music band based in Rotorua that was set up by Sid Hughes in the 1980s. They were playing around with names for the band and came up with Rotovegas. I have not been able to find anything yet to confirm this - unfortunately we have not indexed the Daily Post for the 1980s yet. <i>(May 1, 2003,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: MaryEllen.Wilson@rdc.govt.nz]

### rotten
 
 <i>adj.</i> very drunk, espec. in GET ROTTEN. (cf (GET) STINKING [ca1940 Les Cleveland]

### rotten corn
 
 <i>n.</i> kanga pirau, fermented corn

>  <b>MERDEITH</b> I sampled a new Maori dish on this trip - kanga kopirau (rotten corn). It has a bouquet just about as potent as the dead whale I mentioned in my last. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,146,1935 ed.</i>)

>  Nothing can be more pitiable than the nonsense talked to these poor men and women, taken from their pigstye-like hut, and their diet of rotten corn, and assured that they are the greatest and finest fellows imaginable. <i>(Jan 14, 1864,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Professor Behring .. might try to induce us to follow in the "healthful" exercise of feeding on the rotten corn which our dusky brethren relish so much ... <i>(Apr 20, 1905,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  The stench from the rotten corn near the settlements is something awful. <i>(Jul 29, 1887,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  He had had two wives before and he married them in the Maori style, sans ceremony, sans kai, try s'sark [sic] or rotten corn banquets, and he found the results eminently satisfactory. <i>(Feb 20, 1915,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  Yet this sensitive organisation always appeared to be the more strange when the horribly stinking smells of two of their common articles of food - often, in the olden times, in daily use - are considered: rotten corn (maize, in the cob) long steeped in water to soften it, and dried shark. <i>(Mar 4, 1893,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

### rotten eggs
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> a flower, Eschscholtzia california [also rotten egg plant]

>  Even more of a nuisance to New Zealand farmers are Californian poppies, Escholzia californica, better known in Centra Otago in my childhood as rotten eggs. This was probably for the intense orange of the flowers... In its home in California, great sweeps of egg-yolk gold against the blue leaves are breathtaking. Since the rotten egg has become a darling of the landscape designer, several colours have evolved, including pink, a near brown, and a beautiful soft clotted-cream that comes true from seed year after year. <i>(Jun 4, 1999,Dominion,12</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [schoolboys' game]

>  <b>GEORGE TURNER</b> `A game played by rolling a tennis ball into holes each occupied by a player, who, if the ball gets into his hole, tries to `brand' the roller. Possibly a game peculiar to our school.' <i>(1931,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1931 George Turner Diary (Dannevirke)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> In small schools without proper playing fields, games were spontaneous and enjoyable, and holes could be dug freely in the school grounds for Rotten Eggs, in which a tennis ball is rolled into the nest of one of the players and all run while the boy with the ball has to brand another player (i.e. hit him with the ball). <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,159</i>)

### rotten fish plant
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>rotten fish plant [=] fish-guts plant [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,65</i>)

### rotten rock
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  He simply followed a sheep track across the rugged, steep terrain, some of it like granite, some of it crumbling "rotten rock", debris from which almost buried the crawler. <i>(Mar 2005,NZ Geographic,72/27</i>)

>  The fern and scrub covering the hillside is some 140 feet above the road, and immediately beneath it irregular strata of 'rotten rock' juts out. <i>(Sep 6, 1929,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  About 5 miles of clay road was given a good coating of soft stone which was called Rotten Rock. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Memories,No. 30/13</i>)

### rottie
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a rottweiler dog [ORIGIN: abbreviation]

>  Having a murderous, heaving rottie lunging about in the front yard makes an ugly statement about the humans within. <i>(May 3, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Did ya see Hartie being torn to pieces by those rotties last night? <i>(Dec 5, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A135</i>)

>  Except for the rottie and the baby,.. he seems free of encumbrances of any kind. <i>(Jan 11, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  Who knows what a rottie's tail looks like, or what might have grown out the back of a boxer? <i>(Nov 13, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  Till recent years, you could say there were A-grade biters.. breeds that, if not well-handled, could go to the bad side, such as rotties, german shepherds and dobermanns. <i>(Mar 8, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Plans by TV show-maker Cloud 9 of Avalon for a movie theme park a rottie's bark from Naenae sound surreal <i>(Aug 26, 2000,Evening Post,20</i>)

### rotty
 
 <i>n.</i> as rottie q.v.

>  It's just a small place. It's a bit rundown and looks like it should have a rotty out the back. <i>(Mar 21, 1999,Sunday Star Times,F1</i>)

### rotutu
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The manuka, mahoe, akeake, rotutu, lacebark and flax seedlings will grow to be "gigantic and humungous" says Mitchell Campbell and Jason Ranguia, though the exact names of the plants escape them. <i>(Aug 2000,Elements,No. 5 : 6</i>)

### rough
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish [(fishermen's)]

>  [species name] mixed fish .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] roughs, rubbish fish, felix <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to shear sheep roughly and badly, esp. as ROUGH 'EM [(shearers')]

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> But he wouldn't shear [sheep] ... in to rough them through. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,37</i>) [NOTES: (`The Bloke that Ran Across a Snag')]

>  ROUGH 'EM. The opposite to `pink 'em', and meaning rough shearing and a bad job of the sheep. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,156</i>)

>  <b>MILLS</b> To keep level [with the ringer] the challenger started to `rough them out a bit'. This did [130] not suit the boss who `chipped' him quite a few times ... and then sacked him. <i>(1960,Sheep-O,129</i>)

>  Rough 'm. Rough shearing. Opposite to pink 'em. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,58</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to shear carelessly.

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Rough 'em. The opposite to 'pink 'em' and meaning rough shearing and a bad job of the sheep. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,156</i>)

>  <b>MILLS</b> To keep level the challenger started to 'rough them out a bit.' <i>(1960,Sheep-O,129</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Rough 'em: Rough shearing. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,58</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a land area containing second growth blackberry, bracken or scrub.

>  In the breaking in the land from the rough, Mr Anderson follows a regular system and is gradually covering the whole farm. <i>(Apr 1, 1944,NZ Dairy Exporter,4</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> Now a highly developed property, Putere was taken up in the rough in 1928. <i>(1955,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,33</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Most of the sheep are now coming down into the valleys and getting the young grass have taken the rough off. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,75</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> There are of course still about 2,500 acres still in the rough - bush and scrub ... [in the rough] <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,167</i>)

### rough as (old) boots
 
 <i>phr.</i> [ca1920s WHB Orsman; -->ca1950s Wanganui see rough as houses; rough as old boots (DEH) Taranaki 1930s]

### rough as a bag of files
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  ... but as the engine was still rough as a bag of files, the pilot wared a `washout' and headed for home. <i>(Sep 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,Second Series 3/2,1981 ed.</i>)

### rough as a bag/bags/sacks
 
 <i>phr.</i> [also (as) rough as a pig's breakfast]

>  Idiomatic use of `as' to introduce a simile. ... Two may possibly be Canterbury expressions (at least, I never heard them outside the province): (1) As rough as a bag. (2) As rough as a pig's breakfast. <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BAKER</b> ... rough as a bag (the Australians also have rough as bags), and rough as a pig's breakfast. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> The Australian `rough as bags' has became `rough as sacks'. <i>(1943,English as it is spoken in NZ,91</i>)

>  Smarten `em up. .... Rough as bags. <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in the Middle East,23</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> Rough as a bag or rough as a pig's breakfast. Uncouth: New Zealand and Australian: C. 20. B., 1941 and 1942. Cf rough as .. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1157</i>)

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> ... the kids were rough as bags and the food wasn't very choice. <i>(1950,Otago Interval,50</i>)

>  <b>SYDER & HODGETTS</b> Very rough in manners, and/or appearance. .in 4;`He's unkempt, spits and swears regardless of time and place he's as rough as a bag.' <i>(1974,Australian & NZ English [TS]</i>) [NOTES: (1962-63) 1974 SYDER AND HODGETTS Aust. and NZ English [TS] 15]

>  <b>TURNER</b> There is simile: `as mad as a snake', `as bald as a bandicoot' (an embroidery on the English `bald as a coot'), `as happy as larry', `rough as bags' (which I know better in the variant `rough as sacks'), `as wet as a shag'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The</i>)

>  THERE are twenty-two acceptors in the event, and most of them are, as they say in the classics, rough as sacks. <i>(Oct 7, 1926,NZ Truth,11</i>)

### rough as a pig's breakfast
 
 <i>phr.</i> [see Baker NZ Slang p.53 (also rough as a dog's breakfast: ca1910 W.H.B. Orsman)]

### rough as a soojee bag
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> [Sugar] came in mats, the big ones weighing 180 lbs, and was often called `mat sugar'. This mat was native made, being a pleated plant, and was pretty well square. Outside this mat, and covering it, was a coarse bag called a soojee (or suji?) bag. A familiar saying of the early days was, `As rough as a soojee bag', and this saying was applied to both people and things. Up to 1863 I never saw sugar except in mats ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,97</i>)

### rough as bags
 
 <i>phr.</i> roughly made or done; rough and ready; very untidy [cf. other 'rough as' expressions]

>  <b>HYDE</b> I haven't seen a dance in months, outside of the local hops, and they're as rough as bags. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,144,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He's as rough as bags but his heart is right. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,44</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> There is simile: `as mad as a snake', `as bald as a bandicoot' (an embroidery on the English `bald as a coot'), `as happy as larry', `rough as bags' (which I know better in the variant `rough as sacks'), `as wet as a shag'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,115</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> sc. violent MAORI GIRL: ... I took him on after my girl friend Tui got fed up with him. But he could be rough, rough as bags. Beat me up once, and that was the finish. <i>(1974,Hand on the Rail, The,30</i>)

>  As a consequence one night towards the end of June there appeared at a farm in the Waerenga Valley a youngish man who might best be described as "rough as bags." <i>(Aug 1, 1929,NZ Truth,7</i>)

### rough as guts
 
 <i>phr.</i> (See [cf. rough-as-guts adj](../R#rough-as-guts-adj))

>  Our Sergeant sez we are as `rough as gutz' <i>(Jul 5, 1918,NZEF Chronicles,253</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> ...a pretty typical pighunter's firearm: rough as guts but still capable of delivering the goods. <i>(1982,Wild Pig,10</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `Rough as guts,' said some. But to stay there [at the hut] for a few days brought an affinity with shattered stumps ... <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,134</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> rough as guts crude, inferior, very rough; eg `This car is as rough as guts. Needs servicing, for sure.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,94</i>)

>  Cool. Rough as guts (edited on two VCRs) and mad as a bicycle, Back of the Y stands as their best attempt at the worst possible taste. <i>(Jan 27, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  Our sergeant sez we are as "rough as gutz". <i>(Jul 5, 1918,Chronicles NZEF,IV/47 : 253</i>)

### rough as houses
 
 <i>phr.</i> [(also rough as boots) p.c. I.W.A.Jamieson His father used it c1950s Wanganui; WHBO c1940s]

 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>rough chervil [s] Chaerophyllum temulum</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,65</i>)

### rough comfrey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>rough comfrey [s] Symphytum asperum</i> Lep. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,65</i>)

### rough country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that is difficult to access easily, to muster, manage and graze [ with a low carrying-capacity and often with growth of weeds and scrub.]

>  The Maories [sic] were emboldened by the result of former wars, especially that in the North, which, carried on in a rough country, where natural obstacles crippled the operations of regular troops, was not conducted or concluded in such a way as to impress the Maories with the prowess of our troops ... <i>(Oct 6, 1860,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  The body was brought 40 miles over rough country to town for an inquest. <i>(Apr 22, 1871,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  He took up a run in South Canterbury, but owing to the rough country and rabbits abandoned it. <i>(Dec 7, 1891,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> This is, in fact, a region which is appropriately spoken of, as `rough country' - a region where a horse will plunge without hesitation into a rushing stream, ... <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,54</i>) [NOTES: 1888 Christian Shirres Diary in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 54]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I might here explain that to a musterer, the term rough country does not imply hard shingle country, but rock-bound bluffy country. <i>Ibid.</i> 85 ... he had cut up a bit rough over the affair. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,56</i>)

>  After crossing the valley of the same name, it passes along some rough country, and again reaches the foot of the hills ... <i>(Jan 3, 1852,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,178</i>)

>  The winged bicycle is expected to be of considerable use in providing fast locomotion over bad roads and even across rough country. <i>(Jan 9, 1913,Grey River Argus,8</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> .. many of the men I wanted to reach lived twenty-five or even thirty miles away with very rough country in between. <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,122</i>)

>  In the Auckland district ploughing varies greatly between light and comparatively level lands and rough or bush country. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,293</i>)

>  100 2-tooth ewes from up-river, 40 18-month steers, off rough country. <i>(Jan 31, 1905,Hawera Star,3</i>)

>  The silent heading dog is a necessity on rough country. <i>(Mar 1931,NZ Farmer,231</i>)

>  On this grade there is a considerable area of rough country suitable only for wethers. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,100</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> .. these two erected forty chains over rough country in five days. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,87</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. to a musterer the term rough country does not imply hard shingle country but rockbound bluffy country. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,56</i>)

>  It took sixteen men 22 hours to carry him twelve miles, owing to the rough country. <i>(Jun 26, 1907,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  Five miles of rough country at Lower Hutt gave the Brooklyn runners an opportunity of tasting the type of stuff they are likely to encounter in the provincial championships. <i>(Jul 25, 1929,NZ Truth,17</i>)

>  Later a number of carloads of searchers arrived from Orepuki and Tuatapere and assisted in searching the farm, which is very rough country. <i>(Oct 13, 1931,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

 <i>n.</i>
