### rough feed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) stock feed containing rank growth, fern and weeds.

>  There were 137 acres under crop and 150 acres of rough feed surface-sown. <i>(Oct 13, 1866,Weekly News,5</i>)

>  When the farm was taken over ten years ago, the 200 acres in grass supported browntop and rough feed. <i>(May 1, 1945,NZ Dairy Exporter,14</i>)

>  <b>CHINN</b> I decided to use bullocks instead of horses to pull the vehicles conveying the flax. Rough feed was available in the forest and river-bed for them, so I had no problem in that direction. <i>(1963,Packtrack to Highway,107</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> They [wild Hokonui sheep] prefer rough feed and will eat heads and thistles before they touch the clover. [rough-fed] <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,101</i>)

### rough gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> widespread in the Marlborough region this gecko has enlarged scales over the upper surfaces of its body, unusual in a New Zealand lizard. [<i>Naultinus rudis</i> aka rough scaled gecko, rough-scaled gecko. The other NZ lizard with pronounced scaliness is the Marlborough green, or manuka, gecko.]

>  Despite such constraints, a boldly patterned rough-scaled gecko appears almost invisible among manuka blossom (top left), even the textured scales assisting in the deception. <i>(Sep 2010,NZ Geographic,50</i>)

>  The Rough Scaled or Rough Gecko is confined to the Kaikoura Ranges, on the east coast of New Zealand's South Island. They generally live in manuka and kanuka, known as tea tree and in forests also. Most active by day. Parts of the gecko are covered in rough scales giving rise to its name. <i>(2009,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: Date is an estimate http://www.nhc.net.nz/index/lizards-new-zealand/rough-gecko/rough-gecko.htm]

>  Ormandy, 20, an installation installer, and Stirton, 30, a dancer, had been jointly charged after the geckos -- two Northland green geckos and a rough gecko -- were found at their home in the Christchurch suburb of Richmond three weeks ago. <i>(May 3, 2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzherald.co.nz/richmond-limited/news/article.cfm?o_id=176&objectid=10380100]

>  Widlife experts feared the two Northland Green Geckos and one Rough Gecko were destined for the international black market. <i>(Apr 12, 2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzherald.co.nz/nz-customs-service/news/article.cfm?o_id=360&objectid=10377174]

### rough it
 
 <i>v.</i> and vbl n ROUGHING IT) `live a rough life; live on rough fare or in rough quarters' is very frequent from 1838 [get quot]. It is often italicised or used within quotation marks, indicating that many early writers considered it `colonial' usage

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Genteel settlers .... unused, as the expressive colonial phrase goes, to `rough it'. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,313</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... so long as he were willing to work, and `rough it' for the first year or so, ... <i>(1861,New Zealand (various authors/titles),257</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... we are supposed to be `roughing it'; ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,89</i>) [NOTES: 1890 Mar 3 in 1894 Wilson Land of the Tui 89]

>  [He] found there was a culture of "roughing it" in Dunedin, whereby students expected some level of discomfort, and just "got on with it". <i>(Nov 11, 2003,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

### rough leatherjacket
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] leatherjacket, rough 2333-40 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### rough on rats
 
 <i>phr.</i> ? as a catchphrase, hard on rats (? with an implied pun on a well-known brand of rat-poison (`Rough-on-Rats') if it existed then [AND 1888 (get this cit: ?literal) = bad luck: HWO- but is the NZ sense this or merely transf.] -

>  <b>DUNCAN</b> [The dog] became .... `rough on rats' <i>(1888,Wakatipians,53</i>)

### Rough Riders
 
 <i>n.</i> Boer War volunteers from NZ

>  <b>C.E. & E.L. CHRISTIAN</b> NZ Rough Riders ... <i>(1900,Our Boys</i>)

>  <b>SKEY</b> [Title] To the New Zealand Rough-Riders (Title page and cover: The Rough Riders Contingent for South Africa) <i>(1900,Patriotic Rhymes,1</i>)

>  <b>HAYDON</b> The third contingent, or Rough Riders. <i>(1907,New Zealanders & the Boer War,59</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> It may not be amiss to explain how the name `Rough Riders' came to be applied to the contingents. The Premier .... expressed a strong opinion that we should not accept the title of `Bushmen' which had been adopted in Australian. [because of possible confusion with the Bushmen of South Africa] .... As they were not regularly trained troops, and were to be good riders ready for rough work, he thought they should be called `Rough Riders', and expressed the hope that they would be particularly rough on the enemy. Accordingly the name was adopted, and under it the Third and Fourth Contingents have been raised. <i>(1907,With the Fourth NZ Rough Riders,10</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> The departure of a few lads to the Boer War at the turn of the century - Rough Riders they were called. .... <i>(1949,Short History of Belfast, Canterbury,12</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> When however the Boer War began and Canterbury started recruiting her special contingent of Rough Riders, prices for many commodities, and especially for oats on which the Rough Riders' horses were to be fed - these commodity prices began to rise. <i>(1951,Canterbury Tales,86</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> At the time [1897] Charlie Golding, who later, during the South African war, served with the famous contingent of rough riders was working at [67] the time [sic] for Rhymers. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,66</i>)

>  <b>HARWOOD</b> [Boer War] At the start of the Boer War he enlisted in the 5th Contingent of `Rough Riders' The name `Rough Riders' was given to the New Zealand troops by thte[sic] Premier, the Right Hon. Richard John Seddon. He was of the opinion that they should not be called `Bushmen' as the Australian men were called. He had been told that the Bushmen of Africa were held in contempt and he objected to New Zealand men starting with a name that would suggest anything but the respects he was quite sure they would be entitled to. <i>(1982,Heritage Trail,64</i>)

>  Kimberly was safe on Sunday last, and Mr. Cecil Rhodes commands the Rough Riders there. <i>(Oct 25, 1899,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  [Caption] Auckland Rough Riders for Fourth Contingent; The First Parade at Potter's Paddock. <i>(Feb 16, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 5</i>)

>  The value of such men as our Rough Riders in actual warfare is very amply demonstrated. <i>(Feb 23, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 8</i>)

>  Aucklanders' farewell to their contingent of Rough Riders was an event which thousands of people will be pleased to remember. <i>(Mar 16, 1900,Auckland Weekly News,Suppl. 2</i>)

### rough sheep
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep that has missed a docking, shearing or muster and is often wild. (See [double-fleecer; double-decker; hermit; long tailer](../D#double-fleecer;-double-decker;-hermit;-long-tailer))

>  The tobacco may be put in wire cages or gunny bags, and allowed to boil ten minutes, 400 gallons at once, in malt tanks, the lids being on; but the best plan is to infuse under pressure of steam from a boiler, a pipe from which would heat the dip in the morning and regulate the temperature afterwards, which with newly shorn sheep should be 115 to 118 deg., and in rough sheep 120 to 125 deg. About 1 minutes for shorn and 2 for rough sheep should be effectual. <i>(Jan 23, 1875,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  CHALLENGE. THROUGH some ill-feeling against me at the Oroua Downs, I am willing to shear Mr. Jenkins, in long-wool sheep, for any sum from GBP5 to GBP20; but I do not call it fair shearing for another man to shear all the rough sheep out of his pen. JOSEPH MORRISON. <i>(Dec 3, 1884,Wanganui Herald,3</i>)

>  In all 6,789 sheep, 25 head cattle, and 6 horses were sold as follows: .. 59 rough sheep at 9s 8d, 1117 crossbred lambs to 4s 11d. <i>(Feb 27, 1896,Otago Witness,26</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Rough Sheep: Domesticated sheep which has escaped musterers and has gone semi-wild. Distinct from the real wild sheep of Canterbury, and unlike them do not shed their wool. Consequently they become overburdened with wool, which at times reached such length that it drags on the ground <i>Ibid.</i> Wayleggo 29 ... Catching stragglers and rough sheep. <i>Ibid.</i> 154 These sheep were real wild sheep - not the ordinary domestic sheep gone wild. The latter, long-tailers and woolies which have escaped muster and gone semi-wild, are known as `rough sheep' <i>(1947,Wayleggo,[Gloss].</i>)

>  We finished shearing on the 6th February, and then there were 14,229; and I believe that near 100 rough sheep that were not mustered at shearing, were included in the count at delivery. <i>(Mar 21, 1868,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> Busy making a paddock with James Smith, my cousin, at back of house for rams & rough sheep (said rough sheep consist of stragglers found on run after shearing.) <i>(Jan 12, 1864,Diary,22</i>) [NOTES: in Patterson (1998) Hey Days and Dray Days]

>  <b>SMITH</b> After this I rode to the Native Pah and got a Maori to shear the rough sheep. <i>(Feb 15, 1864,Diary</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> On the Gap's block where the ridge is particularly ragged, there is nearly always a little mob of these rough sheep. Rough sheep are a curse to musterers, and when they are caught, they usually finish up as mutton. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,94</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. longtailers and woollies which have escaped muster and gone semi-wild are known as 'rough' sheep. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,154</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> This country has only just been taken over by the station off Craigieburn and there was [sic] a lot of rough sheep on it. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,39</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Record tallies, rough sheep, tough tucker and crook guts all figure in yarns that are passed on. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,34</i>)

>  In 9 hours Wi Henare shore 354 sheep - ewes and lambs - and in week of 48 hours his record was 1600, and this among rough sheep, it is said. <i>(Jan 27, 1917,Poverty Bay Herald,8</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> They were big rough sheep on the Maimai, fit to make a shearer's heart Settle down about his boot-tops on the morning of the start. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,35</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> These sheep were the real wild sheep - not the ordinary domestic sheep gone wild. The latter, long-tailers and woollies which have escaped muster and gone semi-wild, are known as "rough" sheep. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,154</i>)

### rough skate
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka spotted skate]

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> ROUGH SKATE ...<i> Other names: Raja australis</i>; whai (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,192</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> ROUGH SKATE [<i>Raja australis</i>] ... Other names include skate and sometimes spotted skate. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,14</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>ROUGH SKATE Raja nasuta</i> ... The rough skate is named for the many small prickles on the upper surface of the disc that give the skin a distinctive rough texture. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,75,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Rough skate Raja nasuta</i> ... Common around New Zealand . <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,34</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i> SKATE</i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Raja nasuta</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Skate. (c) Other common names: Smooth Skate (<i>R. nasuta</i>); Rough Skate (<i>Raja australis</i>); Stingray; Stingaree; Flaps (trade name for Skate `wings'). (d) Principal localities where taken: Napier, Wellington, Nelson, Picton, Banks Peninsula, Timaru, Greymouth. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,43</i>)

>  [species name] rough skate .. [scientific name] <i>Raja nasuta</i> .. [other name(s)] <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] skate, rough 291 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> ROUGH SKATE ROUGH SKATE [<i>Raja australis</i>] ... Other names include skate and sometimes spotted skate. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,14</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] skate, spotted 291 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### rough stock
 
 <i>n.</i> inferior stock (See [cf. clean stock](../C#clean-stock))

>  <b>CRAY</b> The `window dressing' has covered a good deal of `rough stock' of doubtful value. <i>(1933,National Welfare,32</i>)

>  A strong feeling was expressed that the time had arrived when we must do something better with our land than continue to use it only as a run for rough stock. <i>(Apr 16, 1883,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

### rough stuff
 
 <i>n.</i> coarse gold

>  <b>SMITH</b> ... That brought the `colours' up to sight. Some times the `rough' stuff too [i.e. coarse gold] <i>(1946,Poetical Works,200</i>)

### rough tree fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>

>  Sometimes called the Rough tree fern, [Dicksonia squarrosa] has dark harsh leaves and occasionally sprouts offsets at the base. <i>(Nov 30, 2002,Dominion Post,F15</i>)

>  More familiar is the wheki or rough tree fern (<i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i>), which produced buds on its trunk. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/69</i>)

>  <b>FIELD</b> (And) <i>(1890,Ferns of NZ</i>)

>  (And) <i>(1916,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>D[icksonia] squarrosa</i> ... Slender or Rough Tree-fern. WEKI. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,8</i>)

>  <i>rough tree fern [=] wheki [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,65</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Dicksonia squarrosa</i> WHEKII ... Rough tree fern ... Trunk dark brown or black, rough with persistent stalk bases, ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,420</i>)

### rough-as-guts
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The Purple Death has scored a few hits over the festive season. It's a wine with a difference, recommended as `rough-as-guts' drinking. ... Word of the Purple Death has been spread by mouth but has even reached as far south as the Antarctic. ... At around $10 a bottle, few liquor outlets sell it as well as the Sapich brothers out Henderson way, in West Auckland. <i>(Jan 4, 1987,Sunday News,3</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The No. 8 fencing wire is that long-standing bastion of national identity reflecting rough-as-guts innovators living off the land. <i>(Apr 13, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  He even went to shearing school and, during university breaks, when he was about 20, hung about with a rough-as-guts gang. <i>(Apr 22, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

### rough-faced shag
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  <i> King Shag Leucocarbo carunculatus</i> (GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Carunculated shag, Marlborough Sounds shag, rough-faced shag, Cook Strait cormorant</i>. ... The only shag with pink feet in the Cook Strait and Marlborough Sounds area. Prominent caruncles at base of bill. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,118</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PHALACROCORAX CARUNCULATUS. (ROUGH-FACED SHAG.) [Buller 1888 shifts 1873 <i>P. carunculatus</i> to <i>P. imperialis</i>, Emperor Shag; and makes a new entry for <i>P. carunculatus</i>, as Rough-faced Shag.] <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,332,1888 2nd edn 2/155 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Hypoleucus carunculatus</i> Gm. Rough-faced Shag. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/75</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

### rough-guts
 
 <i>n.</i> roughly-behaved person; ill-bred person (See [cf rough as guts](../C#cf-rough-as-guts))

>  How dare that little rough-guts make fun of Firpo. <i>(1949,Landfall,9/50</i>) [NOTES: (B. Mason)]

### rough-meter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A test with a `rough-meter,' an instrument attached to the front axle of a car, recorded only nine inches of rise and fall to the mile over the runway, the lowest runway figures the engineers had heard of and giving almost a concrete billiard table compared with the Whenuapai surface. <i>(Sep 10, 1963,Evening Post,28</i>)

### Rough-on-Rats
 
 <i>n.</i> a former brand of rat poison

>  A young man named William Ayres took a dose of "Rough on Rats" in a brothel in Grey-street... It came out in the evidence that "rough on rats" consisted of arsenic, magnesia, and a little charcoal. <i>(Mar 26, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,00</i>)

>  [Advt] R-r-rats! Rats! Rats! Rough on Rats! Hang your dogs and drown your cats; We give a plan for every man To clear his house with Rough on Rats. <i>(May 28, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,37</i>)

>  [Advt] Rough on Rats is the most extensively advertised and has the largest sale of any Rat, Mouse, Vermin and Insect destroyer in the world. Sold to all nations. <i>(Jul 25, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,35</i>)

>  <b>LAWLOR</b> Willie, and two other brats, / Licked up all the Rough-on-Rats. / Father said, when mother cried, / 'Never mind, they'll die outside.' <i>(1962,More Wellington Days,79</i>)

>  A married woman named Catherine Sims, wife of a labourer, committed suicide by taking rough-on-rats. <i>(Jun 15, 1900,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  Joseph Peebles, who attempted suicide by taking "rough on rats" was committed for trial at the Supreme Court this morning. <i>(Mar 12, 1884,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  She was conscious and admitted having "taken some 'rough on rats' in water," ... <i>(Aug 7, 1890,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  "ROUGH ON RATS." -Cleans out rats, mice, roaches, flies, ants, bed-bugs, beetles, insects, skunks, jack-rabbits, gophers. <i>(Jun 2, 1883,Wanganui Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Afterwards he endeavored to commit suicide by drinking a bottle of "rough on rats," but he was nursed back to a sufficient state of physical fitness to expiate on the scaffold, that penalty which the Supreme Court allotted him. <i>(Sep 29, 1923,NZ Truth,5</i>)

>  The jury returned a verdict "That the deceased [sc. a 16-year old girl] took a dose of 'rough on rats' while in a state of temporary insanity caused by reading sensational literature." <i>(Jul 17, 1890,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  The unfortunate man, after administering "rough on rats" to the children, gave them morphine to ease their pain. <i>(Dec 4, 1890,Otago Witness,31</i>)

>  [Advt] Don't die in the House. "Rough on Rats" cleans out rats, mice, beetles, roaches, bed-bugs, flies, ants, insects, moles, jack-rabbits, gophers, 7d. <i>(Jan 6, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  At the inquest on the body of Alice Barnaby, who committed suicide at Wellington by taking "Rough on Rats," a verdict was returned that the deceased committed suicide while in a state of temporary insanity. <i>(Jun 14, 1885,Wairarapa Daily,2</i>)

>  The deceased, who was 32 years of age, took the greater portion of a box of "Rough on Rats" and immediately became seriously ill. <i>(Oct 13, 1917,Wairarapa Daily Times,4</i>)

### rough-rider
 
 <i>n.</i> a horse-breaker; a rider of unbroken horses [NZ quotations: 1879-1947]

>  <b>W.H.S. ROBERTS</b> Sometimes the horse succeeds in throwing the rider at the first bound, but a good rough rider defies it for a long time, unless the horse manages to jump out of the saddle or burst the girths. <i>(Aug 23, 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Aug 23 W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 35]

>  <b>SWANTON</b> They [unbroken horses] are saddled and mounted by rough riders. <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,122</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DILKE</b> Maori rough-riders .... <i>(1868,Greater Britain,393</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> [A farmer,] his hat a `rough-rider' felt. [i.e., a slouch hat] <i>(1904,Life in NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1904 Baughan Life in NZ (Empire Review Mar: 176)]

### rough-riding
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  On a cattle-run the tasks of mustering and drafting the stock and branding the youngsters are very heavy work, needing the display of considerable powers of rough-riding among the horsemen; while on a sheep-run the lambing and shearing seasons tax every power of the station-hands. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,157</i>)

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Apart from simple climbing there was, for example, `crossing' the plantation on the treetops, and Rough-riding, in which one player sat on a fir tree branch while the others took the end of it and pulled it up and down and then let it go flying up in the air. The player who could stay on the longest was the rough-riding champion. Of a similar nature was Buck-jumping, in which individual players rode a macrocarpa or gum branch [113] to see who could rise the highest. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,112</i>)

### rough-up
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  There is a rough-up jockey in Ameria named Duggan, who is not particular as to whether he causes a jockey to be knocked over the rails, under the rails, or any other old place. <i>(Sep 10, 1910,NZ Truth,272/2/4</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fight, a free-for-all

>  Having given him a rough-up, and knowing that the old horse had a bit of pace left, they thought he was good enough for the journey. <i>(May 31, 1884,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The fourth round opened with a rough-up, in which Thomas lost his coolness and suffered a lot. <i>(Mar 25, 1909,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  <b>CHURCH</b> I enjoyed that rough-up <i>(1916,Tonks,91</i>)

>  <b>MILLER, C.T. & T.</b> Hurt my knee a little in a wrestling match on my bunk during one of the usual roughups. <i>(Aug 15, 1917,Diary,121,1939 ed.</i>)

>  St. Hippo and Royal Rose had a real solid rough-up on the training track on Friday morning. <i>(Aug 3, 1893,Otago Witness,30</i>)

>  The three year old Fanny Fisher colt had a "rough-up" before he left, and acquitted himself so well that I should advise readers of the WAIKATO TIMES who are fond of having "a bet" on a good thing, to invest a pound on him for the Canterbury Derby. <i>(Oct 10, 1876,Waikato Times,2</i>)

### roughage
 
 <i>n.</i> rough growth; scrub [(farming)]

>  The pastures have a magnificent growth, absolutely free from roughage and with good body in the grass. <i>(May 9, 1908,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The first work of the settlers was to burn off the roughage on large areas of country. .... [82] before lighting a rough facing ... covered with roughage. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,80, 82</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Roughage - Pasture growth grown to maturity and remaining in situ for winter and summer grazing. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,362</i>)

>  This will be more needed this season because of the very small hay crop and the small amount of roughage available on all pastures. <i>(Jan 26, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  The pig is an omnivorous animal and needs "roughage" and fresh green feed for his best health and growth, and to produce meat of the best quality. <i>(Apr 24, 1901,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  There should be a good deal of roughage left for the autumn, which always materially helps the winter feeding, because the stock do not require to go on the turnips so early. <i>(Jan 12, 1926,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

### roughie
 
 <i>n.</i> a racehorse which is a rank outsider

>  One would recommend any young fellow taking on the sport to get a good one in the first place, as all know it takes a good sort to win these days, and the best horse is cheaper to keep than a roughie. <i>(Dec 14, 1920,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  It is wonderful the difference a fashionable jockey makes to a horse's price. With a `roughie' on they go out at 20 to 1, but when a crack jockey is up they are often 6 to 4 propositions. <i>(Jul 16, 1910,NZ Truth,264/2/8</i>) [NOTES: [contrast AND 2. an outsider (horse) 1922]]

>  Backing a roughie gives as good a chance of a return as following the money - favourites have a poor record in the cup. <i>(Dec 31, 1989,Sunday Star (Auckland),B12</i>)

>  [Hdg] Graeme oozes confidence in Easter roughie. <i>(Apr 22, 2001,Sunday Star Times,B12</i>)

>  It was of very little avail to them when the "roughie" won easily. <i>(Mar 7, 1908,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  The "jim" went on the Karamu prad, but the jockey became unseated during the race and the second favorite [sic] running off the course let the "roughie" to win comfortably. <i>(Jun 11, 1910,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  Khama 9.13 has a roughie's chance only, but nothing in the tail commands more than a passing thought. <i>(Jul 13, 1907,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  Roughie: a roughie is more than just someone who has had a late night! Also known as 'a long shot' or a 'rank outsider', it is a horse with very long odds. .. if you win on a roughie, you will be basking in the glory all day. <i>(Dec 5, 2007,Dominion Post Entertain Wellington,23</i>) [NOTES: Wellington Cup Carnival promotion]



 
 <i>n.</i> a poor game

>  When you play a roughie, everyone knows about it, but if you get the chance to play again, you lift yourself. <i>(Dec 3, 1999,Evening Post,22</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [(station)] (See [cf. woollie n.; rough sheep](../W#woollie-n.;-rough-sheep))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> [get quot] <i>Ibid.</i> 115 Those odd little lots of dodging old `roughies' <i>(1947,Wayleggo,94</i>)

>  <b>N EWTON</b> When he took over [the station], the only domestic sheep were `roughies' - long tailers and doubles that had multiplied from those which had escaped the wind-up muster four years before. <i>(1973,Big Country,86,1977 ed.</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> a rough or hard-to-shear sheep I did not intend to be caught with `roughies' left in my pen when the pressure was on. A shearer will always try to put one `around' his mate when he has a full pen of sheep to pick from. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,93</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  ... I hasten to explain, the stock agent bought on our behalf, a further dozen 18-month steers. `They're roughies,' he advised by phone on the day of the purchase and I admit we waited with just a little trepidation for them to arrive. <i>(May 12, 1985,NZ Times,8</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> an unqualified or rough-and-ready builder or craftsman

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> A type ... leading characteristics anarchical tendency. Pride in being a `roughie'. <i>(Feb 27, 1915,New Zealander's Diary,20</i>) [NOTES: (c1922)]

>  Mr Quigley expressed concern that there were some `roughies' - unqualified carpenters - engaged in building .... <i>(Mar 10, 1975,Dominion,2</i>)

### roughing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It is a very shorte spell after so much roughing [on a West Coast trip]. <i>(Jun 7, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,189,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Two gentlemen of the proposed party were `new chums' like myself, and were strongly in favour of a little roughing ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,126,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAVIDSON</b> When our family arrived in the colony first we experienced some heavy roughing. <i>(1889,Stories of NZ Life,40</i>)

### roughing out
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the using of machines with blades to clear land of large trees, leaving some debris behind in windrows.

>  <b>FERRIS</b> The roughing-out work was done with either a bulldozer or treedozer. The treedozer had the advantage of leaving a windrow on either side of the blade as it passes through the ground surface in the roughing out operation. When the treedozer was used for roughing out, windrows were left throughout the paddock containing stumps, bush, debris and some soil. <i>(1940,Cavalcade of Farming 1840-1940</i>)

### roughy
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Paratrachichthys trailli</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> COMMON ROUGHY (Sandpaper fish) <i>Paratrachichthys trailli</i> ... This species of roughy has small rough scales and because of this [175] feature it is sometimes known as the sandpaper fish. ... Common roughys are found throughout New Zealand, although they are rarely seen north of Cook Strait. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,174,1984 ed.</i>)

>  Paratrachichthys trailli (Hutton). ... The Roughy or Sand-paper Fish, as the species is called, is not uncommon in trawler catches in New Zealand and southern Australia. <i>(1939,TrNZI,69/231</i>) [NOTES: (Whitley and Phillipps)]

### roughy/roughie
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep that has missed at least one docking or shearing. (See [double-fleecer; double-decker; hermit; double](../D#double-fleecer;-double-decker;-hermit;-double))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> When the Brooksdale gang is out these roughies duck down the Castle Hill side and vice versa. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,94</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> When he took over, the only domestic sheep were 'roughies' - longtailers and doubles that had multiplied from those which had escaped the wind-up muster four years before. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,86</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> Bill had been straggle mustering at the Camden and found there were quite a few roughies that it was impossible to drive in. We were shooting and skinning goats and wild sheep and catching the roughies and shearing them. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,149</i>)

>  <b>PETER</b> We straggle a block and trace the roughies as they usually have a double fleece. ie we muster for the stragglers and blade-shear bellies and under neck so they can walk out. <i>(2000,Peter Glossary,2</i>)

### round
 
 <i>n.</i> a tailor-made cigarette [ca1983 Oct 21 Ray Hancox Heard it used in Havelock Pub.]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Wire is called by number or gauge, while posts are rounds, half-rounds or quarters. <i>(May 9, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (goldmining)

>  <b>HOGG</b> 'Twas when the Rocky River rush began/ ... `and stories told/ Of ounces gotten in a single pan,/ Of nuggety, and round, and shotty gold./ The news like a bush fire through the diggings ran,/ <i>(1875,Lays and Rhymes,176</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> Round: A pattern of holes, such as a drift round. `The round' generally referred to the completely drilled and charged drive face. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),331</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a circular-shaped bale of hay.

>  But small bales have not been completely replaced by the big rounds and big squares. <i>(Oct 19, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)

>  Hay for sale. $4.50, size 15 rounds. Hay, top quality rounds, no weed. <i>(Apr 13, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,24</i>)

>  Hay straw and balage, rounds and squares, good quality. <i>(Jul 18, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,26</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a split post

>  [Advt] 700 No 1 3.0m round posts <i>(Jun 2, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,16</i>)

### round finned whaler
 
 <i>n.</i> [round finned whaler shark; no supporting evidence of usage yet]

>  Round-finned whaler, <i>Carcharinus lamia</i>. This species is the `lamia,' or `requin,' of the early writers. It is found in the Pacific in small numbers, being most probably identical with [261] the fish recorded as <i>Carcharinus macrurus</i> (Rams. & Ogil. ...1888) ... Fig. 3. - Round finned whaler, <i>Carcharinus lamia. </i> <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

### round the world for ninepence
 
 <i>phr.</i> methylated spirits (as a drink)

>  (Round the World for Ninepence, Jessies's [sic] Dream, Fix Bayonets, devotees called methylated spirits, recommending orange juice as a dilutant. Diluted or neat, it can, and does, cause blindness). <i>(Jun 10, 1984,NZ Times,10</i>) [NOTES: (J. Henderson)]

>  <b>McGILL</b> round the world for threepence/fourpence/ninepence drinking methylated spirits: reinforced by Jim Henderson's memorable description of his one snort of meths, mistaken for cough mixture, as `utterly vile, then chest lit up like a cauliflower.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,94</i>)

### round trap
 
 <i>n.</i> [(obs) [?d.] E. Fairburn Maharatanga 8 Previous to this [sc. first cricket game 1833-4] the only game with bat and ball which we New Zealand boys knew was Round Trap,]

### round-leaved beech
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Nothofagus menziesii</i>

>  <b>KIRK</b> With the view of obviating the confusion caused by the misuse of the common names hitherto applied to the New Zealand beeches, I would suggest the adoption of new names based on the obvious characteristics of their foliage:- For <i>Fagus fusca</i>, tooth-leaved beech; for <i>Fagus solandri,</i> entire-leaved beech; and for <i>Fagus menziesii</i>, round-leaved beech. <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,17</i>)

### round-leaved mint
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>mint, round-leaved </i>[s] <i>Mentha rotundifolia</i> (L.) Huds. <i>Ibid.</i> 65 <i>round-leaved mint</i> [s] <i>Mentha rotundifolia</i> (L.) Huds. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,49</i>)

### round-up
 
 <i>n.</i> [also rounding up n. below]

>  <b>SWANTON</b> Previous to shearing those is the general muster, which means the rounding up and bringing in of all the sheep, good and bad, on the `run' <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,97</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> Minor adventures in rounding up wild cattle in swamp country running up from the lake on the north side of Taratu also befell Thomas. Single-handed, he one day made a very satisfactory round-up .. <i>(1937,Thomas,44</i>)

### roundabout
 
 <i>n.</i> a (? missionary) garment to hide the nakedness of Maori women.

>  <b>LUSH</b> In two minutes the whole lot [of the bathing Maori schoolgirls] stood quietly looking at us, clothed from top to toe in their long full roundabouts. [177] In came a file of five young Maori women, the head scholars in their blue striped roundabouts. <i>(Jan 17, 1856,Auckland Journals,176</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> For these [domestic] services the whaler engaged to dress his wife in a `round about,' treat her kindly, .... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/293</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> [The Maori women's] usual dress is .... a shapeless sack of printed calico, called a `roundabout', tied round the neck but loose at the waist. <i>(1861,New Zealand, As It Was and As It Is,17</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> I also gave [the Maori girl] eight yards of Navy blue print (which every one knows is enough for a `roundabout') <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,118</i>)

>  <b>PHILIPS</b> The hosters [at Waiwera 1850] did not dress for dinner ... her usual attire being a Maori roundabout. <i>(1890,Reminiscences of Early Days,7</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Ngapuhi girls ... well plumped-out of figure, swing up and down the roadway flaunting the print gowns and the brightly-coloured `roundabouts' and the glittering ear-rings bought with the dollars of the sailormen. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/7</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The payment ... consisted of ... 100 figs of tobacco, four greenstone pendants, ... two `roundabouts' (loose brightly coloured blouses for the ladies), and one axe. <i>(1940,Sir Donald Maclean,33</i>)

### roundie
 
 <i>n.</i> a tailor-made cigarette [(naval and prisoners') ca1948 N.Z. Navy (Les Cleveland) ca1984 also prison slang.] (See [also round](../A#also-round))

>  <b>Ihimaera & Long</b> He was smoking roundies! <i>(1982,Into the World of Light,175</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,253</i>)

### roundmouth
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cyclothone microdon</i> (Gunther)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Roundmouth <i>Cyclothone microdon</i> (Gunther) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,400</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 400]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] roundmouth 562 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### Roundtable
 
 <i>n.</i> the New Zealand Business Roundtable, a business association and pressure group, of right wing and extreme free market views. In extended use, a synonym for well-off people likely to have such views.

>  <b>GEE</b> "It's Round Table country, mate. The rates are something fearful." <i>(1992,Going West,88</i>)

>  This made me think that the allegations of the roundtable involvement are true and the ad is in fact illegitimate. <i>(Oct 12, 2000,City Voice,3</i>)

>  <i>(Jun 5, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>) [NOTES: The Roundtable is sharpening its freemarket axe for the Dairy Board... The Roundtable is intent on grafting its basic freemarket ideology onto agriculture.]

### roupo
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [raupo](../R#raupo))

### rourou
 
 <i>n.</i> contribution; food basket http://translator.kedri.info/translate.php [noun.]

>  The arts and crafts section comprised tukutuku, ... plaiting (rourou [fn A small basket used for cooked food.], rough kits and floor mats), taniko work and making poi balls. <i>(Mar 28, 1936,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/212</i>) [NOTES: Ngata : 1936 Mar 28 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/212 (Ngata)]

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/155</i>)

>  <b>TE PUKENGANUI [sic] O TUWHARETOA WANANGA</b> .. All whanau asked to donate a rourou please <b>For rourou donations please phone: Gerry Karekare</b>... <i>(Aug 16, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,16</i>)

### rouse
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to work as a shedhand or rouseabout with shearers.

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> .. he'd had time to get his first taste of the magical shearing world, 'rousing' in local sheds for Jack Harrison and Wally Smith. <i>(1993,Gone West for Shearing,62</i>)

### rouse (onto)
 
 <i>v.</i> to scold

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... his aversion to children nearly got Madge down. ... He was always inclined to rouse onto our kiddies.... <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,104</i>)

>  <b>Sargeson</b> roused him up hill and down dale: dressed him down thoroughly; roused on to me, scolded me. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,303</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

### rouse about
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  Her involvement with netball was encouraged by her mother. `I used to go along to practices and if Mum needed someone to rouse about on the court she'd yell out to me.' <i>(Sep 27, 1983,Dominion,11/6</i>)

### rouse/roust
 
 <i>v.</i> to make a fuss, become angry

>  About time the Manawatu Railway Company started running Sunday trains. The religion relating rummies will rouse a bit, but that's only a detail. <i>(Oct 20, 1906,NZ Truth,70/1/4</i>)

>  ... for in the Australiasian vernacular the person who `rousts' or `rouses' goes temporarily mad and yells, and is an irresponsible person while he is `rousting' or `rousing', and the condition so nearly approaches the mild insanity of the wowser in his neurotic outbursts of oratory that Churchill must be congratulated upon stumbling upon a great truth. <i>(Apr 15, 1911,NZ Truth,303/1/2</i>)

### rouseabout
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Soon the shuffling of shoes and squeaking of doors multiply as shearers and rouseabouts (shedhands or "rousies") make their way up to the cookhouse. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/80</i>)

>  He was employed as a rouseabout at the Criterion Hotel for the last 12 months and accumulated a variety of goods to the total value of about L40, nearly all from the hotel and the property of the licensee and his family and of the boarders and servants. <i>(Jun 13, 1891,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  <b>DARE</b> My official capacity was cowboy or `rouseabout', meaning odd jobber ... <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,146</i>)

>  Daniel Driscoll, a rouse-about-man in the employ of Gillon and Devery, of No Town, appeared to answer to a charge of maliciously assaulting one John Foley, of No Town, on the 14th inst. <i>(Jul 25, 1876,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  Sergeant-Major Thompson called Peter Stobo, residing at the Imperial Hotel, Herbet, who deposed that Dallas was employed by Mr Watson as general rouse-about, and slept in the room next to witness. <i>(May 4, 1881,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> So Hawk had taken against the nice new house and stayed on in the rouseabouts'shack. <i>(Mar 2, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  Hotel-keepers complain that they are unable to get the aristocrats of labour to work for them when they advertise "Wanted, a rouseabout." <i>(Jun 25, 1904,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The two became devoted to each other, the vigorous little rouseabout-gardener carrying him round on his back at every opportunity. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,26</i>)

>  <b>ELKINGTON</b> When a man first goes on a station and is not an old hand he is engaged as a "roustabout," which means that he is given jobs which do not require much experience and in which he works under other men. <i>(1906,Adrift in NZ,145</i>)

>  On the other hand, Kiwi comedians only have to master Gumboot, the language of roustabouts, fishermen and sportscasters, and they're away screaming. <i>(May 15, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70</i>)

>  He had steadily sunk through the various stages of gentleman at large, digger,.. rouseabout, to the bedrock of continual swagging. <i>(Nov 11, 1897,Otago Witness,50</i>)

>  He [a union secretary] has returned to work three days a week as, in his words, a "rouseabout". <i>(Jan 28, 1993,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Gillespie.. had joined his father as rouseabout, and then shepherd, many years ago. <i>(Jul 22, 1924,Otago Witness,58</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearing gang worker who sweeps the board and occasionally does extra duties.

>  If the shearing is put off for another 10 days the harvest will be on before the shearing is near finished, and the rouseabouts will leave for the harvest, as well as the slow shearers. <i>(Nov 20, 1876,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  And now for the rouse abouts. Their case is not the best in the world. They, like the shearers, have to put up with bad food and bad accomodation, and like musterers they complain of not being paid in proportion to the work they do. The rouseabout has to work ten hours a day, and nine or thereabouts on Saturdays. <i>(Feb 5, 1891,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Would take what came, nor shirk nor fret, 'brander,' 'rouseabout' or dagger ... <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,28</i>)

>  Prior to getting a farm of my own, I knocked around on stations and farms in many capacities from station cadet to rouseabout. <i>(Feb 20, 1930,NZ Dairy Exporter,45</i>)

>  <b>WILSON & FRASER</b> They had a Maori youth who acted as cowboy and general rouseabout. <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,31</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Mr Guthrie-Smith and Mr Stuart and I did all the sheep work, but we were all rouseabouts then and did whatever was wanted. <i>(1959,Guthrie-Smith of Tutira,23</i>)

>  <b>BECKETT</b> No back country sheep station is without its rouseabout. <i>(1978,Mountains of Erewhon, The,24</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> rouseabout or rousey: a general farm hand. In that context the term is now rather rare. In a shearing gang it refers to all those who are not actually shearers. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,67</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> There were several men who went under the designation of rouseabout, or general hand. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,7</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to act as a general hand on a farm or station.

>  <b>GORDON</b> I would have been about three and a half years rouseabouting and also packing for the shepherds [with packhorses.] <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,106</i>)

>  WANTED, an active Lad, about 15, who can ride and rousabout. <i>(Feb 11, 1890,Hawke's Bay Herald,1</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a general farm hand, who does many different duties.

>  Their mode of living is on the strict hand-to-mouth principle; the future is a disagreeable prospect, which they endeavour to ignore, content if the present affords them a fair modicum of enjoyment; big cheques are earned, but they are melted with much greater celerity; and so the round of life goes on from year to year, until old age finds them still on the wallaby, burdened with aches and pains, unfitted for the only work of which they know anything, and glad to accept the billet of a farm yard "rouseabout," or "wood and water Joey." <i>(Dec 2, 1882,Waikato Times,2</i>)

>  WANTED, a steady Man, a Permanent SITUATION on a Farm or Station, as Rousabout, or to drive a team of horses. <i>(Dec 14, 1878,Timaru Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Would take what came, nor shirk nor fret, 'brander,' 'rouseabout' or dagger ... <i>(1889,Colonial Couplets,28</i>)

>  Prior to getting a farm of my own, I knocked around on stations and farms in many capacities from station cadet to rouseabout. <i>(Feb 20, 1930,NZ Dairy Exporter,45</i>)

>  <b>WILSON & FRASER</b> They had a Maori youth who acted as cowboy and general rouseabout. <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,31</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Mr Guthrie-Smith and Mr Stuart and I did all the sheep work, but we were all rouseabouts then and did whatever was wanted. <i>(1959,Guthrie-Smith of Tutira,23</i>)

>  <b>BECKETT</b> No back country sheep station is without its rouseabout. <i>(1978,Mountains of Erewhon, The,24</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> rouseabout or rousey: a general farm hand. In that context the term is now rather rare. In a shearing gang it refers to all those who are not actually shearers. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,67</i>)

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> There were several men who went under the designation of rouseabout, or general hand. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,7</i>)

### rouseabouting
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) acting as a general hand in a shearing gang or on a farming property.

>  Rouseabouting can be an international job. <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,29</i>)

>  Shearing, and so rouseabouting, is a seasonal game. <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,31</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [caption] Above: Rouseabouting can be an international job. Here Margaret Way (in red) and friend Robin are pictured atop a wool pile in an English woolshed. `No woolpresses here,' comments Margaret. `Robin and I wound each one of these fleeces by hand.' <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,29</i>)

### rousey
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rousie](../R#rousie))

### rousie
 
 <i>v.</i> [It's one thing to pay a Maori to rousie for you or to shear your sheep ...]



 
 <i>n.</i> a rouseabout

>  Soon the shuffling of shoes and squeaking of doors multiply as shearers and rouseabouts (shedhands or "rousies") make their way up to the cookhouse. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/80</i>)

>  Club is the rousie whose days as a shearer are rumoured to have finished with a gunshot wound. <i>(Jun 10, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  So the hope we fondly cherished that he soon would fade away / Was ne'er fulfilled in eighteen eighty-nine; / But we rallied round the "rousey" when the shed cut out that day, / And gave three ringing cheers for auld lang syne. <i>(Jan 6, 1909,Otago Witness,71</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> They entertained the `rouseys' with a lot of fairy tales . <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,49</i>) [NOTES: c1900-1910 W. Goldstone in 1950 Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 49]

>  <b>MEEK</b> And the rouseys in their shirt-sleeves passed the pannikins around. <i>Ibid.</i> 111 ROUSEY, ROUSEABOUT: Shed hands in a shearing-shed who can be called on to do, or help with any job. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,11</i>)

>  <i>(1952,Arena</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> <i>(1966,Short Stories (various authors),189</i>) [NOTES: 1953 Middleton in 1966 Short Stories 189 [get quot]]

>  <b>BOWEN</b> ROUSIE. This is the poor old rouseabout who has a great variety of jobs. Australians sometimes call this hand a `blue tongue'. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,157</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> The bell rang, the whole procedure stopped, shearers trooped out, half shorn sheep were pushed back into the catching pens. Jimmy Greave the classer hurriedly whispered to William Steuart to desist, but loppies, rousies, and even young Jim, who was assisting to pen up, followed out from the side door to where the shoots from the stands gave the nearest exit, to the level space of the outside turf, that made eventually the [boxing] ring. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,50</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> `Don't tell me you're one of the rousies, Sonny ....?'[sic] <i>(1964,Walk on the Beach,166</i>) [NOTES: (Not for a Seagull)]

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> What with the dogs the roussies and penners-up and daggers brought to give them a hand in the shed and yards ... <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,127,1976 ed.</i>)

>  SHEEP-O: The call, timid from beginners, imperious from guns for the rousie (rouse-about) to fill the catching pen. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>) [NOTES: [Shearer's language]]

>  [caption] A change from school work: Christine McIntyre, a fifth former at Wanganui Girls College and daughter of Apiti farmer Rusty McIntyre, rousies during the [shearing] competition. <i>(Apr 23, 1981,Dominion,6</i>)

>  <b>CHARLES</b> But you will hear the rousies singing as they head down to the pub. <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea,28</i>)

>  <b>BEATON</b> GINNY: Nah. Only time I stopped was when I worked the sheds. ...I was a fucken good rousy. Even sheared a sheep once. <i>(1984,Outside In,106</i>) [NOTES: [in a shearing gang]]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> I gathered by the ... excitement among the rousies that Roger Pearce was going to `burn me off' with his next full pen. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,93</i>)

>  A rouseabout - also variously known as a rousie, woolhandler, shedhand, fleeco, the person `picking up' or a blue tongue - is responsible for the passage of wool after being shorn from the sheep's back to the wool press where it is baled. <i>Ibid.</i> 33 A rousie, or wool winder as they're called in England, would at least pay for half a return ticket from New Zealand in the two-month season there. <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,31</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> rousie rouseabout or shearing shed hand. ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,94</i>)

>  As youngsters they worked around the sheds as rousies. <i>(Jun 1998,Mana,No. 22: 53</i>)

>  <b>FARRELL</b> So Martin moved on, rousie for a gang, a spell at the freezer in Prestwick, a few months on a fishing boat round Stewart Island. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,57</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shearing shedhand.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> They had eight rousies on the board ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,29</i>)

>  <b>WATERSON</b> Finally Wart's crew was sorted out. Four shearers, five rousies and a cook. <i>(1999,Some Buggers I've Seen,60</i>)

>  <b>WATERSON</b> .. this Ned Jones came over from Wales to work as a rousie in my shearing gang. <i>(1999,Some Buggers I've Seen,151</i>)

>  He became involved in the wool industry when he met a guy in a pub, who offered him a job as a rousy. [rousey; rousy] <i>(2001,Wool Grower,Winter 24</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to act as a general worker, especially in a shearing shed.

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> But now he 'rousies' for the gang, his shearing days are done. <i>(1975,More From the High Country,49</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> This fella was a wee Aussie guy, he didn't know bugger all, he knew nothing and he couldn't rousie for nothing. <i>(1999,Tales from the Shearing Shed,15</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Like most people Tui Two Bob entered the shearing industry through her father, starting out rousieing for him in 1982. <i>(1999,Tales from the Shearing Shed,31</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a general farm assistant or rural dogsbody.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The 'rousie' had the billy boiling so .. they collected the tucker box and sought a prominence, where they could get a full view of the flat. <i>(1949,High Country Days,22</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Besides Ray Birdsall there are nine musterers and shepherds, the majority of them there only for the mustering season. There are also two or three 'rousies', a cowman-gardener, a book-keeper, and a permanent rabbiter. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,29</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> rouseabout or rousey: a general farm hand. In that context the term is now rather rare. In a shearing gang it refers to all those who are not actually shearers. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,67</i>)

>  <b>FIRTH</b> For other outside work, a rousie will have to be employed. <i>(1988,Farmers are Bananas,136</i>)

>  But I served for years as a rousie during my varsity vacations and I have studied the art in great depth. <i>(May 2002,Growing Today,61</i>)

### rousie's chewing gum
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  rousie's chewing gum: Dags. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/27</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### rousie's handpiece
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a woolshed broom.

>  <b>WARD</b> rousie's handpiece: a broom. <i>(1997,Glossary,1</i>)

>  rousie's handpiece: broom. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### roust
 
 <i>v.</i> to stir up, scold [ca1900 He rousted them up for getting drunk cf P. rouse 2. pron [raus] to grouse, scold esp. if coarsely: Aust: 20c. 3. upbraid with many words: Aust:20; AC 1900-1910.]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to dig out; to rummage and find; to rake out; rout out - [ca 1900 He went in and rousted her out of bed]

### roust on
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  She told her ... husband she'd `rowst' on him. Her idea of `rowsting', as she called it, was to send for a peeler. <i>(Jan 27, 1906,NZ Truth,32/8/6</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to turn on (a person)

>  He "rousted," as the saying goes, on the man who sacked him. <i>(Feb 13, 1904,NZ Observer,2</i>)

### roustabout
 
 <i>n.</i> a rouseabout, a handyman (espec. on a station or farm,a slushy

>  After a while, I thought to meself thim good paple had betther business than showing a roustabout the road, and that if I was to foind it, I'd betther use the sivin sinses that had been given me for that purpose. <i>(Sep 6, 1884,Waikato Times,1</i>)

>  Just as the first roustabout dumped his basket of coal a quick, little squall came up, from where nobody knew, and lifting my Panama off my head, whirled it beyond my reach. <i>(Dec 23, 1899,Taranaki Herald,4</i>)

>  We have had our fill of swamp bush felling in winter ... Only the roustabouts count among all hands. Not the ploughmen. <i>(Jun 25, 1888,Chudleigh Diary,362,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ELKINGTON</b> ... roustabout ... <i>(1906,Adrift in NZ,145</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> `I'm willing to ... get any kind of job I can.' ... `I didn't send you to Oxford to become a roustabout.' <i>(1925,Allen Adair,4,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> Rabbiters, harvesters, shearers, ploughmen, roustabouts, leaving their jobs, carrying their swags, and with eyes turned towards homes in Dunedin, were frequently headed off by the hotels. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,26,1949 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> ... roustabout ... <i>(1936,Hunted, The,207</i>)

>  <b>BIERNACKI</b> When one fo the roust-abouts [on the station] kicked him <i>(1949,Poems, Verses and Rhymes,6</i>)

>  <b>FRAME</b> Alan found it hard to decide ... finding a job as a wharfie, farm roustabout, shearing hand, [etc] <i>(1963,Reservoir, The,162</i>) [NOTES: (Triumph of Poetry)]

>  <b>STEWART</b> Frank March had begun life as a roustabout on Hawke's Bay sheep farms. <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,18</i>)

>  roustabout ... 2. Austral. & N.Z. a variant of rouseabout (sense 1). <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1006</i>) [NOTES: [contrast NZPOD 665]]

>  The work of roustabouts was covered by the New Zealand Oil Exploration Award and their membership should be that of the New Zealand Labourers Union. <i>(Dec 20, 1983,Dominion,5</i>)

>  Rousei.a-bouti. (rousi.&adot.-bouti.), n. [Add.] 2. A general Rousti.a-bouti. (rousti.&adot.-bouti.), hand or man of all work on a sheep station; esp., a man employed at shearing time to assist the shearers but not to shear sheep himself. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)

>  WANTED, Roustabout for hotel work; must be sober and good cleaner. <i>(Nov 11, 1910,Evening Post,1</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a general farm or stationhand.

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> Only the roustabouts count among all hands. <i>(Jun 25, 1888,Chudleigh Diary,362</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>ELKINGTON</b> When a man first goes on a station and is not an old hand, he is engaged as a 'roustabout.' <i>(1906,Adrift in NZ,145</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> .. I secured a job at fifteen shillings a week, as roustabout on St Helen's station ... <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,24</i>)

>  <b>MCCONOCHIE</b> In his younger days he had worked as a roustabout on a Marlborough sheep station. <i>(1966,You'll Learn No Harm from the Hills,24</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. during the evening he cunningly drew the old roustabout out. <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,113</i>)

>  Shearers, washers, and half-grown louts, / Pickers-up, and "roustabouts" ... <i>(Jan 13, 1883,Otago Witness,13</i>)

### roustabout steward
 
 <i>n.</i> also called a patrol steward, one on a shift with no fixed work location who can be called to wherever needed [ca1989 Sep 11 Public address system of ferry `Arahura' Would the roustabout steward please call at the shop.]

### roustabouting
 
 <i>n.</i> = rouseabouting

>  The next two years were spent busking, labouring, casual electrical work, and roustabouting with Wirths Circus and as a member of a touring troupe, 'The Gaieties". <i>(Aug 2002,NZ Memories,37/6</i>)

### rouster
 
 <i>n.</i> [ca1938 espec. schoolboys'] (See [cf. poofter](../P#poofter))



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long-tailed sheep.

>  <b>SCURR</b> Rouster:- long-tailed sheep. <i>(2000,Rabbiters' Glossary,3</i>)

>  Rouster: a long-tailed sheep. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> anything excellent of its kind [ca1938 Havelock schoolchildren]

### roustie
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a general hand in a shearing shed or in a station work team.

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The rousties flew up and down the board while the table-hands tore at the necks and skirtings the moment the fleece was thrown. <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,258</i>)

### rousy
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rousie](../R#rousie))

### route
 
 <i>n.</i> special sense used in DOC's national track classification and familiar to trampers

>  <b>RHODES & CEDERMAN</b> A <i>route</i> is lightly marked or possibly defined only by natural features such as ridges and streams. <i>(1989,Wairarapa Walks,00</i>) [NOTES: check page]

### rover
 
 <i>n.</i> an extra man on the chain to help out when needed [(freezing workers')]

>  <b>COLIN GORDON</b> If you get behind [on your part of the chain] yell out for the rover, an extra man who [helps out at need] and keeps the chaps `up the chain'. He helps anybody, and will give you a cut up the chain, that is, do jobs for you. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a multiskilled slaughterman.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> rover: that chain slaughterman who knows every cut and who therefore has the task of stepping in and helping where pressure of work has become heavy .. unless he shares such favours very fairly he will soon find the delegate asking the foreman to appoint a different rover. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,67</i>)

### row up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to tedder hay.

>  They hay is ready to be baled, usually within 24 hours - it doesn't need teddering or rowing up. <i>(Sep 24, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,Farming Today 7</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> ?=rouse [(? nonce ) [? possibly rowsing meant]]

>  <b>SATCHELL</b> [Of Maoris who were not keeping up a contracted log-supply] I'm really doing your work in rowing them up <i>(1902,Land of the Lost,17,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SMITHYMAN</b> The locution is strange to me. ... Is this Satchell's version of rousing or rousting? <i>(1971,BLANK ENTRY,213</i>) [NOTES: fn 2]

### Rowdies
 
 <i>n.</i> the Radical Association, or Rads; abusive [Political name for Wakefield's party] by Featherston : the `Rads' [Radicals] for Wakefield's party frequent : OED Orig American : in genl use 1st quote 1865 - and 2 following quotes of political rowdies [n pl (hist)]

>  `The Rowdies' and the `Rads' [radicals] <i>(1858,Wellington Election Songs,8</i>)

>  <b>BAILEY & ROTH</b> Show a bold front, the Rowdies are beneath us. <i>(1967,Shanties,25</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Wellington Independent 30 June in 1967 Bailey and Roth Shanties 25]

### rowdy hat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Hither come more of the shaggy-complexioned men with the rowdy hats; looking at them with awe and befitting respect. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,29</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The working settler commonly dressed in a blue woollen shirt or blouse, moleskin or cord trousers, boots, and the slouch felt hats known as `rowdy' (that is, backwoodsmen's) hats. Butler called them `exceedingly rowdy hats'. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,101</i>)

### rowdy, the
 
 <i>n.</i> the ready cash, money [obs.]

>  Mr Punch... abjures (?or abhors - check) all contact with "filthy lucre" but he will accept instead.. any amount of the following substitutes, that is to say - any amount of "the rhino", "the ready", "the stumpy", "the brads", "the tin", "the brass", "the dumps", "the rowdy", "the shiners", "the mopusses" (wherewith people buy diamonds and topazes). <i>(Dec 5, 1860,Taranaki Punch,8</i>)

### rowdy-dowdy
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  One of those rowdy-dowdy days on the Waipa, Midshipman Foljambe had a pretty narrow escape. <i>(Dec 21, 1912,NZ Free Lance,5</i>)

### rowdy-hatted
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>BUTL;ER</b> How shall I describe everything - the little townlet, ... the scattered wooden boxes of houses, with ragged roods of scrubby ground between them - tussocks of brown grass - the huge wide-leaved flax ... the healthy clear-complexioned men, shaggy-bearded, rowdy-hatted, and independent, .pictures of rude health and strength .. - the stores, supplying all heterogeneous commodities - <i>(Jan 27, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iii/28</i>)

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> It was practically over when the opposing witnesses ... [found] their old friend in the `rowdy' hat now attired in the wig and gown of the cross-examining counsel. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,44</i>) [NOTES: [c1860s]]

### rowi
 
 <i>n.</i> the South Island brown kiwi [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  All professional kiwi hunters, we had worked together before with the rowi, <i>Apteryx rowii</i>. <i>(Jul 5, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),29</i>)

>  Amongst the specimens of birds, there are skins of the rowi and kiwi. <i>(Jan 11, 1869,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  57. <i>Apteryx australis</i>, Shaw. Rowi, Big Kiwi. ... One of the former nesting places I examined near Lake Mapourika, on the West Coast. <i>(Sep 1885,NZ Journal of Science,505</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <i>Apteryx oweni</i> Gould. Little Grey Kiwi; Rowi. Three localities are reported in the South Island where this small species still occurs. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/82</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  Ornithologists have separated the brown kiwi into three subspecies: the North Island brown kiwi <i>Apteryx australis mantelli</i>; the South Island brown kiwi <i>A. a. australis</i>; and the Stewart Island brown kiwi <i>A. a. lawryi</i>. ... OTHER NAMES: Rowi, Tokoeka. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,38</i>)

>  They don't have the vice-like grip, steel-band arms and powerful piston legs Reay has developed ridding rowi (new Zealand's rarest kiwi) habitat of stoats. <i>(Jan 1, 2006,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

### Rowley's parakeet
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> PLATYCERCUS ROWLEYI. (ROWLEY'S PARRAKEET.) <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/147</i>)

### royal
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) a wharfie who got regularly selected for work by the foreman under the old `auction block' system (occas., by seamen, for a mate's `mate') [ca1963 p.c. J. Winchester, Wellington: boss's royal a waterfront term for a boss's stooge or pet]

>  Invariably the "royals" receive preference. These watersiders are well known to the stevedores. <i>(Nov 24, 1938,NZ Observer,7</i>)

>  Each foreman had his regulars, known as "bulls" or "royals" ... <i>(1993,Wharfie,p12</i>)

### royal albatross
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) royal albatross. <i>D. epomophora</i>, a huge black and white seabird breeding exclusively in the NZ region; of two races: northern and southern royal albatross. <i>D. e. sanfordi</i>, breeding on the Chatham Islands and Otago Peninsula, and southern <i>D. e. epomophora</i>, breeding on subantarctic islands.

>  While on a recent cruise, Commander Ayscough, of H.M.S. Ringdove, collected specimens of a number of birds, which he presented to the Christchurch Museum. These have now been placed on exhibition in the New Zealand room, and include a Royal albatross from Campbell Island, a pair of woodhens from Stewart Island, a paroquet and a ground lark from the Antipodes Islands, and a bush hawk from the Auckland Islands. <i>(Feb 7, 1900,Star, The,3</i>)

>  The Buller collection of butterflies and several cases of butterflies from the islands adjacent to New Guinea are now on view at the Museum. The new case of birds, which is now only wanting labels, comprises, amongst other things, an adult Royal albatross, two stages of the young in down of the wandering albatross, also a specimen of the shy albatross, and two or three others of the rarer kind. <i>(Jul 1, 1911,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  The arrival at the heads last year of ten Royal Albatross was regarded as an event of the first importance in local scientific circles. <i>(Sep 21, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,20</i>)

>  Harold is a royal albatross, not only an endangered species but a spectacular one. Dunedin has the good luck to have a royal albatross breeding colony on the end of its peninsula. <i>(Oct 2, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  A 5km wire-netted boardwalk provides easy access to observe hillsides of royal albatross, the birds calling and clacking beaks in greeting as their mates wheel and glide home each evening. <i>(Mar 18, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> ROYAL ALBATROSS <i>Diomedia epomophora Maori name</i>: Toroa. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,30</i>)

>  19. <i>Diomedia epomophora sanfordi</i> (Murphy, 1917) Northern Royal Albatross ... 19.b. <i>Diomedia epomophora epomophora</i> Lesson 1825 Southern Royal Albatross <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,60</i>)

>  19. <i>Diomedia epomophora sanfordi</i> (Murphy, 1917) Northern Royal Albatross ... 19.b. <i>Diomedia epomophora epomophora</i> Lesson 1825 Southern Royal Albatross <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,19</i>) [NOTES: 1980 edition]

### Royal Colonial Institute
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In New Zealand, alone of the self-governing colonies, there was no local Imperial Federation League. The Royal Colonial Institute had a tiny membership of the respectable rich. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,220</i>)

### royal fern
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Less threatening are the now widespread male ferns (<i>Dryopteris affinis</i> and <i>D. filixmas</i>), [80] royal fern (<i>Osmuda regalis</i>), which is well established in swampy areas of the northern North Island, and the ferny azolla (<i>Azolla pinnata</i>), which is common in slow-mowing freshwater in northern New Zealand. <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/79</i>)

>  Royal fern is well established in the Lower Waikato/Hauraki Plains area, reaching heights of up to 2.5 m, with no current options for eradication. <i>(Jul 2006,Climate Update, The (NIWA),Aquatic Biodiversity & Biosecurity</i>)

### Royal Gala
 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of dessert apple

>  Royal Gala: Developed in New Zealand from a red sport of the Gala in 1965, and named in honour of Queen Elizabeth II, this is our second largest volume variety. <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

>  <b>BATEMAN</b> Royal Gala... A redder sport of the Gala. <i>(1986,Illustrated Encycl. of NZ,45</i>)

>  ROYAL GALA. Discovered by Mr Ten Hove of Matamata in 1969. A red sport of the Gala variety. <i>(1991,New Zealand's Best Apple Recipes,12</i>)

### royal penguin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  One case contains birds only, including .. a furry baby royal penguin. <i>(May 24, 1906,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  In appearance and size somewhat similar, but belonging to a district genus is the Royal Penguin, in which the yellow margin of the crown is found only on the front portion of the head, and the beak is longer than in the other varieties of this genus. <i>(Apr 14, 1920,Grey River Argus,6</i>)

>  A muff made made by Mr. Liardet from the skin of a royal penguin, from the Macquarrie Islands, was sold at a large figure. <i>(Jan 8, 1890,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>15/401</b> <i>Eudyptes sclegeli,</i> `Royal Penguin.' ... The `Royals' leave the island in June, and return in October. <i>(1883,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Scott Macquarie Island)]

>  <i>Eudyptes chrysolophus</i>. (The Royal Penguin.) <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/71</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  The chief industry now [on Macquarie Island] is the boiling-down of the royal penguin (<i>E. schlegeli</i>). <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/563</i>)

>  <i>Royal Penguin Eudyptes chrysolophus</i> BRANDT, 1837 ... The royal penguin nests on Macquarie Island, in vast colonies ... <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,48</i>)

### royal soaks' express
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  More colourful.. is the "fruitiest" train of them all, running after hotel closing hours between Dunedin and Port Chalmers, and rejoicing in the soubriquet of the "Royal Soaks' Express". <i>(Jul 18, 1928,NZ Free Lance,35</i>)

### royal spoonbill.
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A communication was received from Mr. J. M'Donald, Acting Director of the Dominion Museum, stating that, from the description of the bird given by Mr. Leith, the bird was, without doubt, a specimen of the royal spoonbill (platalea regia). <i>(Jun 15, 1922,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  Two different kinds of ibis have turned up in the Dominion, the Australian white ibis and the glossy ibis, while the royal spoonbill of Australia has also been seen on four occasions, two of these being in the Wairarapa district. <i>(Jul 16, 1932,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  Oystercatchers, royal spoonbills, godwits and turnstones flock to the spit from late September to earfly April. <i>(May 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 63/13</i>)

>  <i>Kotuku-ngutupapa, Platalea melanorhyncha, royal spoonbill </i>. [1957/150 P. regia] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,175</i>)

>  <i>101. Platalea leucorodia regia </i> Gould, 1838 <i>Royal Spoonbill (Kotuku-ngutupapa) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,36,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Royal Spoonbill Platalea regia </i>GOULD, 1838 ... OTHER NAME: <i>Kotuku-ngutupapa </i>. ... Known to the Maori as kotuku-ngutupapa, the royal spoonbill must have reached New Zealand from Australia many times before European settlement. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,137</i>)

>  Could it be another kotuku? No, no. This bird flew with outstretched neck - a royal spoonbill. <i>(1987,MsCellany: Women Writers' Prose & Poetry,72</i>)

>  The following papers were read and discussed .."On the occurrence of the <i>Platalea Regia</i> (royal spoonbill) in New Zealand," by the President ... <i>(Jul 31, 1876,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  We were approaching Waverley, where, perched on flood-strewn logs, was the reason for our journey - royal spoonbills.... Flying with slow rhythmic sweeps of their wings, the royal spoonbills were the epitome of grace. <i>(Oct 2, 2004,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

### royaller
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Joe Devcich almost brought off a "royaller" in the last when Peranui ran third. <i>(1987,Racing for Gold,174</i>)

>  On the first Saturday I was out, I decided to make it a royaller. To make it a royaller....to have a really good time <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

>  NEA LAP, after being abused for her first day run, all but paid a royaller when she ran second to Pewa at Woodville on the second day. <i>(Dec 18, 1930,NZ Truth,14</i>)

>  The North Midland game was a royaller for Parker, who scored five tries, and then had to go off with an injury to his leg, which however is not serious. <i>(Oct 18, 1924,NZ Truth,11</i>)

### Roys Peak gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> a rare and brightly spotted gecko from Otago highlands. [<i>Hoplodactylus "Roys Peak"</i> aka Orange-spotted gecko]

>  The data collected during the three surveys of Roys Peak and outlined in this report provide only limited data on distribution. However, it appears Roys Peak geckos are an alpine gecko, as all specimens were found above 1000 m, and below this altitude common geckos <i>Hoplodactylus ` Southern Alps'</i> (Hitchmough 1997) were numerous. <i>(2001,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.doc.govt.nz/upload/documents/science-and-technical/casn344.pdf 2001 Tocher, M.D.; Marshall, LJ, 2001. Surveys for <i>Hoplodactylus</i> aff. <i>granulatus</i>, Roys Peak and Mt Alpha, Wanaka.]

>  The discovery of a lizard assumed to be Roys Peak gecko on Cluden Pastoral Lease is of national significance. This species has a threat status of Nationally Critical (Hitchmough, 2002). <i>(2002,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.linz.govt.nz/docs/crownproperty/high-country-leases/leaselist/cluden-crr-pt1.pdf Copied to site Feb 05]

>  Other reptiles present in the catchment include McCann's skinks, common skinks, cryptic skinks, Southern Alps geckos, common geckos, Cromwell Gorge geckos and Roys Peak geckos. <i>(2009,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://web.tarrasnz.com/Otago%20Regional%20Council/Lindis%20Catchment%20information%20sheet%20-%20Feb%2009.pdf]

### RSA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Returned Services' [formerly Soldiers'] Association

>  It was the Dawn Paraders. The R.S.A. men marching. Marching in honour of their fallen comrades. <i>(1987,Tall Half-Backs,34</i>)

>  New Zealand's social life for the old has been built around the RSAs, the TABs, the Cosmopolitan Clubs. <i>(Oct 25, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> It was a long concrete and corrugated iron building between the RSA and a yard of yellow and red agricultural machinery. <i>(2005,Watch of Gryphons,164</i>)

>  Some still gather later at an RSA or in a bar, remembering old battles when they were young men. <i>(Apr 25, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),8</i>)

>  And now the RSAs are emptying. <i>(Feb 1992,Metro,8</i>)

>  Wellington's infamous 'harbour breeze' battered me down to Ghuznee Street to the old RSA building. <i>(Dec 1991,Cuisine,92</i>)

>  I can only assume that your reporter has no knowledge of the position which the RSA holds in the local community. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,16</i>)

>  [Hdg] Support from R.S.A. <i>(Sep 21, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,23</i>)

### RSA badge
 
 <i>n.</i>
