>  There weren't too many of us from the S.A.S., but I know us and the R.T. (artillery that is) were getting stuck in. <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories III,140</i>) [NOTES: 1975 Mitcalfe `Black Cat' in 1975 NZ Short Stories III 140]

### RTD
 
 <i>abbr.</i> ready-to-drink [ORIGIN: acronym, Internet search Feb 2006: also used in Austral, Brazil, China, India, Taiwan, N Amer]

>  ...the ready-to-drink (RTD) market was behind a revitalisation of the spirits market, which had been flat for about 10 years... A greater variety of RTDs is also coming our way, and in many cases they will end up in the hands of young women. <i>(Apr 25, 2004,Sunday Times (NZ),D4</i>)

>  Driven by the ready-to-drink, or rtd sector, consumption has gone up from 2.45 million litres in 1955... <i>(Feb 4, 2006,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  The New Zealand RTD (ready-to-drink) market is heading towards three million cases... Independent Liquor's decision to replace its beer halls with RTD halls, and its development of many successful RTDs, has seen it break successfully into the Australian market. <i>(Nov 2000,Legends in Their Lifetime,11</i>)

>  But the loss has been offset, at least in part, by strong growth in sales of "ready to drink" (RTD) spirit mixes popular with young people.... "Nothing new that's come out has kicked off in the way RTDs have." <i>(Nov 21, 2000,Evening Post,2</i>)

### RU
 
 <i>abbr.</i>

>  [check quot] <i>(Jun 1958,NZ Timber Journal,59/2</i>)

### rua
 
 <i>n.</i> a storage pit for root vegetables, esp. kumara and potatoes (cf PIT n)

>  ... the object was very simply attained in the dry porous soil by the rua. This was a circular pit sunk in the ground 5 ft. or 6 ft. deep and about the same in diameter, narrowing in at the top and closed by a trap-door made of a wooden slab. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/21</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> D|ua, s. A house, vessel, &c. to contain stores. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,151</i>)

>  <b>H. WILLIAMS</b> In the afternoon shifted our quarters on account of the rain to a Rua where we were very comfortable by the aid of a good fire. <i>(Jun 11, 1831,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,181,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> It is like a person carrying a large back load of kumara to the rua (an underground store with a very small entrance to it) ... <i>(Oct 7, 1852,Journal,8/46</i>)

>  <b>T.S.GRACE</b> He very kindly took my Natives to his `rua' of potatoes and told them to take what were in it! <i>(Oct 23, 1863,Pioneer Missionary Among the Maoris, A,123,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> The kumera were dug ... then all hands set to work ... to ... carry off the crop to the storehouse or rua ... <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(xi) 171</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Maori word (used in North Island) for a pit, cave or hole. A place for storing roots, such as potatoes, etc. Formerly some of these rua had carved entrances. <i>(1898,Austral English,396/2</i>)

>  <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),54</i>) [NOTES: 1901 Grace Tales of a Dying Race in 1953 Short Stories 54]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Artificial caves or pits (rua) were excavated for the purpose of storing tubers as food. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,282</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads,286</i>)

>  ~ a. storage pit for edible roots. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,10</i>)

>  In the `ngahuru' he would turn up with a horse to haul the `kumara' to the rua [ed fn: Pit, in this case for storing kumara] and empty kits, sure that he would have the loan of the sledge at the end of the day to take some of the harvest home with him, - ... <i>(Oct 17, 1930,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/64</i>) [NOTES: Ngata]

>  <b>Andersen</b> rua, pit; hole; pit-store for provisions. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Maori Words in English]

>  rua: storage pit. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,1/34</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> Their chief quest was a pot hole which might be described as much like a Maori rua or potato pit. A soft seam of rock played on by the tumbling waters and some stones always on the grind and move, wore down and sideways till the top was smaller than the bottom and an arched roof was the result, and a small opening at the top received through the ages particles of gold. The heavy swirl and pressure of water was unable to twist the gold contents out of the pot hole, and so a store of gold collected in the bottom and might contain nuggets as well as dust. <i>(1952,Faring South,158</i>)

>  ~ n. Maori word for a storage pit for edible roots [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),624</i>)

>  rua ... n. Maori word for storage pit for edible roots [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),17</i>)

>  <b>JENKIN</b> At the south end of the beach [in Waipao Bay, Moturoa Isd] he found several rua (kumara pits) half washed away by the sea. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,56</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Apart from kumara, which usually had to be kept in the rua or storage pit, the pataka was used to store all the food which would be needed to eke out the meagre supplies which would be harvested over the lean winter months. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a variety of potato

>  I am a couch potato / A chip off the old block / Who sits upon the sofa / And flops around the clock. / My mother's a King Edward /My father is a Rua / My ancestry's bicultural / No wonder there's no cure... <i>(Jul 11, 2004,NZ Catholic,7</i>) [NOTES: "Iva Verse"]

>  <b>BRANDENBURG</b> Rua. This is a maincrop variety still gaining ground in Canterbury... Rua produces a large, coarse-leafed plant keeping up top growth well into the autumn. <i>(1980,Outdoor Vegetable Growing,111</i>)

>  The best potato buy would be the 10-kilogram handgrip paper bag of ruas. <i>(Aug 24, 1999,Dominion,9</i>)

>  <b>BENTLEY & FRASER</b> A chap growing spuds near Opua/ Sent a ton to a lady, to woo'er,/ Of ancient King Edwards,/ But what got her bedwards/ Were newer, and fewer, and Rua. <i>(1985,Grand Limerick Tour, A,No. 5</i>)

>  <b>HILL</b> As I lifted the third root - `Crikey, you wann try ruas instead.' <i>(1988,More from Moaville,40</i>)

>  Rua - a good all-purpose variety. <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Gardener,20</i>)

>  "Rua" is a selection from the cross 'Katahdin by Harford .... Rua may be a safer variety to grow where there is a risk of soils being wet in the autumn. <i>(1960,AJHR,H-34/3</i>) [NOTES: Check pagination]

>  She rightly gets stuck into the growers of the despicable Rua potatoes, and has some interesting things to say about our new approaches to roast dinners. <i>(Dec 18, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),105</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> Hence, when ... a large tangi or other party reduced his pataka or rua kumera contents, he ... made shift with fern-root, berries, or mamaku (tree fern) until the next year's crop came in. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,281</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The</i>) [NOTES: [spg RUAKUMARA]]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  These subterranean chambers [for protection from shell attack, etc] (ruas, or pits, the Maoris called them) were usually six feet deep; ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/52</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BELICH</b> ... Kawiti had independently invented the anti-artillery bunker. These pits, or rua as they were called by the Maoris, were underground compartments roofed with beams of timber. <i>Ibid.</i> 337 rua pit, anti-artillery bunker <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,52</i>)

### rua kumara
 
 <i>n.</i> [storage pit for kumara]

>  Replicas of ancient pataka, (carved food storage house), kauta (shelter for food preparation) and rua kumara (carved kumara storage pit) will be built using traditional Maori stone tools. <i>(Oct 25, 2003,Waikato Times,6</i>) [NOTES: carved kumara storage pit - sic]

### Rua, the
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) Porirua

>  As a newish lad to the "Rua", I was not surprised of people's perceptions as outlined in your article about Porirua's dangers. <i>(Jan 12, 2004,Dominion Post,4</i>)

### Ruaite
 
 <i>n.</i> a follower of Rua (Kenana) (Tuhoe prophet)

>  [Caption] `The rising generation of Ruaites' ... [to picture of Maori children at Maungapohatu]. <i>(Apr 23, 1908,Auckland Weekly News</i>) [NOTES: 1908 Apr 23 Auckland Weekly News in 1979 Binney et al Mihaia 56]

### ruanui
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a strain of New Zealand-bred perennial ryegrass (Lolium perenne). [ORIGIN: Maori, see Turner ELANZ p.171]

>  <b>LEVY</b> Ariki ryegrass will be the first away, followed shortly by Ruanui perennial ryegrass. <i>(1970,Grasslands of NZ,174</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> For primary forest burn..Ruanui perennial ryegrass 10lbs. <i>(1970,Grasslands of NZ,183</i>)

>  Ryegrass cultivars differ markedly in susceptibility. Ruanui and Ariki are often more severely infected [with blind seed disease] than manawa and Paroa, probably because the latter two cultivars flower later. <i>(Oct 1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>) [NOTES: No page number given in RNZEL (Bardsley)]

>  Establishment data from the experiment on direct-drilling Ruanui or Nui into existing swards indicate that Ruanui was not as suited to this method of introduction as the more vigorous Nui. <i>(Mar 1979,NZ Journal of Agriculture,57</i>)

>  One contributing factor I think has been a change from Ruanui to Nui in the seed mix. <i>(Jun 25, 1981,NZ Farmer,27</i>)

>  Ruanui has been popular overseas because it was available and cheap.. <i>(Oct 9, 1986,NZ Farmer,61</i>)

>  Ruanui - a general-purpose perennial reyegrass with peak growth in late spring-early summer, for use in permanent pasture. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,17</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [(skiers')]

 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>(1983,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: (W.J. Orsman)]

### Ruatangata Sandstone
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol defn.

>  The northern three quarters of the peninsular is underlain by undeformed, northeast-dipping, massive to crudely bedded glauconitic sandstone (Ruatangata Sandstone, Te Kuiti Group, Runangan-early Whaingaroan) known from drillholes to be over 129 m and possibly up to 300 m thick (Fig. 2; Ferrar 1925 p. 43). <i>(Aug 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,85/19</i>)

### ruatara
 
 <i>n.</i> tuatara (obs.)

>  Unu ngarara or ngarara burrows were frequently met with on the plains. ... The ngarara was darker in colour than the ruatara. They varied in size from two or three feet in length, and ten to twenty inches in girth ... <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/296</i>) [NOTES: (Stack)]

### Ruaumoko
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori god of earthquakes

>  Morey puts them in their place with the belly-rambling laughter of a Ruauamoko or a Tangaroa. <i>(Sep 18, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

>  In the darkness, Mo sees and hears spirits that don't know they are dead. Angus can't see them, but he knows the mine is the way to reach Ruamoko, god of earthquakes, volcanoes and the unborn child. <i>(Sep 29, 2003,Dominion Post,B9</i>) [NOTES: check spelling Ruamoko]

>  Rongopai Broughton introduces us to the ever-threatening and unpredictable Ruaumoko, god of earthquakes, who vents his wrath wherever geological faultlines are... <i>(Dec 3, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),36</i>)

>  ...the air of ominous portent builds to true catastrophe by way of a bass rumbling, which may be the awakening of Ruamoko, the god of volcanoes, earthquakes and the unborn child. <i>(Oct 5, 2003,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),25</i>)

>  He knows the mine is the way to reach Ruamoko, god of earthquakes, volcanoes and the unborn child. <i>(Dec 4, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### RUB
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Rich Urban Biker

>  Rosemary McLeod's interest in rebels.. raised some wry smiles among the hardcore motorcycling fratenity; the men (and women) who have always ridden large motorcycles but whose thunder has largely been stolen by recent arrivals on the scene. The so-called RUBs - ruch urban bikers. <i>(Mar 17, 2002,Sunday Star Times,A10</i>)

### rub noses
 
 <i>phr.</i> to hongi [a long-used descriptive Pakeha misconception of the Maori HONGI `salute by pressing the noses together; incorrectly called rub noses.' (Williams)]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> When the public confusion was a little over, Moodee Why and the hoary warrior rubbed noses as a token of reconciliation, but Matanghee refused this salutation and appeared sullen. <i>(1819,Letters & Journals,187,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>EARLE</b> <i>(1832,Narrative of a Nine Months Residence in NZ</i>) [NOTES: check]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... Moyterra [Moetara] as the Europeans called him came on board and rubbed Noses with Captain Crow and Oakes and greeted all the Men, ... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),29,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARSHAL</b> ...rubbed noses in token of amity <i>(1834,Narrative of Two Visits,165</i>)

>  <b>DARWIN</b> On coming near one of the huts I was much amused by seeing in due form the ceremony of rubbing, or, as it ought to be called, pressing noses. The women, on our first approach, began uttering something in a most dolorous voice, they then squatted themselves down and held up their faces; my companion standing over them, one after another, placed the bridge of his nose at right angles to theirs, and commenced pressing. <i>(1845,Voyage of the Beagle,Ch.XVIII (Everyman edn) 407,1860 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1835 Dec 23 in 1845 (1860) Darwin Voyage of Beagle]

>  <b>BOULTBEE</b> ... these people saluted us by rubbing noses. <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,71,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: See also 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 189]

>  Many of the old chiefs insist[ed] on rubbing noses with me <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/16/199</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> N.B. To rub noses is a mark of great esteem but the Mauries seem aware that we do not admire the custom and only Nose with us on peculiar occasions. <i>(1840,Journal,233,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HENRY WEEKES</b> Acquaintances on meeting ... rub noses <i>(1841,Journal,6</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> Such disgust is only surpassed when such a being condescends to ... rub noses with the white man. <i>(1844,Excursion in the Northern Island of NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Colenso Excurs. North Island in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 12]

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> Every native in the little villages greeted us, and one venerable old lady would cry over and rub noses with the teacher <i>(Mar 2, 1854,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1854 Mar 2 J.Matthews Journal in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 193]

>  Just before Mr. MacLean went away ..., the contending chiefs met and rubbed noses, so that no doubt Mr. MacLean will report in Auckland that all is right here ... <i>(Sep 2, 1857,Lyttelton Times,5</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> Here we stopped, and the canoes were drawn up in front of the pa, amidst much tangi-ing, and shouts of `Haeremai, haeremai, haeremai!' - the usual amount of shaking hands and rubbing noses being over, a long korero, or talk, ensued. <i>(1866,Chatham Islands,17</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> After the recital of this, they then saluted by rubbing noses (hongi), as with the Maori, and wailed over their losses. <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham Islands, The,5</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> ... they [old Maori women] rubbed noses in the old Maori fashion instead. I couldn't have stood too much of that, though; my nose would have got raw. I didn't need to look in a mirror to know it was getting shiny. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,24</i>)

>  <b>CORBALLIS & GARRETT</b> Further changes were made before the first `Tangi' (in Contemporary Maori Writing) became the definitive version (in Pounamu, Pounamu). ... The characters `rub' noses in the earlier version; they `press' them in the later one. ... [24] Finally, in three passages (pp. 126, 130) - but not in a fourth (p. 128) - the `pa' becomes a `meeting house'. ... (`Pa' tends to have pejorative associations in Pakeha parlance, ...) <i>(1984,Witi Ihimaera,23</i>)

>  I was introduced to him and metaphorically rubbed noses with him on behalf of the Race. <i>(Mar 11, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/66</i>) [NOTES: Buck]

>  <b>LINKLATER</b> This old lady cannot speak English, but she has asked me to say that she would like to press noses with you. <i>(1953,Year of Space, A,201,1953 ed.</i>)

>  ... after the speeches were completed an invitation was made to rub noses, shake hands and have kai. <i>(Nov 12, 1988,Dominion,3</i>)

>  <b>Campbell</b> Had we been Maoris of course we should there and then have also squatted down and rubbed noses. Hereafter I will explain why at such meetings the natives do not jump up and say how glad they are to see you, but welcome you in woe-begone `Haere mai's', squatting down and rubbing the lugubrious nose until the spectacle is anything but pleasant to look at. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk3/2/142</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>phr.</i> transferred uses: see citations

>  <b>BAKER</b> In modern colloquial speech we find that to rub noses is used loosely for (a) to greet (a person), (b) to kiss (someone), (c) to plunge into a private conversation with heads close together. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,21</i>)

>  <b>Johnson</b> The hilltop view.. Allows you to rub noses with [get at close quarters with] a natural arrangement of the common trees and shrubs. <i>(1997,Pick of the Bunch,153</i>) [NOTES: cf. rub shoulders with]

### rub-a-dub
 
 <i>n.</i> the drug, ascetic anhydride

>  A large number of the terms gathered from the prison were to do with drugs and devices and ways of taking them... Some interesting ones include:.. <i>rub-a-dub</i> (used occasionally to refer to ascetic anhydride, an agent added to Class B drugs, for example, to turn them into Class A drugs, for example, heroin). <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2) : 2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a rub, a massage

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> `What's the matter with you bums, anyway? My rub-down not classy enough for you anymore? ... I mean is Carlton ready for a health studio, mate?' ... `Right. Got to give them something they think they are missing. What about you, Les? You'd be in for a bit of the ah ... [sic] other, the high-class rubadub, wouldn't you?' <i>(1979,Skin Deep,18</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the pub [rubbity-dub ca1950 Vic. O'Leary Common in Hawkes Bay. 1981 Auckland Savings Bank, Queen Street (Goldie Brown Collection) Rub-a-dub: hotel, pub] (See [rubbity-dub](../R#rubbity-dub))

>  The genial Sandy / Serves out the shandy / And keeps up the `rep' of the `rub' <i>(Jun 23, 1906,NZ Truth,53/3</i>)

>  I gazed upon the motley crowd / Within this `rub-a-dub' <i>(1926,Transport Workers' Song Book</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> unlovely; in poor repair; disreputable [(?esp. by adults), ca1950 p.c. Ken Griffiths (Auckland)]

### run
 
 <i>adj.</i> [one ref to Austral Eng]

>  <i>(1898,Austral English</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> sc of gold [goldmining]

>  The different runs of gold .... have not been traced further than Wetherstone's Flat. <i>Ibid.</i> D/6/18 The gold is more dispersed, so as not form any distinct run ... <i>(1863,AJHR,D/6/15</i>) [NOTES: (Pyke Goldfields Reports)]

>  The greater part of Bluebottle Terrace has not been subjected, to sufficient reconcentration to render the drifts payable `runs' of gold may yet be found on or near the Blue Bottom. <i>(1906,NZ Geol. Survey Bulletin,No.1 (N.S.): 93</i>) [NOTES: (Bell-Hokitika)]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> There was a `run' of gold somewhere in the head of the valley but it had not been satisfactorily plotted. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,41,1963 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> grazing land [also cattle run, sheep run Acland distinguished six uses of the noun, because of change or extension of the meaning of the original borrowing; run off, (Farming) land which is not part of a developed farm but which is used for intermittent grazing.] (See [also cattle run](../A#also-cattle-run),[sheep run](../S#sheep-run))

>  FOR SALE, a Small Farm, part of which is heavily timbered, convenient to a shipping place, with plenty of water, and a good run for cattle. <i>(Apr 23, 1864,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  Run.- The pioneers of sheep farming in New Zealand and Australia came from various classes; but there were few among them who had been sheep farmers before and fewer still who were scientists. If they had been sheep farmers they would probably have used the English or Scotch sheep farming words (as they did to some extent) and fitted them to the new conditions. If they had been scientists they would have invented exact names for everything new, and translated them into Latin. What they did do was to take the handiest word they could think of and give it a new meaning, As time went on the word they had chosen changed or extended its meaning. The best example of all this is the word run. <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: >Acland's senses 1 and 5 seem to be the central ones, with the others being specialisations of, or obvious inferences from those. -->cf also Morris]

>  [Cont] (1) The country held under a particular lease or pastoral license. [sic] (PAUL, p. 92) (2) Group of rs. held by one owner, or in one station. The whole area is spoken of as a r. (C.RH., p. 90) (3) Leasehold as opposed to freehold. (Sheep returns, 1878) (4) Open as opposed to fenced country. `Out on the r.'; i.e., not in any of the paddocks. ([1874 Lady Barker Station Life], p. 62) (5) Adjective. R. sheep, r. cattle, as opposed to paddock sheep or milking cows and hand-reared calves. (C.) (6) As equivalent to station: e.g. `He has sold his r.'; `One of the best rs. in Canterbury.' In this sense r. did not always include the sheep, as the word s[tation]. does. This usage is now uncommon and r. in these contexts would probably mean small grazing r. ([1874 Kennaway Crusts], p. 86;Butler A First Year in Canterbury Settlement],p. 152) (7) A block of country, a division of a r. E.g. ewe r. upper r. This sense was always rare, but is still in use on some ss. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) (8) To keep or carry. `He runs merinos'; `the station runs 10,000 sheep' ([1898 Morris Austral-English]; C) <i>(Nov 15, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Run, n. (1) Tract of land over which sheep or cattle may graze. It is curious that what in England is called a sheep-walk, in Australia is a sheep-run. In the Western United States it is a sheep -ranch. Originally the squatter, or sheep-farmer, did not own the land. It was unfenced, and he simply had the right of grazing or `running' his sheep or cattle on it. Subsequently, in many cases, he purchased the freehold, and the word is now applied to a large station- property, fenced or unfenced. (See quotation, 1883.) [1826] <i>(1898,Austral English,397</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> First a run is a parcel of land as leased. A sheepfarmer might own several adjacent runs of land, in this sense, and the whole `station' so formed might also be termed a run. In the third sense a run was leasehold as opposed to freehold land; in a fourth sense, open land as opposed to fenced land. It might be used as an adjective, e.g. run cattle. It might be the equivalent of station but without including the sheep, i.e. `He sold his run' need not imply that he sold his sheep, but `He sold his station' would. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,50</i>)

>  run: [1] This word has several uses but the most common refers to a stretch of work. The official shearing time of two hours is called a `run'. The day is divided into four of these. 7.30am-9.30am, smoke-o; 10.00am-12 noon, dinner; 1.00pm-3.00pm smoke-o; 3.30pm-5.30pm, tea. [2] Also another name for a sheep station. [3] Also the contract circuit. A shearing contractor will arrange a `run' of sheds for his gang each season. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/27</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

>  It is bounded by the Western Coast where there is some good Land, and dry run for Sheep. <i>(Jun 19, 1840,New Zealand Advertiser and Bay of Islands Gazette,3</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [sheep farming, one cit]

>  The station runs 10,000 sheep. <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]



 
 <i>adj.</i> aj or attrib ?or combin. esp. run country, run hunting, run licence, run sheep see also run book, run cattle, run cow, runholder, runholding

>  Lambs ... while ewes are being shorn ... are being wigged or eye-clipped ... Through eye clipping, wool blindness is avoided; a wool-blind sheep is seriously hampered on run country in its search for feed and consequently does not thrive. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,349</i>)

>  <b>THOMAS</b> He proceeded to raise cattle on a large area of leasehold run country, mostly north of Hukatere. <i>(1970,Way Up North,63</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [unclear; one cit]

>  ... the defendant conductor, who has a wife and four children (youngest a week old) was alleged to be running yet another girl besides the complainant. <i>(Mar 13, 1909,NZ Truth,195/5/1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs and their controllers] to work.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. the musterer freqently gets his team to bark while at heel (i.e. alongside him) the object being to frighten sheep along without actually running a dog. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,185</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> If you do run him in a paddock he should never be allowed to get too close to his sheep as this only makes the sheep over exert themselves ... <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,39</i>)

>  <b>DREAVER</b> I was there in the days before major development started on the tussock, and the blocks were large and it seemed you could run a dog for miles. <i>(1994,Catlins River Wisp Run,57</i>)

>  <b>KAY</b> As young men, Anthony II's sons shared a great love of country, for its bush, birds, animals, shooting, horse riding, running sheep dogs, or just the freedom and the challenge of ownership. <i>(1996,Anthony Wall, Settler of Porirua,168</i>)

>  Waipukurau Stock Sale: Well bred dogs ready to run. <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,30</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheep or cattle] to move together in a string or mob in one direction.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. sheep don't run as they used to - and this militates against clean mustering. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,121</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [as a general catchphrase, heard on English TV; no citations]



 
 <i>v.</i> [to run past, meaning to check with]

>  <b>McGILL</b> run past someone present work for assessment or opinion; eg `I'd like to run these figures past you one more time, okay?' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,95</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a liberty-leave [(? nautical)]

>  <b>DILKE</b> Even sailors on shore `for a run' here wear cord breeches and high tight-fitting boots, often armed with spurs, though, as there are no horses except those of the Gold-Coast Police, they cannot enjoy much riding. <i>(1868,Greater Britain,1/335</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to manage or own a property.

>  <b>TAIT</b> After his death, it was run by managers ... <i>(1961,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol III,153</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> In earlier days Orongorongo was run in with Tora Station. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,7</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the effective length of the saw less the diameter or width of the trunk or log being sawn [tree felling]

>  <b>MOORE</b> I decide to put in a `side-scarf' to give a better `run' for the saw, even though this is a 9-foot one ... <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,89</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [farmer or land] to graze or carry stock.

>  He further maintained that the defendant, holding a license [sic] to run cattle on the Dunedin Hundred, his bull running on that Hundred could not be held to be straying or running at large, and that the Wardens of the Hundred having the power under the Land Regulations of determining the description of cattle to be allowed to run within the Hundred, the Entire Animals Ordinance was inoperative within Hundreds, except so far as the Wardens adopted it as one of their regulations. <i>(May 15, 1858,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  The person, or in the case of a lease to two or more persons holding or occupying any land within a district, under or by virtue of any grant from the Crown, or any mining, mineral, or agricultural lease, shall be entitled to run three head of great cattle free of charge, provided that in the case of a Crown Grant, or a lease to two or more persons jointly, the number of cattle which they shall be together entitled to run shall be no more than three head. <i>(Mar 17, 1870,Tuapeka Times,5</i>)

>  Mr. Robertson hoped that applications would only be granted to those who were really entitled to run cattle on the goldfields. <i>(Sep 5, 1868,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  At the present partial law allows the Maori to prohibit all cattle from running on his land, a [sic] meeting of the Europeans and Maoris was held here some time since, at which the latter claimed, and the former, without much demur, agreed to pay, 5s. per head annually for such of their cattle as should run on the Maori land. <i>(Oct 22, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  The Mayor informed the members that Mr Jas. Bayly had waited on him, in company with Cr. Klenner, in reference to the recreation reserve, and stated that permission had been given him by Mr Knight to run cattle in the reserve, Mr Knight at that time being a member of the old Town Board, and also a member of the Reserves Committee. <i>(Feb 11, 1905,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  "..Taking the majority of farms here, a certain proportion of cattle are run with the sheep with the object of keeping down the rank or coarser grasses, so that it would be an easy matter to test whether some such system as the following would not give such good results ..." <i>(Aug 19, 1908,Tuapeka Times,4</i>)

>  A number of the farmers have lately taken to sheep-feeding, and I suppose they are not disposed to substitute at present cows for sheep; yet a combination of cattle and sheep is better for the land, it being advisable to give land which has been much run with sheep a rest for a while from stock of that description and run cattle on it exclusively. <i>(Aug 30, 1894,Otago Witness,23</i>)

>  The ewes can then be boxed up again and run in convenient sized mobs. <i>(Mar 1918,NZ Farmer,271</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> The dry cattle were run further out than the sheep. <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,241</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> The word might be used as a transitive verb. 'He runs Merinos' i.e. has Merino sheep as his stock. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,iii 50</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> Run in this manner, the sheep fatten quickly ... <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,122</i>)

>  <b>FEARON</b> In 1956 Dunolly was running 2,100 sheep and 280 cows, but by 1965 when it was put on the market, it was running 4,100 sheep and 370 cows. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,40</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [sheep or cattle] to graze.

>  We have 36 head of cattle running this winter on the back bush land. <i>(Aug 8, 1854,BLANK ENTRY,154</i>) [NOTES: in Scholefield (1960) Richmond-Atkinson Papers I]

>  <b>DEANS</b> The hardest day I can recall was about 1901 when the lambs from 3,000 Merino ewes running on the Bush Gully block had to be marked in one day. <i>(1964,Pioneers on Port Cooper Plains,114</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> At the time of my visit the hoggets were running on one of these new blocks ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,164</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> .. stock run on less productive land make a contribution toward overhead costs ... <i>(1989,BLANK ENTRY,122</i>) [NOTES: Economic Evaluation of Agroforestry in Otago]



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a course followed by a dog in a dog trial competition.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Run: ... Also a dog's performance during a particular job is referred to as his `run', i.e. he puts up a good run or otherwise. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,189</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> But for the fact that that his outrun was excessively wide he put up a good run ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,195</i>)

>  The sheep must be removed if a competitor's dog has made contact with them and the run is subsequently terminated for any reason. <i>(1991,Constitution of NZ Dog Trial Assoc,31</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> I had long decided being pedantic over poor runs was a waste of judging time; anyone scoring 85 or under these days has no show. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,159</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> [AND used elsewhere but recorded earliest in Aust. and apparently chiefly Aust. OED(S v. 43. c.: 1. trans to provide pasture for (sheep, cattle); to raise stock (also absol) 1795, 1828 2. v.i. of stock, to graze 3. of land 1. to provide sustenance for animals] (See [run n](../R#run-n))

>  (8) To keep or carry. "He runs merinos"; "The station runs 10,000 sheep." (AE.;C). <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss]

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Asked 80 acres and back hill ... to run sheep and cattle upon <i>(1848,Journal,169</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Under the original terms, land purchasers were entitled upon payment of an annual license fee of 10s. 6d. to depasture or run sheep and cattle upon such waste lands within the block as remained unappropriated. ... though Captain Cargill was then gazetted Commissioner of Crown Lands for the Otago block only, he received no definite instructions upon the subject of granting the runs. [139] The result was that he merely filed the applications for depasturage licenses as they were presented to him, whilst the applicants were left to pursue their further course as seemed best to them. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,138</i>) [NOTES: 1890 J.C.H.Chapman in 1898 Hocken Contributions 138-139]

>  <b>NEAVE</b> I have some recollections of my uncles only running about one thousand, two hundred sheep on the entire block. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,72</i>)

>  run v. ... NZ & Austral. have (specified livestock) on one's property (run cattle, sheep, etc.); <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,668</i>)

>  By 1850 Flaxbourne was running 11,000 Merion sheep. <i>(2008,NZ Memories,73/51</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a period in which shearers shear sheep between intervals [ measured in hours or in numbers of sheep.]

>  Run .. (3) Stretch of work. Shearers work for an hour before breakfast .. <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Run. The shearing time between official stops, smokos or meals. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,157</i>)

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Each [catching pen] pen should be large enough to hold a normal "run", eg, for a two hours' run, 30 sheep. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,353</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> in specific uses, chiefly station or farming

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> ... the record shearing run of 19 nothing 9 <i>(1909,Farm & Station Verse,52</i>) [NOTES: 1909 Geo. Meek. `Wet Sheep' in Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse 52]

>  Run.- ... 3. Stretch of work [shearing]. Shearers work for an hour before breakfast, two stretches in the morning and three in the afternoon. The stretches are divided by meal-times and smokos (q.v.). (C.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> No one knows better than I how a bad run can tend to get a man down - a run of sticky sheep, or gear not cutting the best, ... <13> A shearer should not start an early morning run without a cup of tea ... 157 RUN. The shearing time worked between official stops, smokos, or meals. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,7</i>)

>  Tally: The number of sheep shorn during a run or other specified period. However, the general term is also used to describe a shearer's capacity to shear in a day. E.g. `He has a tally of 120.' <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: H.A.L. Laing]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ... actual shearing time is probably nine hours, with `spells' (breaks) between six typical `runs' totalling three hours. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/229</i>)

>  Run:The period of shearing between breaks for `smoko' and meals. <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. Laing)]

>  <b>MOORE</b> The men work in `runs' none of which exceeds one and three-quarters hours - a work day totalling eight and three-quarter hours. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,84</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Andrew was troubled about the [shearing] gang's knocking off ... He had enough sheep in for another run. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,64</i>)

>  RUN: The period of shearing between breaks for `smoko' or meals. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>) [NOTES: [Shearer's language]]

>  Run: Interval of time between breaks when shearing. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,58</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Sometimes several combs and a dozen cutters may be used, or blunted, during a `run'. . <i>Ibid.</i> 94 The following day, Jim (later Sir Eruera [Tirikatene]) became my `barrow-boy' and washed and dried my used gear each run for the privilege of shearing the first sheep. <i>Ibid.</i> 113 A steam whistle ... blew for the commencement and finish of each run. The wool tables for rolling and skirting were in the centre of the woolboard. Close by stood the classer's table, where he sat and classed each fleece before it was carried by a shed hand to its respective bin. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,72</i>)

>  My first `run' (the period of work between breaks for tucker) went by in a blur. <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,30</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Run. The area held as a station; includes the area under pastoral lease. Also refers to a period of work; the shearers divide the working periods of their days into runs. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,97</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> various periods of work: 8-10am etc



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) [shearing or crutching gangs] a circuit or number of woolsheds to work. (See [crutching run](../C#crutching-run))

>  When a man [shearer] made arrangements to go to a `run' of two or three sheds he only deposited 20s, which carried him through the run. <i>(May 26, 1911,Maoriland Worker,12/11/4</i>)

>  <b>SMITH</b> As an inducement for the men to work for them [shearing contractors] arrange a `run of sheds' to follow one another. <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,72</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> Each group of Maoris developed its own `run', or succession of woolsheds in which the group did shearing as of right ... <i>(1962,Country Occasions,36</i>)

>  .. focusing instead on rugby and taking a variety of jobs - including a crutching run - to support himself. <i>(Jun 9, 2006,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,10</i>)

>  <b>RUSSELL</b> This year I have Maori shearers, Taranaki Te Ua engaged them for me and they do his shed, the Tuna Nui shed and Hill at Fernhill, which gives them a good run. <i>(1904,Diary,136</i>) [NOTES: in Hill (1990) Between the Rivers]

>  <b>WATERSON</b> When he heard that this Steve fellow was a top shearer and could use another hand when his run started up, Wart became quite interested. <i>(1999,Some Buggers I've Seen,24</i>)

>  Quality shearers and shedhands required for large 2nd shear run. <i>(Jun 22, 2002,Hawke's Bay Today,27</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [farming; run licence, run-holder]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Pastoral lands are the wild grazing lands ... leased ... for a term of years under a deed termed a `run licence,' to the emigrant who, embarking on pastoral pursuits, becomes what is indifferently termed a run-holder, sheep-farmer, flock-master, or Squatter. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/512</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [farming; one cit: run-sale]

>  ... 155,500 acres of similar land, the temporary licenses of which expire in 1901; and 22,918 acres of pastoral land passed in at the last run-sale. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,30</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a usual beat or haunt (?on a free range) [[move forward to ?@n4 if admit] cf RUN @v4 cf also PIG-RUN (?does fowl-run belong here]]

>  My new horse got through the fence yesterday and has gone full bare [?=bore] back to his old run. <i>(Sep 22, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,104,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [farming; one cit: runseekers]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Next day the two run-seekers [in Otago Central in 1859] met her husband coming with the cattle. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,26</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to send a dog out (See [cf also running out](../C#cf-also-running-out))

>  <b>15</b> Run.- (1) To send [a dog]. (2) Work [of a young dog]. `That dog is just beginning to r.' 3. Stretch of work [shearing]. Shearers work for an hour before breakfast, two stretches in te morning and three in the afternoon. The stretches are divided by meal-times and smokos (q.v.). (C.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  `Never run a dog unless you must; never run a dog behind you.' <i>Ibid.</i>34 It was wise to start them from a distance, because if started suddenly they were likely to scatter, and that meant running a dog. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,24</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large grazing or pastoral property. [rural]

>  <b>PAUL</b> The occupiers of runs will also take charge of cattle ... <i>(1853,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,21</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> In New Zealand a well-bred merino flock, on a good run, will fully average 4 lbs. <i>(1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,40</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> The dwelling-house is generally termed the 'station:' while the land, the pasture of which is rented from the Government, is termed the 'run.' <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,147</i>)

>  <b>MCKEE WRIGHT</b> .. the run is broken up for hungry cockatoos ... <i>(1898,Station Ballads,46</i>)

>  Two-thirds of this number are on small grazing-runs .. The remainder are on freehold runs .. The runs of the Ashburton County lie behind, and are a modified extension of, a belt of brown-top country.. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,99</i>)

>  A case is known where all sheep were wintered on the run, and the death-rate was 20 per cent. to 30 per cent. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,103</i>)

>  Stores are brought down off the hill runs to be finished off on the luscious paspalum-white clover pastures on the flats. <i>(Jan 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,15</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> Te Aratipi was originally the outstation of Mrs G P Donnelly's Waimarama run. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,41</i>)

>  .. this run is still regarded as being good, although isolated summer country ... <i>(Sep 28, 1961,NZ Farmer,16</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> He portrays an "ideal" run as representing extensive use of land, located in a remote area at high elevation with steep and rugged terrain, with harsh winters, short growing seasons, shallow soil and little pasture development. Bob Hall .. differentiates a sheep station from a run ... <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,139</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> on pasture

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> (Letter from Nelson) We have about 1400 [sheep] running in the Upper Motueka Valley <i>Ibid.</i> 310 On the site of Dunedin ... a Mr.Anderson had, in April 1847, 300 sheep and 50 head of cattle running. <i>(1847,Handbook for New Zealand, The,149</i>)

>  We have 36 head of cattle running this winter on the back bush land.... The bush feed keeps the cattle in fine order in winter time. -->1865 Dec 1 in 1914 History of North Otago (1978) 177 Mr. Holmes' sheep are running on native grass and the above increase has been obtained at one lambing. <i>(Aug 8, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/154,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W.Richmond)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [slang for dismissal]

>  <b>BAKER</b> to give a person his running shoes ... an extension of the phrase to get the run, to be dimissed or fired - <i>(1941,NZ Slang,53</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to have a free range on a place, pasture etc cf RUN @n8

>  All persons are hereby cautioned against disturbing the Horses now running in the neighbourhood of Rangiora, or driving any of them without permission of J.H. WARD, Rangiora. <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> ?? run=station, station=run in some early usage cf 8copied from 1a)

>  He also reports having sold by private contract, on account of Messrs Miles and Co., Christchurch the Lake Heron Run, with the sheep thereon, to Messrs Dunbar Bros. <i>(Apr 8, 1881,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> The first care of the intending sheep-farmer will naturally be to select a `run' or sheep-walk. ... But if the selection of the station or `run' is important ... <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,6</i>)

>  As to the preemptive right, please send me the best description you can of what we should apply for. We have a right to `80 acres at one of the stations and 10 acres at each of the other stations erected upon the run' ... It seems, however, that there must be a station of some sort at the place where preemptive is applied for; but it would be easy to have a hut put up at any place where we wanted to take our land. <i>(Apr 20, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/37,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> Fuel is ... very expensive, as we have no available `bush' on the Run; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 73 Our friend . owns a large station ... At the back [74] of his run the hills rise ro a great height .. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,62,1870 ed.</i>)

>  n: an extensive grazing property usually in South Island tussock country <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> `range' ?without reference to leasing or licensing an area (=STATION in sense of total run)

>  run n. a range of pasture for feeding stock <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,10</i>)

>  run A large area of land on which sheep or cattle, or both, are grazed; in America a `ranch' <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),349,8th edn ed.</i>)

>  run n. grazing area for sheep or cattle; <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),624</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The areas shown as `high country' runs or stations vary in size from 2,000 to 40,000 hectares and are used for grazing sheep. When these stations were first taken up ... There were no fences on these early high country runs, ... . <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,8</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> leasehold as distinct from freehold

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> Thirty acres of average available land may be found with many miles of run for the cattle. <i>(1831,Journal and Letters (Walker),1/50</i>)

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> ... looking out for a run upon the coast. <i>(1846,Journal,19</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Another advantage is to be found in the absence of burrs in the New Zealand runs; [fn] in New South Wales these are found to tear the wool, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 169 The custom has been, like that of New South Wales, to let the cattle ... rove at large over a tract of country, generally called a run. ... When wanted to be branded ... they are driven by men on horseback, armed with long whips, into a strong fold, called a stock-yard. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,123</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [farming, unspecified]

>  ... give us possession of a new run for a period of years. <i>(Sep 5, 1849,Deans' Letters,155,1937 ed.</i>)

>  [The cattle] are always in the open air, or, as they would say in New Zealand, `on the run' <i>(1849,Handbook of New Zealand,24</i>) [NOTES: 1849 Allom in Earp's NZ Handbook (Notes on NZ 1849) 24]

>  <b>DILLON</b> The run which [the sheep] are on is magnificent <i>(1850,Letters,295</i>)

>  ... splendid sheep runs <i>(Jan 9, 1851,Journal of Edward Ward,107,1951 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SAM STEPHENS</b> Each owner rents or has a licnce for a `Run', as it is called, from 5 to 50,000 acres. <i>(1851,Journal,504</i>)

>  <b>WELD</b> I consider double that sum as the most favourable amount that can be invested in the formation of a sheep-station; for on a good run, 2000 sheep can be kept ... <i>Ibid.</i> 6 The first care of the intending sheep-farmer will naturally be to select a `run' or sheep-walk. ... But if the selection of the station or `run' is important ... <i>(1851,Hints to Intending Sheep-Farmers in New Zealand,5</i>)

>  I have secured the prettiest spot and the best run in the whole district. <i>(Feb 26, 1851,Torlesse Papers,1958,200 ed.</i>)

>  <b>H. TOPELL</b> Here it all runs upon Stations - Sheep runs - good and bad Country - Cattle and Stock. <i>(1853,Journal,1/312,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ROCHFORT</b> [The Station owner] has some excellent paddocks fenced in. The run is bounded on one side by the Turakina river, on the other three by a good post-and-rail fence. The sheep require but little looking after, as they cannot get over the boundary. <i>(1853,Adventures of a Surveyor,25</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> February, 1855. Run 39. 35,000 acres <i>(Feb 1954,Te Waimate,45</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Feb in 1940 Studholme Te Waimate (1954) 45]

>  WANTED TO PURCHASE The Good Will of a RUN inside the Canterbury Province, with or without from 2000 to 3000 Ewes. <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Stayed with [Tripp] some days to see the working of the run, a mountain tract of 40,000 acres, scarcely trodden as yet by foot of man. <i>(Sep 1, 1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,21,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> ... it will be as well to mention the way in which a sheep-farmer settles himself on his sheep-walk, or `run', as it is called ... <i>(1858,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> The dwelling-house is generally termed the `Station'; while the land, the pasture of which is rented from the Government, is termed the `Run' ... The term [`station'] is often applied collectively to represent the whole occupation. <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,147</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Thus, a run of 20,000 acres will be worth 2,000l. <i>(Feb 10, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,iv/41</i>)

>  <b>2/54</b> When two or three adjoining runs are in one man's holding he must have as many flocks and establishments as runs to fulfil the exact letter of the regulations, which require stock to be on every run during the whole term of the licensee .... [sic] <i>(Jul 3, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1960</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> I go out on the run, bring in the first mob of sheep I come across, and select one that is not too fat, generally a merino if I can find one. Most of the crossbreds would have [42] about an inch thick of fat on the ribs. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,41,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> On the high runs, the snow is lying very deep, and a large number of sheep are snowed up in them, ... <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,56</i>) [NOTES: 1877 May William Shirres Copybook in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 56]

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Under the original terms, land purchasers were entitled upon payment of an annual license fee of 10s. 6d. to depasture or run sheep and cattle upon such waste lands within the block as remained unappropriated. ... though Captain Cargill was then gazetted Commissioner of Crown Lands for the Otago block only, he received no definite instructions upon the subject of granting the runs. [139] The result was that he merely filed the applications for depasturage licenses as they were presented to him, whilst the applicants were left to pursue their further course as seemed best to them. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,138</i>)

>  The stock on these high-country farms may graze in altitudes as high as about 1500 to 1800 metres, though most of the `runs' utilise areas up to about 900 m only <i>(1974,NZ Agriculture,57</i>)

>  run ... 61. (esp. in Australia and New Zealand) a tract of land for grazing livestock. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1010</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 668; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 426]]

>  <b>BREMNER</b> Run large area needed for early sheepfarming, often held on Crown Pastoral Lease before land was available for freehold. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)

>  I do not know the number of horses and oxen on the run. They roam over miles of country and are as wild as if they had never seen a man and many very savage. <i>(Jan 22, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,26</i>)

>  Draper ... and myself were on the run all day cutting out bullocks we succeeded in yarding about 60. It is very hard to cut out so many; ... When they are hot and savage they do not care for the stockwhip. <i>(Mar 13, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,28</i>)

>  The Government intends cutting up the Ormaglade run, in the Teviot district, of 28,000 acres, into four small grazing runs of 7000 acres each. <i>(Apr 3, 1908,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> [3. (does following definition apply here, or with cits go to 1 above: an extensive grazing property (usu. in South Island tussock country))]

>  <b>LADY BARKER</b> We have no available bush on the Run. <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,62,1870 ed.</i>)

>  They [friends] have been abusing me for not coming to stay with them ever since they started the run [Castle Hill]. <i>(Jun 21, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,191,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SWANTON</b> We now leave the smaller class of sheep farm and come to the large `runs' or `stations', as they are called. <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,93</i>)

>  <b>MULGAN</b> Mark felt a real sympathy for some of the men and women out back in the high country - where runs ran right up to the glaciers <i>(1934,Spur of Morning,236</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> It is usually understood that in the early days, the word `station' [qv.] denoted the owner's house and its immediate buildings, while `run' meant the whole property .... <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,115</i>)

>  run: station property on which sheep and cattle are grazed. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> [gloss.] Run: a large grazing property. <i>(1947,Wayleggo</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> The run was then taken up by Mrs. Margaret Preston ... of Longlands Station, ... and went with that station to Joseph Preston who held it until 1938 when it was divided. <i>(1948,Run, Estate and Farm,65</i>)

>  <b>McLAGLAN</b> A distant connection of mine had a run in a lonely place touching the upper reaches of the Wanganui River. <i>(1965,Stethoscope & Saddlebags,184</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Run: A large grazing property. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,189</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The back country runs have quite a vocabulary of their own ... Among these are the terms run or station, terms which the New Zealanders took over from Australia, ... <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>)

>  Run: an extensive grazing property usually in South Island tussock country. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

>  run n. grazing area for sheep and cattle; <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),17</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Run. The area held as a station; includes the area under pastoral lease. Also refers to a period of work; the shearers divide the working periods of their days into runs. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,97</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>v.</i> (of news) to spread rapidly

>  <b>HOGG</b> The news like bush fire thro' the diggings ran. <i>(1875,Lays and Rhymes,176</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>v.</i> [run like a hairy dog]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I've just seen him running like a hairy dog all over Lancaster Park. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,127</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>v.</i> [run like a hairy goat]

>  <b>SLATTER</b> An it ran like a hairy goat an I did me chips. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,23</i>)

>  <b>TURN ER</b> Even in this [passage from Slatter Gun in My Hand], there are two expressions with which I am not personally directly familiar, cat's bar, presumably `ladies bar' and ran like a hairy goat `ran badly', an Australianism, recorded by Baker. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,120</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> run like a hairy goat very slow or very fast; often of racehorse; eg Gordon Slatter's A Gun in My Hand: `It ran like a hairy goat an I did me chips [money].' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,95</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,120</i>) [NOTES: [see GOAT: transfer to here ?]]



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> ?possibly add here cattle-, sheep-run; dairy-run [one citation: dairy run]

>  WANTED, a PARTNER, sleeping or active, to take a share in a good Dairy Run, near a thriving town; ... <i>(Feb 2, 1861,Otago Witness,10</i>)



 
<b>8</b> <i>v.</i> [run the ship]

>  It has been the custom for a good number of years for the Shipping Companies to select an experienced man from the ranks of the Union to act as Foreman, or what is known in waterside parlance as `running the ship' <i>(Aug 3, 1912,NZ Truth,371/4/4</i>)



 
<b>8</b> <i>v.</i> [no entries]



 
<b>9</b> <i>v.</i> [one unconnected citation]

>  Pitch the tent. <i>(1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: Acland]

### run a banker
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) [braided or small rivers] to be filled bank to bank with water.

>  <b>BELL</b> Mr Couper found the Tukituki running a 'banker' and rising fast. <i>(1967,Hawke's Bay Stations Scrapbook II,9</i>)

>  <b>BRUCE</b> .. owing to heavy rain in the mountains, when they arrived at the Ashley River, it was running a banker. <i>(1977,Life in the Hinterland of the South Island,40</i>)

>  <b>FORRESTER</b> It was running a banker, and was impossible to ford on foot. <i>(1983,Chopper Boys, The,114</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> A few weedy, wool-blind hoggets held up proceedings and one, just to be awkward, staggered out onto a rock jutting out into the river which was running a banker. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,97</i>)

### run a charge
 
 <i>phr.</i> to put a `brew' of liquid through (an illicit) still.

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... they proposed lighting the fire and letting me see them `run a charge.' <i>(1944,Moonshine,94</i>)

### run a mile
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. West Coast: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; Wellington: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; Auckland: My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; Hawkes Bay: Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; Canterbury: Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; Golden Bay: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, I've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; Otago: Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,231</i>)

### run bait
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McDOWALL</b> The fat shape of koara whitebait, their milkiness, and the fact that they tend to be more slimy than other whitebait, has resulted in them being called "jelly bait". Others know them as "run bait" because they start to run earlier after floods than other species. <i>(1984,NZ Whitebait Book, The</i>)

### Run Book
 
 <i>n.</i> [station hist]

>  <b>WAITE</b> It appears the Run Book in Dunedin was opened on September first 1852. -->nf OED. <i>(1948,Pioneering in South Otago,38</i>)

### run bull
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a bull grazed on an extensive area with little attention.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Their cattle also have an enviable reputation and they sell anything up to forty run bulls. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,185</i>)

>  <b>AXBEY</b> No one lived permanently at the Ada, and the run bulls were confined in a large paddock with a high-wire fence. <i>(1991,Along the Track: a collection of NZ hunting yarns,114</i>)

### run cattle
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also run cow](../A#also-run-cow))

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I climbed up to see some of the cattle, and run cattle are always wild, and found there were no planks on top only the railing. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,151</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> It is also necessary to have a certain number of run-cattle on a sheep farm. They eat out any rank and tufty grass, weeds ... that are unpalatable to sheep. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,149</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a store cattle beast used for breeding and grazing and seldom handled.

>  An appreciable shortage in run cattle is apparent. <i>(Sep 1, 1925,NZ Farmer,1425</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> Then if on the way home a paddock of dry sheep or run cattle requires looking at and perhaps shifting, they have the dogs with them to do the job. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,97</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> run cattle, which apart from providing additional income, play an important role by regulating and controlling pastures in the best possible condition for sheep; and fattening cattle, usually grazed and finished off on heavier country. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,103</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> It had carried approximately 5,000 sheep as well as run cattle. <i>(1980,Farm on the Wanganui River, A,18</i>)

>  WAIRARAPA HEREFORD & SHORT-HORN STUD AND RUN CATTLE. <i>(Apr 5, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,10</i>)

### run country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) pastoral grazing land [usually high country.]

>  In some of the abandoned run country in Otago the ground was quite bare. <i>(Dec 4, 1909,Tuapeka Times,4</i>)

>  The aggressive nature of the brown-top is plainly seen throughout the run country, where it is a common exotic grass ... <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,99</i>)

>  The small area of turnips grown on the run country (about 1,000 acres) is, of course, quite inadequate for the needs of such country. <i>(Feb 20, 1937,NZ Journal of Agriculture,102</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Late burning in itself is bad enough, but combined with bunny, it is often fatal to run country. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,97</i>)

>  .. too hilly to become, in parts, first-class sheep and run country. <i>(Dec 5, 1940,NZ Farmer,25</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. although the run country is badly infested with manuka scrub, it is a good block. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,179</i>)

### run cow
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POWDRELL</b> Practical farmers sometimes put two calves on to one duffer cow, or to a run cow for five or six weeks ... <i>(1920,Dairy Farming in NZ,42</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a store or station cattle beast or one that is left grazing with little attention.

>  Run cows made from GBP11. 6/- to GBP11. 7/-. <i>(Sep 1, 1949,NZ Farmer,24</i>)

>  Run cows with calves at foot sold at GBP19 8/-. <i>(Dec 20, 1951,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

>  1800 ewes, 1000 hoggets, 170 run cows. <i>(Sep 14, 1961,NZ Farmer,93</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Besides the stud stock there are another 500 run cows ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,204</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> A run cow which broke from the railway yards at Taihape caused a vast amount more trouble. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,86</i>)

>  Run cows generally in lower condition than last year. <i>(Nov 13, 1975,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> The station 'run' cows are left to return to their favourite areas in the East and West branches. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,160</i>)

### run cray
 
 <i>n.</i> a light-coloured crayfish

>  <b>WILSON</b> Fishermen often use the term `run crayfish' for light-coloured crayfish, with yellow being a more prominent colour than usual. These runcrays are either males or juvenile females, and they are usually potted on the outer edges of the rough bottom where it merges into sand, or on light rough bottom where the rock outcrops are small. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,41</i>)

### run crayfish
 
 <i>n.</i> see cit.

>  <b>WILSON</b> Fishermen often use the term `run crayfish' for light-coloured crayfish, with yellow being a more prominent colour than usual. These run crays are either males or juvenile females, and they are usually potted on the outer edges of the rough bottom where it merges into sand, or on light rough bottom where the rock outcrops are small. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,41</i>)

### run down
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>NOLAN</b> Run down: to reduce the flow of water through a sluice box and to let the waste slowly clear itself prior to extracting the gold-bearing residues. <i>(1983,Gold Fossicker's Handbook,113</i>)

### run for one's colours
 
 <i>phr.</i> to serve a probationary period [gang slang]

>  In December, 1974, there were about 20 patch members and there were also a few `running for their colours' - serving a probationary period. <i>(Jun 17, 1975,Dominion,3</i>)

### run house
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a homestead on a sheep or cattle run.

>  <b>KERR</b> They bought a farm down country near Duntroon, sold the run house and woolshed and a bit of land around it ... <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,162</i>)

### run in
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to muster (sheep)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We wanted a mutton one night, and against my better judgment, I took Sam out to run in the killers. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,74</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> of milking cows, to bring them into the milking shed [ca1940 Still used of bring cows to the bails at milking.]

>  He has to `run in' the cows by himself which takes a long time for sometimes they are feeding some little way from the homestead. <i>(1889,Colonia,23</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Unfortunately in the afternoon we got ahead of the musterers, so the manager had to run in a mob of undagged cotty sheep that were being held until the cut out. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,130</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [[? omit]]

>  <b>WAITT</b> Akaroa is conveniently situated as a run in [for whaling vessels] <i>(1856,Progress of Canterbury, NZ, The,14</i>)

### run like a hairy goat
 
 <i>phr.</i> of persons, to run away quickly or suddenly; of motors, etc., to `run' badly, unevenly.

>  <b>LETHBRIDGE</b> `I'd been asleep. I went charging up that / face like a hairy goat, just in time to see you jokers starting the muster. <i>(1971,Sunrise on the Hills,38</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `Damn thing's [sc. a shearing motor] running like a hairy goat.' ...`I suppose it's worn out,' said Bessie ... <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,33</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Ever since, she [a moped]'s going like the one big hairy goat. Crook benzine, that's the story. <i>(1964,Watersiders,137</i>)



 
 <i>phr.</i> perform badly in a race

>  <b>JOURNET</b> Should Z [a horse] win his race he is said to have struck form. Should Z lose his race, especially when you have backed him, he is said to have run like a hairy goat, another foolish term as hairy goats are reputedly both agile and swift. <i>(1954,Take My Tip,77</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> An [sic] it ran like a hairy goat an I did me chips <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,23</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... ran like a hairy goat `ran badly', an Austral- ianism, recorded by Baker. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,120</i>)

### run like a redshank/like redshanks
 
 <i>phr.</i>

>  We rode up, and received the first confirmation of our fears; they told us that no one would venture in with despatches, that the troops were driven clean off the field and left their dead and wounded. The expression used was "They ran like red shanks." <i>(Aug 26, 1882,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  Dr. Findlay, who is nick-named the Government apologist, ran like a red shank until he got the ear of a reporter, and to him he confided his utter astonishment at such a change being made by a retired magistrate against his administration. <i>(Nov 19, 1910,NZ Truth,282/1/2</i>)

### run of one's teeth
 
 <i>phr.</i> rations, food [station]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> If wet weather should come on .... everything would be stopped in the way of [shearing] work, while all hands would still require to have `the run of their teeth' <i>(1881,Waitaruna,173</i>)

>  I arranged to join him as a cadet .... at the cost of ten shillings a week and the `run of my teeth' <i>(1888,Wakatipians,4</i>)

### run off
 
 <i>v.</i> draft or direct some stock to a new paddock or pen.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Anything gone in the mouth had been dotted and these were run off and penned on their own. <i>(1949,High Country,117</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> There may be 'strangers' in the mob. These he will pick up by their various earmarks and run off into a separate pen if available... <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,72</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> run off: (v): to separate by the use of drafting gates. <i>(1981,Glossary,69</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (of sheep) be worked successfully by a dog.

>  <b>KERR</b> I can see the way the sheep run off him. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,14</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> turn out stock on to paddocks.

>  When sheep are being fed on turnips, they should be run off on to grass at night. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,14</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> Mr Hull had purchased 230 acres of sandhill country on the coast west of Waiuku, where forward conditioned cattle are 'run off' during the wettest part of the winter. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,127</i>)

>  The hoggets are run off for the nights and weekends when they are grazed on pasture. <i>(Mar 2, 1961,NZ Farmer,11</i>)

>  While the ewes are on their winter sacrifice paddock they are run off daily for a two-hour feed on either grass or turnips. <i>(Aug 13, 1970,NZ Farmer,38</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a supplementary feed paddock for animals being fattened on crops.

>  <b>PERRY</b> .. it is always advisable to give them [lambs] a run off on to grass whilst they are on the rape. 71 A paddock with surplus grass, as a run off to the turnip paddock is quite good ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,70</i>)

>  A run-off from the rape on to a grass pasture is also desirable. <i>(Dec 20, 1928,NZ Journal of Agriculture,414</i>)

>  <b>EDGAR</b> Hoggets which are wintered on turnips should be provided with a grass run-off. <i>(1959,NZ Farmers' Veterinary Guide,164</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> run off; a paddock used as a camp for sheep being grazed on turnips etc. and where they normally receive their dry rations such as hay. Cattle on supplementary feed will usually have a run-off too. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,69</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a spell or period of time that stock can be temporarily placed on crop or pasture.

>  When feeding rape it is good practice to allow the lambs a run off on to a grass field. <i>(Oct 1, 1926,NZ Farmer,1413</i>)

>  In June they are put on ridged swedes with a run-off to young grass. [run-off] <i>(Nov 8, 1951,NZ Farmer,60</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a draft of animals away from the main flock or herd.

>  .. 'Will you take the line or a run off, asks the auctioneer, meaning 'Will you take them all, or part of them?' <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> run-off: stock counted straight out of a mob without drafting; the usual practice if the buyer does not want the whole line. A shrewd buyer will insist on the first run-off, this on the principle that the strongest animals take the lead. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,68</i>)

### run out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [a sheepdog on a dog trial course] to make a move toward sheep.

>  The second dog, Sweep, ran out on a good cast to the right and took charge well. <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,951</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> It is instinctive for a heading dog to cast when running out. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,153</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> 'John, it's taken most dogs three minutes to run out.' <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,158</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [ post and batten splitting] to complete a split too soon with a short length or narrow width.

>  Posts which run out in the splitting and are short can be split into battens ... <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,927</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [fencing] to feed out wire from a coil.

>  Mary had 'run out' the wire for him when he had put that first fence up. <i>(Apr 29, 1932,NZ Dairy Exporter,69</i>)

>  <b>FISK</b> Once the end strainers and angle posts are all in you are ready to run out a wire to get to a line. <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,13</i>)

>  <b>FISK</b> Real fencers seem to prefer running out all wires at once. <i>(1996,Practical Smallfarming,15</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>BOWEN</b> This way the comb enters nicely on the off leg and it takes little time to run out. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,26</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Run-out: The act of running out to head sheep. Outrun - the manner in which the dog runs out. <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,169</i>)

### run plan
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long-term plan formed by Catchment Board personnel and property owners for high-country land under ecological threat.

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> Technical proposals on land retirement schemes for tussock grassland properties are known as 'run plans' ... <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,2</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> .. a further 600 acres has been oversown by the runholder independently of the run plan. <i>(1970,Soil Conservation and Land Retirement,18</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> Revealing initiative, the "Downs" runholder and his lawyer decided to form a Land Company in 1969, the year before the pastoral lease was to be renewed, and to implement a run plan. <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,107</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> A run plan based on the report was drawn up by the Catchment Board ... The run plan and the development scheme were worked out to carry 3,000 stock units. <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,108</i>)

### run rings around/round
 
 <i>phr.</i> to overcome or surpass (a person) easily; to easily better (a person) at; to beat hollow [ca1900 (well pre-WW1); orig. Aust/NZ? now in UK dictionaries unlabelled]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Run Rings Round to run round: to beat out and out. A picturesque bit of Australian slang. One runner runs straight to the goal, the other is so much better that he can run round and round his competitor, and yet reach the goal first. [1891] <i>(1898,Austral English,390</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [The Australasian] to run rings around a person, which can be used in both literal and figurative senses ... [On] January 3, 1940, the version to make rings round was used in a leader of the London Times. ... There is every reason to suspect that it came from the shearing shed ... to ring a shed and to be a ringer. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,38</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> A new chum ... would ... hear such colloquial expressions as to run rings round `to excel easily'. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,51</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> My new [95] friends warned me that he would `run rings' round the lot of us. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,94</i>)

### run sheep
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [cf. paddock sheep](../P#paddock-sheep))

>  The sheep from Otago were principally grass fed, and too few run sheep were received to form any conclusion as regards the respective merits of the sheep in the three Provinces. <i>(Oct 13, 1871,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  9548 sheep shorn, 139 bales pressed. I think sheep and wool will be up to last year; run sheep much better. Ewe and paddock sheep not so good. Lice in wool and [dust from] ploughed ground all through Ewe mob. All the paddock sheeps wool must be scoured. <i>(Nov 15, 1889,Chudleigh Diary,368,1950 ed.</i>)

### run station
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an early dwelling on a large land holding.

>  <b>BARRER</b> They drove with their goods in a bullock dray, taking with them twenty head of cattle and three horses, besides the six bullocks. At night, they camped at run stations whenever possible, and at other times they made up a bed under the dray. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,268</i>)

### run the bottles on
 
 <i>phr.</i> to provide drinking water during a rugby game

>  It was great to have Filo running the bottles on as well. <i>(Oct 23, 2000,Dominion,28</i>)

### run the cutter
 
 <i>phr.</i> to procure beer in a vessel (poss. orig. on the sly) for drinking off the licensed premises, often after hours.

>  `He runs the cutter between a brothel in Frederick-Street and a nearby hotel', remarked Tec. Cassells of a crook in the S.M.'s court during the week. `Running the cutter' is a variant of the Sydney expression `running the rabbit' and the Yankee ones of `rushing the growler' or `the can' Which all mean the same thing i.e. fetching beer in a vessel. <i>(Dec 22, 1906,NZ Truth,79/5/8</i>)

>  I used to `run the cutter' with a dish From Side Street where we set the Daily Sun. <i>(Aug 1933,Oreti Anthology, The,12</i>)

>  [Cartoon of Jack Lovelock running with a rigger of beer in his arms, captioned] Jack Lovelock `Running the cutter' <i>(1937,Otago Capping Book,25</i>)

>  <b>BENNETT</b> <i>Running the cutter</i> (i.e. obtaining liquor for people who cannot get it themselves) is used without any appreciation of its original meaning of smuggling. <i>(1843,English as it is spoken in NZ,89</i>)

>  The party [of railway track layers] is provided with ... a jigger to maintain communications with the tunnel-mouth - and to `run the cutter' for the morning lunch and afternoon tea which is brewed outside. <i>(Feb 4, 1953,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>BIDDY PURCELL</b> . `Pat [her husband] used to run the cutter when he was at St. Pats College in the 1930s' [= bring in illicit booze] <i>(Oct 5, 1972,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1972 Oct 5 Havelock Biddy Purcell, publican's wife]

>  <b>BAXTER</b> And Horse had run the cutter down to the pub to bring back three gallon jars of beer. <i>(1962,Horse,20,1958 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1962 Baxter Horse (1985) (Mills of God) 20]

>  <b>CHARLES67</b> The expression `running the cutter' has its origins in the whaling days. ... `Grog shops' sprang up wherever ships called regularly ... When a ship lay at anchor, most of the crew would go ashore and an `anchor watch' would be left aboard. Very often, some thirsty soul ... would `borrow' the cutter and slip ashore for rum ... This was `running the cutter'. Later, in days when some areas were under prohibition, the term was used for the job of going to the nearest licensed hotel for liquor. ... And the driver comes in and breasts up to the bar./ He says, `Give me a jar and a couple of crates,/ For I am running the cutter tonight for me mates!'/... `We are fresh out of beer,/So that's why I'm here,/ Running the cutter tonight./ <i>(1981,Black Billy Tea</i>)

### run up
 
 <i>v.</i> to travel to the source of (a creek)

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> Up by daylight to run this creek up. Reached its Sources that afternoon. <i>(Dec 31, 1863,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Dec 31 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 395]

### run, the
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The diggers being of migratory habit, it is exceedingly difficult to identify the individual culprit, and if caught he invariably claims a right to fire what he calls "the run". <i>(Jan 29, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

### run-bred
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of store stock] born and raised on a station or grazing run. (See [station-bred](../S#station-bred))

>  Run bred cattle are also in favour at the moment. <i>(Oct 6, 1938,NZ Farmer,42</i>)

### run-of-the-land
 
 <i>adj.</i> (Rural) ordinary or utilitarian [the NZ farmer's equivalent of run-of-the-mill].

>  From the late 60s and early 70s the emphasis moved away from those pampered highly-fed cattle to more of a run-of-the-land animal. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,31</i>)

### run-off
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) (dog trials) a dog's final run.

>  In the run-off which he won, he struck three spirited sheep. <i>(Aug 2, 1951,NZ Farmer,20</i>)

>  Runoff - at Island and National Championships, the seven dogs in the call at the end of the first round run off for the title - aggregate of two runs. <i>(Dec 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,45</i>)

>  I feel sorry for Don as in both run-offs he was unable to complete. <i>(Aug 15, 1984,NZ Journal of Agriculture,21</i>)

>  .. he and his Hinkle made the yarding runoff at this year's Nationals. [runoff, run off] <i>(Sep 1985,NZ Journal of Agriculture,48</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a grazing area away from a main run or property; an off-run.
