>  Run-off: an area of land, perhaps separated from the main area where young stock or dry (non-lactating) animals are run <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

>  .. they [cows] should not have more than an hour or two a day on the crop and they should have hay fed to them on the grass paddock used as a run-off. <i>(May 20, 1933,Hawera Star,7</i>)

>  A winter run-off can be of tremendous assistance to the satisfactory management of soils which poach ... <i>(May 1957,NZ Journal of Agriculture,481</i>)

>  Dairy farmers with rough runoffs are faced with a difficult problem. <i>(Feb 1966,NZ Journal of Agriculture,63</i>)

>  About the end of July the cows were taken from the run-off to the home farm and run as a springing mob. <i>(Nov 1975,NZ Dairy Exporter,11</i>)

>  <b>FEARON</b> Reg had bought the block as a run-off for cattle, in the hopes that the recently developed manuka blight would destroy the young manuka on the block. <i>(1980,Te Wharau,51</i>)

>  They lease a property from Mrs Brown's brother. "We use it as a sort of run-off." <i>(Mar 29, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  Bareland - runoff situated approximately 18km south of Waipukurau. An ideal runoff for both sheep or cattle. <i>(Aug 18, 2001,Hawke's Bay Today,26</i>)

### run-off paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a grazing area adjacent to rotationally grazed or set stocking areas that is only occasionally used.

>  Press (Acland Gloss) Nov 25 Run off - paddock used with turnips or green feed on which the sheep may camp and get what extra feed they can. <i>(Nov 1933,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),25</i>)

>  .. crops are fed off in breaks, lambs are not allowed over areas already grazed, and heavily-contaminated run-off pastures are avoided. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,492</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Although a grass run-off, or hay - or both - is usually provided ... <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,I 19</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> we drove the remainder of the flock back to the run-off paddock and then rode home for the knives. [run-off pasture] <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,125</i>)

### run-on
 
 <i>adj.</i> describes the team that commences a game

>  Isaia Toeva was stunned to hear his name in the run-on XV. ... After just on full NPC match for Auckland, the shy Samoan-born fulback is still getting over the shock of being named in the 35-man squad - let alone the run-on side - to play Scotland on Sunday (NZT). <i>(Nov 25, 2005,NZ Herald,D9</i>)

>  Former All Black captain Taine Randall and Otago teammate Pita Alatini have been replaced.. in the run-on team for Sunday's rugby test against France. <i>(Nov 9, 2000,Dominion,1</i>)

### run-out
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a paddock or pasture area where the feed has been eaten out or died or where reversion has taken place.

>  On the farm of Mr G.F.Hughes at Springston, cereals and grass were overdrilled into a runout towards brown top reversion. [runout] <i>(Dec 31, 1964,NZ Farmer,8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land] spent through over-grazing and lack of replenishment with fertiliser etc.

>  We can also resuscitate run-out pasture far better, quicker and cheaper than by re-ploughing and resowing. <i>(Apr 1, 1944,Dairy Exporter (NZ),24</i>)

>  Not many people can be making a more praiseworthy effort to rehabilitate a run-out, badly reverted farm than Mr Bob Wech ... <i>(Dec 2, 1954,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> Run-out paddocks are ploughed up, put into swedes, to be fed off the following winter, and then put down in both grass and oats. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,182</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [dog trials] the initial part of a course for heading dogs.

>  <b>LILICO</b> A perfect run-out should be a circle all the way from the start till he gets round behind the sheep. <i>(1919,Breeding and Training of Sheep Dogs,18</i>)

>  Crutchley's Rae lost points in the run-out for taking a zig-zag course, but headed and lifted well. <i>(Jul 5, 1951,NZ Farmer,32</i>)

>  It was considered by many to be about the longest run-out in the country, and the pull also is far from easy. It's a run-out where a dog can easily go wrong. <i>(Mar 9, 1967,NZ Farmer,37</i>)

>  The runout went across a long flat then into a face that had clumps of Matagouri across and up it. <i>(Jul 15, 1986,NZ Journal of Agriculture,60</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The orthodox run out or cast on a dog trial hill is on the triallist's right of the sheep. <i>(1998,Three Sheep and a Dog,48</i>)

### run-owner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Those Wanganui cricketers who formed the team that visited Nelson in 1873 will regret the news contained in the following telegram from Nelson dated Friday last:- "Yesterday afternoon an inquest was held at Wakefield on the body of Melville Sellon, who was a runowner, and well known a few years since as a crack cricketer ..." <i>(Apr 26, 1880,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIDGOR</b> ... the run-owners are [73] clamouring for more ships, though they grumble at the average price of 4-1/2d. a pound, which is all their mutton realizes in London. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,72</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> When I first read this inscription [in the Memorial Church, Cave], the word `sheep-men' made me involuntarily flinch. Years later, when I had forgotten it, I suddenly met it again, and again I recoiled. So aghast was I that I spent weeks collecting every old term for `runholders' I could, and I found thirteen, viz., `flock owners,' `flock masters,' `pastoralists,' `run holders,' `run owners,' `sheep owners,' `sheep holders,' `sheep masters,' `squatters,' `station holders,' `station owners,' `stock holders,[sic] and `stock owners.' The word `sheepmen' is a modern Americanism, and using this anachronism mars a splendid memorial. ... [12] Of all the thirteen terms found in old documents, the word `runholder' was the most used, and it is the word that I think should be substituted. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,11</i>)

>  Stories of all sorts have come to light, one being that he, himself a very large run-owner, organised the squatter class to reduce the wages of sheperds by importing Pacific Island labour; but the Islander did not take to sheperding, and the attempt failed. <i>(Jun 10, 1907,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Jack was advised that it would be to his future benefit, probably, if he could obtain employment on a station; and among his letters of introduction was one to Mr John Campbell, a small runowner at a station some five miles or so from Riversdale, which, as everyone knows, is on the Gore-Lumsden line of railway. <i>(Dec 21, 1893,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### run-through
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a dipping/spraying race.

>  He hasn't yet found a 100% effective dip and uses a run-through. <i>(Sep 10, 1970,NZ Farmer,35</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> type of milking shed

>  <b>LOCKE</b> It was a "run-through" type with gates that opened for the cows to leave the bails and move straight into the paddock. <i>(1984,Kauri & The Willow, The,148</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a race that runs alongside and parallel to another, for ease of access between pens in sheepyards.

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> The run-through, which consists of a long alleyway, parallel with the drafting race, is a useful adjunct in any set of yards. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,341</i>)

### runa
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Runa, <i>Lindsay. Rumex </.I>sp. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Boiled flax-root water poured on the wounds, and also dock-root (<i>runa</i>), well scraped and boiled, were our favourite remedies for gunshot and bayonet wounds. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/26,1983 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Rumex flexuosus </i>NATIVE DOCK ...Runa ... Leaves brown-green. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,164</i>)

### runanga
 
 <i>n.</i> a tribal corporate body to manage iwi affairs [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  She says the runanga has accumulated funds of between $6 million and $6.5 million... Established under its own act of Parliament, after a robust consultation period of 18 months, the Ngati Porou runanga includes 20 trustees who are elected by postal vote every three years. <i>(Apr 12, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  [Ngai Tahu's] development arm had a host of programmes, as did the 18 papatipu (local) runanga, through which development money was also channelled. <i>(Sep 28, 1997,Sunday Star Times,A6</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an assembly, council. [ORIGIN: Maori, ?divide into purely Maori contexts and those where the word is used in Pakeha administrative contexts]

>  It appears that the natives there are beyond all restraint. They have broken loose altogether, laugh at the idea of a Commissioner coming among them, refuse to suffer even the forms of enquiry, and use language so outrageous concerning the Queen, the Governor, and the white <i>runanga</i> as to be without a parallel in the country up to the present time. <i>(Jan 10, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Runanga - assembly, council <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/834</i>) [NOTES: NZ (Dictionary NZ Language)]

>  This arrangement was proposed by Potata's runanga. <i>(Jun 21, 1858,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/411,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.Morgan)]

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> That the native runangas, or councils of the tribes, should ... be recognised by the Government ... <i>(1949,Selected Documents,108k</i>) [NOTES: 1860 May 12 W. Martin Memorandum to Governor Gore Browne in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 108k]

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> That no European Magistrate shall have jurisdiction within native boundaries, but all disputes shall be settled by the runanga. <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,6</i>)

>  At 9 a.m. the different runangas were called out, armed, and marched two deep round the flagstaff twice ... <i>(Apr 6, 1861,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  <b>RUTHERFORD</b> It is the desire of the Queen, and this also is the thought of Governor Grey and of the runanga of the Pakehas, that the Maoris should also do for themselves as the Europeans do. <i>(1949,Selected Documents,115</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Dec 8 Govenor Grey to the Duke of Newcastle -->transf = General Assembly, ?Legislative Council in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 115]

>  <i>(1971,Documents and Speeches,137</i>) [NOTES: (1861) 1971 Documents and Speeches 137 quoting A to J's 1861 [=District Council]]

>  ... on one occasion [in the 1850s] during the sittings of the General Assembly, the outside of the house was beseiged by crowds of Natives, listening and wondering what the white people inside could possible be doing, and whether they were holding a secret meeting or runanga to kill the Maories! He [Mr. Rogan] remarks that at this time the Maories first adopted the term `runanga,' the term `komiti' having previously been used by them in respect to deliberative meetings. <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  <i>(Mar 8, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/29,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond) [SEE HUI: ?Pakeha context]]

>  <b>MOSER</b> A few years back we used to hear a meeting of the tribes simply termed a `korero', a `talk', or a `ko-miti', a `committee', and it is only lately that our ears have been regaled with the high sounding term, `runanga'. <i>(1863,Mahoe Leaves,28</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> Village Runangas were chosen by the New Zealand Ministry as the foundation on which the `new institutions' were to be built. <i>(1864,Maori King, The,248</i>)

>  <b>MEADE</b> The runanga ... was assembled at the great pah today This plan of having native magistrates and runangas, or councils, composed of them and the teachers, originated with Wanganui. <i>(Dec 21, 1864,Ride Through Disturbed Districts</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Dec 21 Meade Ride through Disturbed Districts in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 433]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The result of a grand runanga was embodied in a brief and highly figurative letter written to all the tribes. <i>(1868,Past and Present of NZ,121</i>)

>  <b>GORST</b> ... the Waikato district was very especially under the jurisdiction of the Maori King and his Runanga. <i>Ibid.</i> 210 The supreme authority, legislative and judicial resided in the village Runanga. <i>Ibid.</i> 211 ... a official Report of Mr Turton. . . a magistrate in Waikato, made in 1861 ... `The Maori Court; or Runanga,was opened. Old Riwai sat as judge. The case, one of Koreroteka (slander) was introduced, and argued by two young men as roias (lawyers)' . . <i>(1908,New Zealand Revisited,1</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> This runanga consisted of Rewi Maniapoto (the tumuaki, or head of the council), his ... cousins ... and several other chiefs. <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/184</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1928,Where the White Man Treads,33</i>)

>  runanga, assembly; council; so, whare-runanga, meeting-house. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  runanga: council, assembly. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Grey and the New Premier, William Fox, set about introducing in Maori districts a system of `indirect rule' such as the Stafford ministry had advocated in 1858. Maori runanga (assemblies) in the various districts were to recommend to the Governor the laws they required. He could introduce them by Order-in-Council without seeking parliamentary assent. <i>Ibid.,</i> 302 [Maori glossary] runanga Assembly <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,132</i>)

>  No more [land] should be sold to Europeans without the consent of the federated tribes, and Maori disputes should be settled by their own runangas. <i>(1960,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/139</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> Here [in Turanga, c1851], particularly, the native council, the runanga, was in control of Pakeha trading, and it set the price of products, not the Pakeha. b. a meeting with <i>(1986,Matriarch, The,68</i>)

>  Mr Rolleston held a runanga [meeting] of the Hau haus. <i>(Jan 27, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,214,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Mr Tau has not released details of specific locations, but Ngai Tahu members, runanga (local councils) and the tribe own land across the South Island... <i>(Jun 6, 2006,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

>  It is fair to say the runanga (Maori council) is open to working with us to make sure we can continue to provide the 24-hour learning and boarding school concept in the best way. <i>(Nov 28, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

>  In the absence of authentic tribal structures such as runanga, trust boards have, in recent years, expanded their functions to include lobbying Parliament on a wide range of political issues. <i>(Dec 1992,Metro,148</i>)

>  Pursuant to the provisions of the New Zealand Public Health and Disability Act 2000, the following meeting of the Maori Health Runanga is notified for the month of November 2005. <i>(Oct 26, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,11</i>)

>  "You may go to any Maori region, there's a runanga or trust. That didn't use to be the case. Runanga have the physical resourses and the language, whereas the kohanga trust doesn't have the localised capacity." <i>(Apr 9, 2005,Dominion Post,E3</i>)

>  A great native runanga will be held at Galatea, Bay of Plenty, in a few days respecting the sale of land to the Government. <i>(May 9, 1874,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  'Right,' said Maria. 'It's time to educate your Maori friends. I'll offer a cookery school down at the runanga.' <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,27</i>)

>  He began attending runanga meetings at Takahanga in the early 1990s and was elected a trustee in 1994. <i>(Jul 29, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Over the past five years about $10 million has gone into the runanga. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 65</i>)

>  The young officer's costume was as primitive as his river boat -a cabbage-tree hat, a Crimean shirt, and a kilt mat of woven flax belted round his waist. <i>(Nov 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,18</i>)

>  Te Waka.. paddled three miles up the river.. where the Kingite runanga, a Council of the district chiefs, was sitting. <i>(Nov 1937,NZ Railways Magazine,18</i>)

>  On the Runanga, from 1928, I had the privilege fof hearing from the sons of the men who had actually signed the document [sc. the Treaty] <i>(Aug 11, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  Others signed on behalf of waka (canoe confederations), trust boards and runanga (multi-tribal councils). <i>(Feb 1993,Metro,126</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a house or place of assembly, a meeting house

>  <b>HARPER</b> After service they [Maoris] provide dinner for me in their `Runanga' or Social Hall, a low long wooden room, <i>(Feb 5, 1868,Letters from Gunner 7/516,126,1914 ed.</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> The baskets were bound together with strings of flax, and piled up in separate heaps to a height of about 15 feet on the free places before the meeting-house (runanga). <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,148,1933 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> ... and then to write sitting down on the floor of a Maori runanga and without table, chair or other convenience to record word by word a series of rapidly delivered translated speeches. <i>(1969,Seddons, The,63</i>)

### runanga house
 
 <i>n.</i> meeting house (Maori whare runanga)

>  They accordingly opposed the passage of the carts, and when he succeeded in getting two of these by, they rushed upon all four, seized all the goods, and carried them into their runanga house, Te Ika-roa-a-Maui. <i>(Oct 4, 1862,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  The Hon. Messrs Bryce and Rolleston are here. The former meets the natives at Runanga House <i>re</i> certain land claims at Piako. <i>(Mar 21, 1882,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  <b>KENNEDY</b> The building was formerly a Runanga, or meeting-house of the Hau-Hau rebels ... <i>(1876,Colonial Travel,252</i>)

>  ) ... each people should retain its whakapapa and korero and not be led astray by the versions of other tribes. But the marae and runanga house discussions between hosts and distinguished visitors must have resulted in a fair amount of interpolation. <i>(May 30, 1931,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/152,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Ngata]

>  <b>GRACE</b> they assisted him to re-robe, and led him to their runanga-house, treating him with every consideration. <i>(1899,Sketch of the NZ War,2</i>)

>  [S.I. use] ... the tang ... lasted for several days ... the remains laid [sic] in state in the runanga house at Tairooa [sic] Pah. <i>(Aug 19, 1905,NZ Truth,9/5/6</i>)

>  These will have to be revived in the carvings of the many runanga houses projected throughout the North. <i>(Jun 11, 1933,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/87</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  ... Matuahu - Te Kooti's most important pa where he had his runanga [31] council (house) but which was found abandoned. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,30</i>)

>  Early on the morning of the 26th, the British ensign was seen flying in front of the Runanga house, and the expression on every face of the assembly was that of pleasurable excitement. <i>(Apr 22, 1862,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  The carvings for the runanga house and various whares having been prepared in Rotoroa. <i>(Sep 1, 1910,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  At Okahu, a runanga house has been erected. <i>(Feb 1, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

### Runangan
 
 <i>adj.</i> Geol. classification

>  These collections are especially important as this sequence is the type of the Runangan Stage, and also contains the Whaingaroan hypostratotype. <i>(Mar 1986,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,71/45</i>)

>  The northern three quarters of the peninsular is underlain by undeformed, northeast-dipping, massive to crudely bedded glauconitic sandstone (Ruatangata Sandstone, Te Kuiti Group, Runangan-early Whaingaroan) known from drillholes to be over 129 m and possibly up to 300 m thick (Fig. 2; Ferrar 1925 p. 43). <i>(Aug 1989,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,85/19</i>)

### runanganui
 
 <i>n.</i> a general council [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [runanga](../R#runanga))

>  And so it happened that Sharples spent most of the 1980s chairing both the Ngati Kahungunu runanganui (overall council) and the Hoani Waititi Marae committee, dashing between Hawkes Bay and West Auckland, all the while working for Maori Affairs and raising a family. <i>(Dec 2004,North & South,69</i>)

### rung-in
 
 <i>adj.</i> substituted unexpectedly or at short notice (cf. ring in v., ring-in n.)

>  <b>Fanning</b> They are ...disgusted at seeing a `rung-in' man in the place which they had been fitting themselves to fill in many a hard battle. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,45</i>)

### rungateeda
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rangatira](../R#rangatira))

### rungatyeeda
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rangatira](../R#rangatira))

### runhold
 
 <i>n.</i> [Jim writes: Not recorded in DNZE in Acland's sheep station glossary.]

>  <b>BURDON</b> I shot a mob of eight on Hull's runhold. <i>(1996,Hunting for DOC,15</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a grazing property.

>  <b>BURDON</b> Access was through a runhold in the back of Springfield <i>(1994,Hunting for a Buck,15</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> I shot a mob of eight on Hull's runhold. <i>(1996,Hunting for DOC,120</i>)

### runholder
 
 <i>n.</i> owner (occas. manager) of a stock-run or station [(cf the various (near) synonyms: flock-owner, pastoralist, Sheep-farmer, sheep-king, squatter, station- owner, stockholder, Stockowner, woolgrower, etc) [AND spec comb ru@n4 1863]

>  Apparently the runholder has only to petition for large blocks of the choicest agricultural land, and they are granted forthwith, but should the adjoining runholders follow the example of the holder 327 (and in common justice they have an equal right to the same privilage - viz., free-selection before survey), the land that will be left to be dealt with for settlement when the leases of the runs fall in will be of inferior quality. <i>(May 13, 1882,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> Pastoral lands are the wild grazing lands ... leased ... for a term of years under a deed termed a `run licence,' to the emigrant who, embarking on pastoral pursuits, becomes what is indifferently termed a run-holder, sheep-farmer, flock-master, or Squatter. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/512</i>)

>  ... the month's grace customarily allowed to defaulting runholders by the land Office had elapsed, ... <i>(Jul 1, 1857,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  He the prince of all the squatters,/ Largest holder of runholders:- <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,61</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Feb in 1866 Canterbury Rhymes 61]

>  <b>FULLER</b> the runholder ... . <i>(1859,Five Years' Residence in New Zealand,162</i>)

>  George and I started early ... with all the D brand cattle on the run. They belong to Deans the oldest runholder in Canterbury. <i>(Sep 22, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,59,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRIFFITHS</b> It is absolutely necessary for a runholder to wait long enough to learn what will be the best site for his head station. <i>(1971,King Wakatip,98</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Feb W.G. Rees in 1971 Griffiths King Wakatip 98]

>  <b>BUTLER</b> Every [Canterbury] run-holder has a preemptive right over 250 acres round his homestead, and 50 acres round any other buildings he may have upon his run. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,viii/115</i>)

>  The runholders, squatters, sheepfarmers, or whatever else they may be called ... <i>(1865,Evening Post,1/26/2</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> Dear Mother, Your son is at last a runholder. I have bought a run ... <i>(May 20, 1865,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1865 May 20 Neave letter in 1981 Anderson Both Sides of the River 53]

>  But then one third of the runholders have had to do the same. <i>(Jun 3, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,188,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>T.S.GRACE</b> Runholders of a certain stamp will not thank me for this. <i>(Jun 1868,Journal,193</i>) [NOTES: 1868 June T.S.Grace Journal in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 193]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> ... and as time passed, and gave the run- holders power to smooth [sic] them away, the roughings and campings gradually ceased, and the life bettered every day, . <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,224</i>)

>  <b>VOGEL</b> The tenants of the waste lands, or `runholders' were allowed a right of pre-emption. <i>(1875,Official Handbook of NZ,128</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> I `cooeed' for a long time, and when I had given up all hope Mr.Clapcott, the runholder, kindly came across for me, ... and made me comfortable for the night. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,150</i>)

>  Boundary disputes between adjoining runholders are settled by arbitration under the direction of the Land Board, ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,73</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> For these reasons it usually pays a runholder best in the long run to breed up his own flock ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,92</i>)

>  Run holder.- Squatter, station owner, or (recent) owner of a small grazing run. This or stock owner was the proper designation of a squatter in the early days. (C.) <i>(Nov 25, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>MARSH</b> They were the wool-growers, the run-holders, the sheep-cockies, the back-countrymen. <i>(1945,Died in the Wool,12</i>)

>  <b>MILLER</b> There is the ...runholder and more important station owner; there is the cow cocky, the gold miner ... the Public Works raceman ... <i>(1946,There Was Gold,13</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> [In 1851 a number of Australian `squatters' came over and took up runholding] Over here [in Canterbury the Australian `squatters'] were called runholders. ... [11] see SHEEPMEN... [11] When I first read this inscription [in the Memorial Church, Cave], the word `sheep-men' made me involuntarily flinch. Years later, when I had forgotten it, I suddenly met it again, and again I recoiled. So aghast was I that I spent weeks collecting every old term for `runholders' I could, and I found thirteen, viz., `flock owners,' `flock masters,' `pastoralists,' `run holders,' `run owners,' `sheep owners,' `sheep holders,' `sheep masters,' `squatters,' `station holders,' `station owners,' `stock holders,[sic] and `stock owners.' The word `sheepmen' is a modern Americanism, and using this anachronism mars a splendid memorial. ... [12] Of all the thirteen terms found in old documents, the word `runholder' was the most used, and it is the word that I think should be substituted. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,10</i>)

>  (`Central Otago') [Regulations] legalized at that time [1856], allowed land outside the original Otago Block to be bought for grazing-runs at a low cost. (Runholders were allowed 80 acres for the homestead block with 10 acres for each outstation.) <i>(1948,Our Own Country,135</i>) [NOTES: 1948 Our Own Country (from Korero, AEWS magazine) 135]

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> The sheep farmer needed considerable initial capital, not to buy land, ... but to buy sheep, and if he were not the original run-holder, to purchase the `goodwill' of a sheep run. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,91</i>)

>  <b>OLIVER</b> Sheep brought the runholders and later the station-owners. The runholders owned only the sheep which they pastured on great stretches of leased land; ... <i>(1963,Story of New Zealand, The,107</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> The early run-holders brought their sheep down for shearing to what <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,35</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> Jim, the runholder, persuaded Bluey to stay on and give a hand to lamb the paddock ewes. <i>(1970,Tat,89</i>)

>  run holder: A run holder is the LEASE HOLDER on a cattle or sheep station when the government actually owns the land. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,86</i>)

>  <b>BREMNER</b> Runholder usually men of education and capital who took up Crown Land for sheepfarming - `squatter'. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>SHERWOOD</b> The words flew, and enriched Celia's [an English visitor's] vocabulary. `Duck shoving' was the local name for buck-passing between government departments. A `Queen Street farmer' was a city slicker who owned land or stock for tax-fiddle reasons. `Skite' and `drongo' were terms of abuse. A `cow-cockie' was a dairy farmer and inferior for some reason to a `runholder' concerned only with sheep. A `piker' was a faint-hearted person who gave up the fight too soon, ... <i>(1985,Botanist at Bay</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> `The run-holders were hard,' continued Mr Carrodus, `but times were hard too. <i>(1985,Coldstream,125</i>)

>  runholder n. NZ & Austral. owner or manager of sheep or cattle run. <i>(1968,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,668</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Runholder. Station owner <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,97</i>)

>  First, let an impartial estimate of all the pasturage be made (as to the estimate contained in your leading article, I can only say "Credeat Judaeus Apella," or, - a Wairau runholder may believe it but I can't), however, I should imagine the <i>smaller</i> the pasturage, the more reason for its <i>equitable</i> and <i> economical</i> distribution. <i>(Apr 17, 1852,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,31</i>)

>  The large runholder is losing money. <i>(Apr 1908,NZ Farmer,320</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a manly rural male

>  Thanks to Meads, we might inadvertently be coining a new phrase for an old trend - "ruralsexual"... One of those ruralsexuals, prop Carl Hayman, made a name for himself as a 21-year-old with a 41-year-old's beard, becoming the 1000th All Black when he made his debut in 2001. <i>(Jun 27, 2004,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),19</i>)

### ruriruri
 
 <i>n.</i> ? love poems or songs; song, ditty, generally of an amorous nature, accompanied by gestures (Williams p. 352) which Best may be using light-heartedly; or ? a mis-reading of pukapukas `books'. [occas. humorous: spg Roody Doody, Rure Rure Maori ruri, ruriruri] (See [rowdy-dowdy](../R#rowdy-dowdy),[kow-rowdy-dowdy](../K#kow-rowdy-dowdy))

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The Women will sit on a heap of Potatoes or log, and do what is called `Roody Doody' [ruriruri]; ... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),50,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> The Women will sit on a heap of Potatoes or log, and do what is called `Roody Doody' [ruriruri]; ... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),50,1963 ed.</i>)

>  I have collected some more Rure Rures which are good. Some time since I bought Lady Blessingtons and ... waded through the whole of them except her conversations with Lord Byron. <i>(Mar 10, 1842,Journal of Ensign Best,355,1966 ed.</i>)

### ruru
 
 <i>n.</i> owl [The form (nf W.) poss. represents he ruru.]

>  Many [kiore] are slow and weak-looking and will invariably be taken by native predators such as kahu, ruru and pukeko. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/24</i>)

>  <i>Athene Novae Zelandiae.</i> More-Pork. Heruru. Kou-kou. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  It was easy to see why he was nicknamed "Ruru", the night owl. <i>(Feb 1997,North & South,29</i>)

### ruru-whekau
 
 <i>n.</i> whekau [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>BULLER</b> SCELOGLAUX ALBIFACIES. (LAUGHING-OWL.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Whekau, Ruru-whekau, and Kakaha; `Laughing Jackass' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,21,{1888 2nd edn 1/198} ed.</i>)

### rusa
 
 <i>n.</i> large E. Ind. variety of deer farmed in NZ

>  rusa n. large E. Ind. variety of deer farmed in NZ. [L from Malay] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,669</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

### rush
 
 <i>n.</i> . [swamp plant, unspecified]

>  Towards sunset we emerged from the wood, which had served to protect us from a heavy rain, into a salt marsh thickly covered with rushes and flax. <i>(May 9, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> <i>(1777,Cook's Journals</i>) [NOTES: [get quot]]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Rush. <i>Juncus</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),768</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> RUSHES (<i>Juncus polyanthemos</i> [spp]) often called by their Maori name of Wiwi, are well known in all damp pastures. [Several different species grow on cultivated ground.] <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,54,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <<i>rush [=] bog-rush: cladium: leptocarpus: spike-rush: wire-rush [s] </i> <i>Ibid.</i> <i>rushes [s] [12 Juncus spp</i>.] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,65</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the drug isobutyl nitrite [drug]

>  Those charges relate to supplying the youths with isobutyl nitrite, a drug populatr with homosexuals and commonly known as rush. It is a relaxant and enhances sexual pleasure, but is dangerous and can cause sedation, dizziness, fainting and even death. <i>(Feb 23, 2003,Sunday Star Times,A12</i>)

>  Defence counsel Tim Fournier said the drug - isobutyl nitrite, known as "rush" - had only a short-term effect. <i>(Apr 10, 2003,Dominion Post,A5</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> >v.t. to hurry along (cattle) [cattle farming]

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> Down the slopes we rushed them [sc. cattle] hell for leather,/ Towards the open country and the yard. <i>(1950,Farm & Station Verse,67</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from STUDHOLME 1938]

>  Then the success attending the export of frozen mutton caused a number of the settlers to rush their cattle into the markets, and sell at any price in order to get on to sheep. <i>(Jan 25, 1884,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [plant disease]

>  A disease called "Rush" has made its appearance among the peach orchards in the district, and has wrought considerable damage among the late varieties. <i>(Mar 6, 1918,Otago Witness,22</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to seize upon, either by force or before the appointed time. Compare Jump. [1896. Modern: Those who had no tickets broke through and rushed all the seats.' The dancers becoming very hungry did not stand on ceremony, but rushed the supper.']



 
 <i>v.</i> to stampede [cattle farming]

>  [We] went to get some more cattle for Mellish out of Rowleys mob. ... We brought some home and vainly endeavoured to yard them but as all took to rushing we let them go. <i>(Oct 2, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,105,1950 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (? in general sense of rush v) [goldmining]

>  <b>HEYWOOD</b> We brought from Sydney several men rushing to the Otago diggings. <i>(1863,Vacation Tour at the Antipodes, A,161</i>)

>  Joe Potter, humping his bluey across the scrub-covered Waitui flats, when he and Steel rushed Flannigan's Find, little thought he would ever see an important town in that place ... <i>(Nov 1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,108</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to occupy by an influx of gold diggers [goldmining]

>  The Highbury has been rushed, condemned, almost deserted, and yet survives. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and ReSources,26</i>)

>  The place was `rushed' by about 500 men, who speedily deserted it and declared the Waitahuna to be a `duffer' <i>(1863,AJHR,D/6/3</i>) [NOTES: (Pyke Report Goldfields. See also V. Pyke's Report in 1887 Handbook NZ Mines 5]

>  A man comes to a place some time after it has been `rushed'; ... <i>(Apr 2, 1864,All the Year Round</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Apr 2 All the Year Round in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 18]

>  <b>PYKE</b> Our object was to work only the richest spots, as we did not know how soon we might be discovered and `rushed.' <i>ibid.</i> 85 `The fact that we were liable to be `rushed' at any time, and our claims limited to 24 feet square did not affect Fox's or West's parties, who worked eight hours a day, with frequent `spells' and Saturday half-holidays. <i>(1887,History of the Early Gold Discoveries  in Otago,74,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Westland, or `West Coast', as it is more commonly called, was rushed by gold-diggers in the early sixties, <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,30</i>)

>  Our object was to work only the richest spots, as we did not know how soon we might be discovered and `rushed'. <i>(Aug 23, 1962,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  I am afraid the place will be over rushed, as 4000 are reported as on their way from Melbourne. <i>(Sep 17, 1861,Wellington Independent,5</i>)

>  A nineteen ounce nugget was unearthed at the rush last week and several other heavy finds are also reported, but the area is very limited, and is already greatly over rushed. <i>(Apr 9, 1877,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a place where gold is found, and to which consequently a crowd of diggers `rush.' [1855] [goldmining; nb ref to place not process]

>  <b>PFAFF</b> We were on the Five Mile, and then went on to Hunt's rush. I was there when Hunt got away from a big crowd in the bush. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,8</i>)

>  rush n. a stampede of cattle, etc.; a hurried gathering of miners as a place where gold has been found for the first time;-v.i. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,10</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Their father lived at Caversham, and at the time of Gabriel's rush he built an hotel at Caversham ... <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,81</i>)

>  ~ n. the hurrying of diggers to a new gold field; the field itself; a stampede of cattle. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),624</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Undefeated, on they came to the West Coast rush. <i>ibid.</i> 38 The exciting days when fresh finds of gold were reported and wild rushes took place were over. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,37</i>)

>  rush ... 2 n. ... NZ & Austral. sudden migration of numbers of people to goldfield or other claim <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,669</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 1012, sense 10]]

>  The ... impossibility of keeping workmen whilst the perpetual excitement of new `rushes' is maintained, was making contractors hang back ... <i>(Feb 15, 1863,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/23,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]



 
 <i>n.</i> [goldmining] (See [gold-rush](../G#gold-rush))

>  <b>SWAINSON</b> In order to check an immediate and indiscriminate rush to the spot, ... the Government now issued provisional regulations,* under which licenses might be obtained by the public to dig, search for, and remove alluvial gold. <i>(1853,Auckland,104</i>)

>  There is some talk of a rush to a spot some miles beyond the Slate River, ... <i>(Aug 22, 1857,Lyttelton Times,4</i>)

>  I've circulated rumours, not a few,/ Of people finding nuggets by the score,/ To cause a rush, ... <i>(Oct 22, 1862,Lyttelton Times</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Oct 22 Lyttelton Times in 1948 Von Haast Julius Bon Haast 259]

>  <b>J.G. WALKER</b> He [the miner] was very civil, told us that two of his mates had followed a man to see if they could find a new rush, ... '[ <i>(Jan 12, 1863,Journal and Letters (Walker)</i>) [NOTES: (Central Otago)]

>  <b>HEYWOOD</b> No doubt the old settlers have made a good thing out of the rush. <i>(1863,Vacation Tour at the Antipodes, A,167</i>)

>  I've circulated rumours .../ Of people finding nuggets by the score,/ To cause a rush, ... <i>(1866,Canterbury Rhymes,92</i>) [NOTES: 1862 Oct in 1866 Canterbury Rhymes 92]

>  <b>McCOMBIE</b> <i>(1861,Australian Sketches,86</i>)

>  <b>FARJEON</b> ... when the diggings first broke out, he was a Cheap Jack, as they call them, trading at all the new rushes. <i>(1866,Grif: a Story of Colonial Life,39</i>)

>  <b>McLEAN</b> New `rushes' on Goldfields, as a rule, are not advantageous to the digger or the state. <i>(1869,Papers,31/190</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> .. it was the time of the great Dunstan `Rush', and a host of miners were hurrying from all parts of New Zealand ... <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/ii/8 .,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> (I) The hurrying off of diggers to a new field <i>(1898,Austral English,398</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> ... some old Australian diggers, engaged in cutting a dray track ..., discovered the precious metal in sufficient quantity to create a fever of expectation and a `rush.' <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,198</i>)

>  He next found his way to New Zealand, and arrived at the time of the Gabriel's Gully rush, his brothers having arrived prior to him. <i>(Feb 1, 1917,Grey River Argus,2</i>)

>  THE BIG BAY RUSH. <i>(Aug 25, 1886,Star, The,4</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to charge a man [Contraction for to rush-at. [1861] .]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Rush, v. <i>(1898,Austral English,398</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a stampede of lambs or bullocks [animal stampede]

>  Only about half an hour elapsed from the time I heard the rush of cattle in the bush, until I saw them drove into the yard. <i>(Sep 10, 1874,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Let us imagine, if we can, a mad rush of cattle, goaded by desperate men, in the small hours of the morning, upon the position held by that gallant little band. <i>(Mar 4, 1902,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  rush n. a stampede of cattle, etc.; a hurried gathering of miners as a place where gold has been found for the first time;-v.i. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,10</i>)

>  ~ n. the hurrying of diggers to a new gold field; the field itself; a stampede of cattle. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),624</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> i.e. to cause them to rush about or away. [1855] ... [no entries]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [gum digging]

>  <i>tailed-seed rush [s] Juncus canadensis </i>J. Gay <i>Ibid.</i> 66 <i> rush, tailed-seed [s] Juncus canadensis </i> J.Gay <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,76</i>)



 
<b>5</b> <i>v.</i> [[1887] No entries]

### rush fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Schizea fistulosa</i>

>  <b>POTTS</b> (And) <i>(1882,Out in the Open</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Schizea fistulosa </i> rush-fern <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Report on a Botanical Survey of Stewart Island]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> ... a man could plant one foot on ryegrass of the deepest green and the other on rush fern (<i>Schizea</i> sp.) a species that delight in utter barren-ness. <i>(1936,NZ Naturalist,46</i>)

### rush ho
 
 <i>exclam.</i> to the goldrush!

>  According to the E. Star, "Rush Ho," is again the order of the day, and the Waimea is now the latest El Dorado. <i>(Aug 13, 1866,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> Thatcher sang [c1860s]:- The cry is now `Rush Ho!' and away the diggers go,/ To the norrard now they make their way. <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,26</i>)

### rush house
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>Mrs HENRY WILLIAMS</b> Mrs. Davis, myself and all the children took possession of the two remaining rooms in the rush hut, or as the natives called it, `The Whare tow tow,' ... <i>(Apr 15, 1933,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1824 Aug 15 Mrs Henry Williams letter in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 9]

### rush hut
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>Mrs HENRY WILLIAMS</b> Mrs. Davis, myself and all the children took possession of the two remaining rooms in the rush hut, or as the natives called it, `The Whare tow tow,' ... <i>(Aug 15, 1824,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1824 Aug 15 Mrs Henry Williams letter in 1940 Matthews Matthews of Kaitaia 9]

### rush whare
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small house made from raupo or rushes.

>  <b>BARRER</b> They camped the first night in a rush whare where, with children and dogs, they lay in their blankets on the mud floor. <i>(1939,Brave Days: Pioneer Women of New Zealand,217</i>)

>  1901-6 General shift in housestyle from rush whare to wooden houses in Maori villages with improvements in sanitation. <i>(Oct 2002,NZ Family Physician,29/5/346</i>)

### rush-raft
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mokihi or raft made of rushes or flax-flower stems.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> During the siege of the island, mokihi or rush-rafts were used, and all sides of the pa attacked. <i>(1921,Tutira,83</i>)

### rusher
 
 <i>n.</i> a beast given to rushing or charging [farming]

>  We allude to the erratic and by no means well-disposed mobs of cattle, and the solitary bullocks known technically as "rushers," that daily terrify and jeopardize the Queen's lieges on the road to Port Charmers. <i>(Feb 27, 1864,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Only occasionally we find it necessary to slaughter some unmanageable rusher, a cow, or bullock. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/158</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> someone rushing to the goldfields

>  <b>BOWMAN</b> When Thomson landed in 1862 .. he found himself among the "rushers", tramping in company with his brother Andrew to the goldfields. <i>(1948,Port Chalmers,18</i>)

>  <b>DON</b> European `rushers' hither and thither preferred to `dwell in tents' <i>(1905,Annual Tour Chinese in Otago,1</i>)

>  <b>BOWMEN</b> He found himself among the `rushers' <i>(1948,Port Chalmers,18</i>)

### rushes and reeds
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. West Coast: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; Wellington: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; Auckland: My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,231</i>)

### rushing
 
 <i>n.</i> [gold]

>  <b>May</b> The goldfields press complained of `headstrong and indiscriminate rushing' in 1865, a complaint still being echoed in 1867: <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,280,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>HEYWOOD</b> Our old friends, Cobb and Co., had followed their `rushing' friends from Victoria. <i>(1863,Vacation Tour at the Antipodes, A,162</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [farming]

>  The rushing of the cow past the horse and very near it, in witness's opinion, caused Scrivener's horse to bolt. <i>(Aug 25, 1908,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  [Rev.J.G.Engst] would face man or the devil [452] bible in hand but he would take a stick to a rushing cow. <i>(Nov 1, 1910,Chudleigh Diary,451</i>)

### Russell lupin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i> lupin, Russell [s] Lupinus polyphyllus </i>. Lindl <i>. Lupinus polyphyllus X arboreus </i>. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,45</i>)

### Russellitis
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Russellitis describes the easy life, on the dole or other income, spending most of the day wind-surfing - an easy life that would be what in other places would be called `gone troppo' <i>(May 5, 1984,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Programme on Russell. 1984 May 5 2YA Wellington]

### Russian comfrey
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Russian comfrey [s] <i>Symphytum X uplandicum </i>Nym. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,66</i>)

### Russian knapweed
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>knapweed Russian [s] Centaurea repens</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,42</i>)

>  Russian knapweed [s] <i>Centaurea repens</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,66</i>)

### Russian scare
 
 <i>n.</i> the threat of coastal invasion of New Zealand during the 1870s and 1880s

>  The fort was occupied by the New Zealand army from the Russian scare till the end of World War I and was again garrisoned during World War II. <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes,2</i>) [NOTES: www.nzlive.com/en/rss?lang=en&type=search_events&sort=ranking&tag=ww2]

>  <b>John Wilson</b> The later Russian scare of 1885 grew out of Anglo-Russian rivalry in Afghanistan and led to the building of major fortifications along New Zealand's coast. <i>(Apr 2007,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.tera.govt.nz/NewZealanders/NewZealandPeoples/RussiansUkraniansAn ...]

>  The walled fort, dating from 1886, survives today and is the most complete "Russian scare" fort in New Zealand. <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes,2</i>) [NOTES: www.doc.govt.nz/templates/PlaceProfile.aspx?id=35293]

>  On the left-hand side of School Road, high up on the ridge above, is one of the forts built in the four main centres during the second 'Russian scare' of 1885. <i>(2005,Internet - see Notes,2</i>) [NOTES: www.wellington.govt.nz/services/heritage/pdfs/northernsuburbs.01.pdf (from 1998 brochure)]

>  <b>Stapleton</b> Around the early 1870s the so called "Russian scare", that is the threat of invasion to New Zealand from the sea, the Boroughs of Hokitika, Greymouth and Westport raised concerns to Government, which led to one of each of these cannons being despatched as a means of defence of the coast and river mouths. <i>(2007,Wanderings in Westland: Trips down Memory Lane,49</i>)

>  <b>W L Ruffell</b> In 1878 diplomatic relations with Russia became strained bringing about what New Zealand history records as 'the first Russian scare'... Early in 1885 when hostilities were believed imminent New Zealand experienced 'the second Russian scare'. <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes,4</i>) [NOTES: www.riv.co.nz/rnza/hist/pf/pf2.htm website of NZPFOCA (NZ Permanent Force Old Comrades Assn.)]

### rusticated
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  The warehouse will have a frontage to Queen-street of 66 feet, with a depth to the rear of 80 feet, the elevation being 41 feet. It is to be built in the Italian style of architecture ; the facings to be of pressed brick, relieved by freestone dressings, with rusticated pilasters and quoins surmounted by cornices. <i>(Oct 4, 1861,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  Cornice, architrave, scotia[,] skirting and dado mouldings, rough and dressed timber of all kinds, including "T" and "G" flooring, lining, rusticated and shelving kept in stock. <i>(Oct 10, 1896,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  THE KARAKA SAWMILLS have large stocks of Timber on hand, both at their Town Yards and at their Mills, Te Karaka, consisting of Rusticated Weather Boards, T. and G. Flooring, Skirting, Mouldings, etc. <i>(Jun 2, 1904,Poverty Bay Herald,1</i>)

>  Flooring, Rusticated Bds., and Matched Lining 8s 6d [per 100ft]. <i>(Jun 8, 1889,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

>  ON SALE, American Shelving .. Rusticated Boards, White Pine, Kauri, and Deal ... <i>(Jun 14, 1879,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  Rusticated Boarding, T. and G. Flooring, T.G.B. and V. lining. <i>(Oct 1, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,3</i>)

>  At the same time (c 1870s) the rusticated weatherboard made its appearance - the invention of it being attributed to Toxwood, who was an artichtect [sic] trained in Denmark. . . In the days of the wooden rusticated house.. the wealth of the fortunate owner was reflected in the character and elaboration of the external adornment. <i>Ibid.</i> 21 The plain rusticated wall with stops was the lowest degree; if quoins were mitred at the corners that was an indication of greater refinement; but for the highest type and the wealthier client the quoins must be ovolo molded. <i>(Apr 1935,NZ Inst. Arch. Journal,20</i>)

>  <b>KNIGHT & WALES</b> There are three arched recesses rusticated with vermiculated piers and arch stones. <i>(1988,Buildings of Dunedin,174</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from 1868]

>  <b>KNIGHT & WALES</b> The lower half of the facade is rusticated and the upper storey is decorated with coupled columns against the wall, with Ionic capitals. <i>(1988,Buildings of Dunedin,205</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> A Greek survival building faced with Tasmanian sandstone, the facade has a rusticated ground floor with attached fluted Doric columns supporting an entablature and cornice. <i>(1991,NZ Architecture,24</i>)

>  <b>SALMOND</b> Another kind of weatherboard which became popular in the 1880's was the "rusticated" board, where the top edge was planed down to fit behind a rebate in the edge of the next board. The completed wall showed pronounced grooves which resembled channel-jointed stonework, and in more elaborate houses timber <i>quoins</i> at corners assisted the illusion. The introduction of the rusticated board has been attributed to the Wellington architect Julius Toxward in the 1860s. <i>(1986,Old New Zealand Houses,114</i>)

>  rusticated or rusticating n. (in New Zealand) a wide type of weatherboarding used in older houses. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,1013</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1986 NZPOD; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 427]]

### rustication
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>STACKPOOLE</b> In all of these.. his [sc. Toxward's] very individual choice of decorative motifs was plain... on the National Bank the rustication of the upper storey behind corinthian columns. <i>(1977,Colonial Architecture in NZ,175</i>)

>  <b>KNIGHT & WALES</b> Rustication: Masonry with sunk joins chamfered or rebated or roughened surfaces. The back of the rustication is the face line of the wall. <i>(1988,Buildings of Dunedin,248</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> Rustication: Masonry with sunk joins chamfered or rebated or roughened surfaces. The back of the rustication is the face line of the wall. <i>(1991,NZ Alpine Journal,207</i>)

### rusty
 
 <i>adj.</i> the sense `bad tempered; cross, nasty,' (OED a3) is used in NZ mainly of animals other than horses, bulls, boars, dogs etc.

>  [Old dogs] get caught by a rusty old boar sometimes. ... We bailed a rusty old sod up one day. a common nickname-shortening of the name Russell, whether or not the person named has red hair. <i>(Apr 19, 1962,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>) [NOTES: (Interview: Murphy / Crump) ca1900 Havelock.]

### rusty fern
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Sphaerocionum ferrugineum</i> [[Omit as transl. bookname]??]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> ... the much larger, handsome, bronze-coloured RUSTY FERN (<i>Sphaerocionum ferrugineum</i>) commonly drapes the caudex of a tree-fern with its numerous, overlapping, pendulous fronds. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,100,1929 ed.</i>)

### rusty gold
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  After lunch, the Professor tested some specimens submitted to him by local men, and amongst other things showed the method of cleaning rusty gold. <i>(Apr 24, 1886,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Prior to the floods the indications were such that the directors and shareholders fully believed that the dredge was about to realise expectations. For the few hours that she was on the first bottom, coarse, rusty gold, characteristic of that particular place, was got, but the river rose again, and another opportunity did not occur. <i>(Aug 7, 1901,Otago Witness,25</i>)

>  Mr Gordon referred to the existence in this colony of "rusty" gold, which was coated with oxide of iron and refused to amalgamate easily. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,21</i>)

>  <b>MAY</b> The mercury plates were seldom perfect: the finest gold was lost by flotation while with cemented gold a thin coating of iron oxide (`rusty gold') often prevented amalgamation. The mercury itself became `sick' or `floured'; both sodium and nitric acid were employed to improve its amalgamating qualities. <i>(1967,West Coast Gold Rushes,238,1962 ed.</i>)

### Ruthanasia
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Labour leader Helen Clark is questioning the appointment of one of the "high priests of Rogernomics and Ruthanasia" as the new head of the Department of the Prime Minister and Cabinet. <i>(Oct 13, 1998,Dominion,1</i>)

>  The leader, deputy and a handful of others are queasy about the Ruthanasia era. Who isn't? <i>(Sep 14, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

>  They speak of a time before Rogernomics and Ruthanasia, when issues were clear-cut, and people still cared enough to put their bodies on the line for something bigger than themselves. <i>(Sep 13, 1996,Independent (Auckland),18</i>)

>  In 2002, Colin James argued that Helen Clark's government was operating during a period of consolidation, smoothing out some of the most glaring social inequalities created by the 1984-92 policy revolution, while consolidating the key economic changes of Rogernomics and Ruthanasia. <i>(Sep 11, 2005,NZ Herald,Herald on Sunday 4</i>)

>  And for most of us, those sensual childhood delights intensify and deepen with age. "Let's go into the garden" becomes a universal cry from the heart that can be heard more often as the economic climate worsens and Ruthanasia (Mary Varnham's delightful term) rampages in the wake of Rogernomics unrelieved by shafts of light or the chiaroscuro effects to be found in the personal Eden of the garden. <i>(Sep 21, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),79</i>)

>  What transformed the two major parties into the activist-based organisations they are today? Rogernomics and Ruthanasia. <i>(Aug 18, 2006,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  <i>(Sep 3, 2010,Dominion Post,B6</i>) [NOTES: By the time Rogernomics and Ruthanasia had run their course, New Zealanders had lost control of their finance sector, most of their news media, and much else besides.]

>  If you doubt this, consider the electorate's rejection of Rogernomics in 1990, and Ruthanasia in 1993. <i>(Feb 3, 1995,Dominion,8</i>)

>  Miss Clark said Labour or a Labour-Anderton government could be trusted. "There was a phenomenon with Rogernomics in the 80s and Ruthanasia in the 90s when the big parties lost their way." <i>(Jun 12, 2002,Evening Post,1</i>)

### Ruthenomics
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Ruthenomics was no kinder coming from Shipley, but it was easier on the ear, thanks to her beautifully mellifluous vowels. <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### rutitira
 
 <i>n.</i> ?= mingimingi

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i>Cyathodes robusta</i>, Hook.f.] Rutitira. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,412</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i>Cyathodes ... robusta </i>... A shrub 4-15 ft. high, it forms large round bushes ... Distinguished from its New Zealand cousin C. acerosa by its broader, bigger leaves ... and its larger fruits it [sic] is only found on the Chathams. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,56</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [rewarewa](../R#rewarewa))
